• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.

• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 50 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 4 OF 96

The programme will be executed by three institutions with clear roles according to their responsibilities. CABEI will be the accredited entity for the programme, but will also be directly executing the major part of the project (activity 3.1). The World Bank will be responsible for the loan and guarantees facility under this component (activity 4.2). CABEI will also be the executing entity for the pre-feasibility study and the feasibility study for the proposed Adaptation demonstration sites.

The Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) will play a major role in the execution of the project, particularly with respect to the development and implementation of the proposed Adaptation demonstration sites throughout the region as the Executing Entity of output 1.2.

The Central American Commission for Environment and Development (CCAD) will play a key supporting role in the project by providing technical assistance on the preparation of the outputs 3 focused on gathering and disseminating key results and analysis derived from the research and demonstration activities, which will be used to inform and mainstream adaptation into key national policies and regional strategies.

# A.20. Executing Entity information

## A.21. Executive summary

### 1. The Central American Dry Corridor and the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic are among the world's most vulnerable tropical regions to the impacts of climate change, with Honduras, Nicaragua, Guatemala, the Dominican Republic and El Salvador recognized as being among the system most vulnerable countries in the world to climate change.

The Central American Dry Corridor emerged in the last three decades due to describing the increasing frequency and intensity of droughts in the region. The Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic are also affected by the impacts of climate change and have had severe impacts on agriculture and food security in the region. The impacts of droughts, floods, and other extreme weather events in the region are exacerbated by the vulnerability linked to concurrent changes in La Niña. This extensive result rainfall leads to reductions in evapotranspiration as well as increases in soil moisture content, which can lead to increased runoff and reduced water management practices – which result in extensive environmental degradation – and widespread poverty.

### 2. With more than one million families in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones reliant on agriculture for their livelihoods, the impacts of climate change have had a significant impact on food security and are likely to increase humanitarian assistance. In 2014 alone, intense drought resulted in an estimated US$656 million worth of agricultural losses in Central America. The region has experienced more frequent and severe El Niño events from 2018, food insecurity has increased annually in the region with indigenous populations, women and rural areas being most affected. In 2017 alone, 3.4 million people were estimated to be food insecure while 5.6 million people had experienced crop losses that year, with 1.4 million urgently needing food assistance. These impacts are expected to increase in the future.

### 3. Climate projections for the region indicate that by the end of the century, temperatures will increase by 2–3°C under RCP4.5 and by 6–7°C under RCP8.5 (0.5 yr at a time). At the same time, mean annual rainfall is projected to decline in most parts of Central America. These changes will be consistent with the La Niña cycle, which has resulted in more frequent and severe El Niño events from 2018 onwards.

## governments have committed US$53.1 million in co-finance which will further scale and mainstream ESA, water and resource-efficiency in their respective countries.

### 6. Together, these interventions will catalyse the autonomous upscaling of ESA and water- and resource-efficient technologies through policy reforms, enabling policy instruments and accessible financial mechanisms.
This approach will transform the financial landscape in the region to enable vulnerable groups to access climate finance for adaptation and resilience building. The programme will directly benefit 10,786,441 people across the target catchments through on-the-ground interventions, with an additional 35,920,000 people indirectly benefiting from these activities. This is achieved by leveraging financial mechanisms established under the programme (lending and guarantee facilities). Furthermore, the combined impact of the project will be felt in 148 municipalities across the region, ultimately directly benefiting 1,364,044 people across the region during the programme lifetime.

### 7. The programme will be executed by three institutions with clear roles according to their capacities and role in the region. CABEI will be the accredited entity for the programme, but will also be directly executing the major part of output 1 (activity 2.3) and output 4 (activities 4.5). The World Bank will be responsible for the implementation of financial facilities of the programme CABEI will therefore be the executing entity for activities 2.3 (grant facility), 4.1, and 4.2. UNEP will provide technical advice to CABEI on the design and deployment of financial mechanisms under output 4 (activity 4.5), supported by UNEP's technical advisor. The responsibility of output 4 is thus shared between CABEI and UNEP. CABEI will also be responsible for the implementation of the Ecosystem-based Adaptation demonstration sites throughout the region as the lead executing entity, with direct support from UNEP. UNEP will provide technical advice to CABEI on the design and implementation of the Ecosystem-based Adaptation demonstration sites in participant countries through the direct execution of output 3 focused on gathering and disseminating key results and lessons learned. CABEI will also be responsible for mainstreaming ESA into national policies, while UNEP will mainstream adaptation into key national policies and regional strategies. UNEP will provide technical advice to CABEI on the design and deployment of financial mechanisms under output 4 (activity 4.5). UNEP will also play a key role in the technical ecosystem services valuation and technical guidelines for ESA implementation. UNEP will play a key role also in the technical experience already developed in the region on the design and deployment of financial mechanisms for ESA and on ESA mainstreaming into national policies.

# PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Context

### 8. The Dry Corridor of Central America consists of semi-arid and sub-humid ecosystems that cover most of the Pacific slope of Guatemala, Honduras, El Salvador and Nicaragua, as well as smaller areas of Costa Rica and Panama. It is a region with high population density (Figure 1). The Dry Corridor covers an area of approximately 205,000 km² in the west of the Dominican Republic (Figure 1). The Dry Corridor is the most densely populated region of Central America, with over 37 million people living there. This represents about one-third of the total population – 12% of the total, amounting to ~1.2 million people. In addition, Guatemala, Honduras, El Salvador and Nicaragua are among the countries in Latin America that have the highest levels of inequality. Costa Rica, Panama and the Dominican Republic are comparatively wealthy and have relatively low poverty levels. However, all these countries have high income inequality, with instances of extreme poverty – particularly in rural areas.

Figure 1: Current (red) and projected extent in 2050 (red and dark grey) of the Central American Dry Corridor and the Dominican Republic’s Arid Zone. The projection is based on the Global Climate Centre for Tropical Agriculture (CIRI), using the current climate distribution, future scenarios and evapotranspiration estimations.

Across the region, poverty in rural areas is significantly higher than in urban areas. Nearly 60% of people in the Dry Corridor live in extreme poverty compared to only about 25% living in urban areas. This is a major concern for the country.* A further example is that in Costa Rica double the number of people in rural areas are living in extreme poverty compared with urban areas.* Similarly, in Panama prevalence of extreme poverty is 27%.

*with a population of 10.5 million, representing ~32% of the total population living on six countries which it extends.

This projection is based on the Global Climate Centre for Tropical Agriculture (CIRI), using the current climate distribution and future scenarios. The projection was made in August 2015 as part of the Second National Adaptation Plan (NAP) Action Area 2: Building Resilience.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND-FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 8 OF 96

## Climate rationale

### The Dry Corridor and And Zones are among the world's most vulnerable tropical regions to the impacts of climate change[1], with Honduras, Nicaragua, Guatemala, the Dominican Republic and El Salvador recognized as being among the fifteen most vulnerable countries in the world to extreme climate events[2]. The term "Dry Corridor" refers to a region that stretches from southern Mexico through Central America into northern South America. This region is characterized by its high vulnerability to climate change impacts due to its unique combination of factors, including poverty and limited access to resources.

### Climate risks

#### Past climate changes and future projections show that the region's climate is changing in several ways, including temperature increases across the region[3], and a shifting rainfall pattern. In most parts of the Dry Corridor and And Zones, rain seasons are shortening and the intensity of midsummer droughts is increasing[4]. Droughts have become more frequent and intense over time, with the number of days without precipitation increasing from 10 to 25 in some areas.

#### The magnitude of change is expected to vary among countries and between geographical areas within countries (Figure 3). For example, temperature increases are projected to be greater for low-elevation regions than high-elevation regions. Similarly, rainfall changes will differ depending on the region's climate[5].

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | Page 9 OF 96

## GREEN CLIMATE

### Extreme events and climate change in Central America

**Abstract:** Similarly, temperatures of cold days[30] are predicted to increase across the region. The rate of these increases is projected to be strongest over the Pacific coasts of Nicaragua, El Salvador, Honduras and Guatemala (Figure 5). In addition, the frequency of extreme wet events is expected to increase by about 1% on average by the end of the century – extreme rainfall is predicted to increase significantly across parts of Central America. The number of consecutive dry spells[30] exceeding 20 days in length and extreme events[30] occurring each year is predicted to increase (Figure 5). The R50mm index is used to represent precipitation intensity, which is defined as the amount of rainfall falling within a given time period that exceeds the 90th percentile for that time period. This index shows an increasing trend in frequency over the Atlantic coast of Costa Rica and northern parts of Panama[31]. A similar trend is observed in projections for the Pacific coast of El Salvador, Honduras, Guatemala and Nicaragua (Figure 5). The R20mm index also shows a similar trend but at a smaller rate. In the Pacific coast of El Salvador, Honduras, and Guatemala the R50mm projection shows some decrease[31], which is attributed to a decrease of winds or hurricane activity.

### 13. Recent evidence[32] and model projections predict that the risk of drought will increase with climate change, as much more frequent and intense Nino events[33] are projected to occur in the region. The frequency of extreme wet spells is expected to increase by about 1% on average by the end of the century – extreme rainfalls are likely to happen twice as often compared to current conditions (Figure 5). The Dry Corridor[34] and Arid Zone[35] have experienced a decrease in rainfall over the last few decades. The number of dry spells is defined as the number of consecutive days with no precipitation or minimal-rain fall. From 2021, the frequency of dry spells will increase by about 1% on average per year (Figure 6). While the number of dry spells is projected to decrease in parts of Panama[34], Costa Rica, Nicaragua and Honduras, it is expected to increase across most parts of El Salvador, Guatemala and northern parts of Mexico (Figure 6)[36]. In addition, annual mean water excess[37] is predicted to decrease across most parts of the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone. The number of consecutive days with no precipitation or minimal-rain fall will also increase in intensity and frequency under both medium emissions scenarios (RCP4.5) high emissions scenario (RCP8.5), for both the 2010–2039 period and the mid-century (2041–2070). The number of consecutive dry spells is expected to increase by about 1% on average per year from 2021, becoming more pronounced at the mid-century (2041–2070) before becoming stable in the long-term. This will be accompanied by an increase in the spread of aridity in parts of the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone as the availability of water is reduced, which could have a significant impact on agriculture[38]. The number of consecutive dry spells is expected to decrease during rainy season with two peaks separated by a day of periods of wetness over a month in duration; termed the midsummer drought[39] (Figure 6). In addition, the frequency of extreme wet events will increase for the M50SD period, which is projected to increase by an average of over a week. Additionally, the MSD maximum precipitation is predicted to decrease by an average of over 20%, with

[30] Monthly minimum value of daily temperature.

[31] The R50mm index represents precipitation intensity, which is defined as the amount of rainfall falling within a given time period that exceeds the 90th percentile for that time period. This index shows an increasing trend in frequency over the Atlantic coast of Costa Rica and northern parts of Panama (Figure 5). A similar trend is observed in projections for the Pacific coast of El Salvador, Honduras, Guatemala and Nicaragua (Figure 5).

[32] Dry spells affecting vegetation growth can be assessed through the relationship between actual ET (ETa) and potential ET (ETo) evapotranspiration, which is defined as the amount of water that would evaporate from a reference crop under standard conditions. The ratio of actual to potential evapotranspiration (ETa/ETo) is used in this study.

[33] Nino events are characterized by an increase in sea surface temperature and associated changes in atmospheric circulation, which can lead to increased precipitation over the tropical Pacific Ocean. This leads to a decrease in rainfall across Central America during El Niño years and an increase of rainfall during La Niña years (Figure 5). The frequency of extreme wet spells is expected to increase by about 1% on average by the end of the century – extreme rainfalls are likely to happen twice as often compared to current conditions (Figure 5).

[34] The Dry Corridor is defined as the area between the Pacific and Caribbean coasts, which includes parts of El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras and Nicaragua. It has experienced a decrease in rainfall over the last few decades.

[35] The Arid Zone is characterized by low precipitation levels and high evaporation rates, with an annual mean water excess below 0 mm. This area is projected to experience a further decrease in rainfall under both medium emissions scenarios (RCP4.5) high emissions scenario (RCP8.5), for both the 2010–2039 period and the mid-century (2041–2070). The number of consecutive dry spells is expected to increase by about 1% on average per year from 2021, becoming more pronounced at the mid-century (2041–2070) before becoming stable in the long-term. This will be accompanied by an increase in the spread of aridity in parts of the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone as the availability of water is reduced, which could have a significant impact on agriculture.

### Climate change impacts

14. Climate change is already having severe impacts on communities in the Dry Corridor and Andes Zones. Indeed, it is projected to have even more severe impacts by mid-century and beyond across Central America and South America, especially from Honduras, Guatemala and El Salvador[20]. In 2014, for example, severe drought across the region resulted in a loss of over 3 million tons of maize and beans. This affected approximately 5 million people and livestock. In Honduras, Guatemala and El Salvador alone, a total ~2.5 million people experienced food insecurity due to this drought[21]. The same year, a severe drought in Mexico also had devastating effects: it resulted in over 3.5 million people requiring humanitarian assistance across the region[20]. This trend is continuing, with about half of the maize and beans produced in Central America expected to be lost by mid-century due to climate change. More than half of their maize and bean crops in 2018, with 2.2 million people affected[22]. These recent droughts have been linked to a number of factors including El Niño events, but are also associated with climate change.

15. Future predicted impacts of climate change include decreased harvests of maize, beans, coffee and other important crops in Central America (see Figure 4). For example, the projected decrease in maize yields is between 2% to 30% by mid-century and up to 60% by 2100. Similarly, coffee yields have been predicted to be between 7% in 2020 and 39% in 2100 across all Central American countries[23]. The projected decrease in maize yield is expected to range from 45% in 2020 and 185% in 2100. These reductions will result from direct climate impacts (e.g., droughts and floods) as well as indirect effects such as increased competition for water resources[24]. Climate change may also impact the land area suitable for growing specific crops, with declining areas expected for coffee and beans across Central America by 2100. This is likely to have significant economic impacts on smallholder farmers’ suitability for producing coffee (40% or more)[25]. Similarly, modeling of maize indicates that land suitability is

## Climate change is projected to increase by up to 34% in El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras and Nicaragua[5]. Although some cooler highland areas that are currently unsuitable for these crops may become more suitable as temperatures increase, the overall effect of climate change will be a reduction in crop yields. This will lead to increased competition for water resources within zones of river catchments thereby reducing aquifer recharge[6]. This impact, coupled with an increase in land degradation and desertification, will have significant implications on food security and livelihoods. In addition, the projected increase in frequency of wildfires, causing further forest degradation and threatening farming, forest-based livelihoods and freshwater supply[7].

### 18. The climate change impacts described above are exacerbated by the baseline problems of forest loss and land degradation. Even with improved agricultural practices and increased investment in agriculture through various interventions, agricultural losses will continue to increase, along with ecosystem loss, water shortages, poverty and food insecurity. This is particularly true for vulnerable communities in the Dry Corridor and And Zoness which are already extremely vulnerable to climate change.

### 17. The programme will target seven catchments for interventions — one in each of the participating countries. In these catchments, the application of the Soil and Water Assessment Tool (SWAT) allows for the analysis potential impacts of climate change on water resources and land use. However, the combination of historical meteorological data and site-specific climate change projections are very limited. According to the modelling results, there were 10 meteorological stations in the Dry Corridor. Only four of these countries, there were stations within the prioritized pilot sites and just one of them (Honduras) had complete observed rainfall and temperature data for a period of over 50 years with less than 15% of discontinuity.

Economic analysis of the benefits of land restoration in Latin America provides evidence to support that the adoption of policy measures and removal of barriers to land restoration will be by national and regional governments, as well as by private sector actors, which will contribute to the production of food security and environmental sustainability. ## Complementary with related projects, programs and initiatives

### 27. Considerable investments have been and are being made through public expenditure and donor-funded initiatives to address the impacts of climate change throughout the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones.

#### Lessons learned and best practices from these investments have been incorporated into the design of the proposed programme. The proposed programme will complement existing efforts by: a) building on lessons between the programmes and existing initiatives; b) identifying new opportunities for collaboration with existing initiatives; c) ensuring that the proposed programme complements other climate change-related initiatives in the region.

### 28. CABI is implementing the GCF-funded Productive Investment Initiative for Adaptation to Climate Change (CABII) programme in the same seven countries the proposed programme will work in but areas outside of the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. The CABII programme aims to: a) increase access to financing through CABII's accredited intermediary financial institutions (PFIs) for the implementation of adaptation measures; b) build capacity among PFIs, including technical assistance from CABII, to provide climate-smart technical and business skills; and ii) establish an incentive scheme to promote adaptation measures in priority sectors.

### 29. The proposed programmes will be complementary to CABII in several regards. First, the two operate in complementary geographical areas: CABII is focused on areas where there is demand from MFMEs – the proposed programme will only work within the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. Second, the two have different focus sectors: CABII's focus sector selection is based on the most vulnerable municipalities in which this programme will be focused. Second, the two have different target groups: CABII works with individual producers and their associated groups, focusing specifically on the most vulnerable enterprises; while the proposed programme will work through financial intermediation with PFIs, channeling funds through credit.

### 30. In addition to the above, the technical assistance provided by the two programmes will be complementary. CABII is providing a variety of technical assistance to MFMEs and MFMEs, where the beneficiaries receive either direct or indirect technical assistance from CABII. The proposed programme otherwise offers technical assistance – both practical and theoretical trainings – for financial intermediaries in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. These trainings will focus on: (a) the technical areas in climate change; (b) accessing and channeling funds through established financial

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 98 OF 164

## B.2. Theory of change (max: 1000 words; approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

### 31. In addition to CAMBII, the proposed program will align with the Microfinance for EEA (MFEA) project that is being implemented in eight countries across Latin America and Africa, including three of the targeted Central American countries. The MFEA project aims to increase access to financial services by facilitating microfinance products aimed at small-scale farmers to invest in climate-smart agricultural practices. This will be achieved through a combination of direct lending and lessons learned from the CAMBII approach, which focused through MEA to guide the establishment of financial mechanisms for the Dry Corridor Zone.

### 32. Several national projects are being implemented in Central America with a focus on water-efficient technologies, EEA and land restoration to improve ecosystem services and livelihoods, increase food security, and reduce vulnerability to climate change. These include: (i) implementing agro-ecological practices such as soil conservation measures in the Dry Corridor agro-ecosystem of El Salvador (RECLIMA); and “Building livelihood resilience to climate change through improved water management” in Guatemala. The proposed program will support the implementation of several adaptation measures that will be replicated and scaled up regionally by the proposed program, including: (i) soil conservation; (ii) agro-ecological practices such as rainwater harvesting; (iii) reforestation; and (iv) water-efficient agricultural technologies such as drip irrigation. The proposed program will also support the implementation of a number of other adaptation measures that are expected to increase access to financial products and services under Component 2 of the proposed program will ensure that a large number of small-scale farmers in the region have access to these services by 2030. The proposed program will also contribute directly to restoration commitments made by several countries under Initiative 4.1 of the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Strategic Plan, which aims at restoring degraded land across Central America and the Caribbean by 2030. The proposed program will contribute directly to goals by restoring >9,250 hectares of forested area across the seven catchments targeted by the programme.

### 33. Finally, a project funded by the German Federal Ministry for the Environment, Nature Conservation and Nuclear Safety (BMU) has been implemented from 2012-2019 by the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) in collaboration with the governments of El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua and Panama. This project — Transforming evidence into change: a holistic approach to governance for EEA in Central America — has been implemented across five countries in Central America, including developing an evidence base for its implementation. One catchment per country was selected to demonstrate EEA measures that are relevant to the region as a whole. The project has generated over 100 publications with information about over 100 EEA-related cases in the region, and a virtual platform known as “EAA Solutions” to share lessons learned from these cases. The project also developed an online tool for identifying EEA projects by type of activity; its availability and management of water resources; and its ecosystem protection architecture. The project has demonstrated that it is possible to implement EEA measures in Central America when implementing a bottom-up approach focused on promoting water and food security in the region. It also successfully implemented EEA measures under GEF6 in several additional catchments within the Dry Corridor and And Zones.

# Description of the problem

34. Climate change is causing increasingly frequent and intense droughts, as well as a higher frequency of extreme heat waves in the Dry Corridor region of Central America and the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic. These areas are densely populated and have high levels of poverty — concentrated in rural areas where most people depend on agriculture for their livelihoods.

# Preferred adaptation solution

35. The preferred adaptation solution to the above-described problem is to improve the climate resilience of rural communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones by increasing water availability, improving hydrological flow and infiltration rate into groundwater reserves through improved land management practices, and promoting water-efficient technologies at farm- and household-level. The selection/evaluation of the specific EEA measures to be implemented will be based on the results of the feasibility study described in Section 6 of Annex B.

# Barriers to the preferred adaptation solution

36. The proposed programs will address the following eight barriers to the preferred adaptation solution in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones. These barriers are detailed in further detail in Section 11 of Annex A: Feasibility Study.


| Barrier | Limited knowledge and understanding of climate change impacts among decision-makers. Decision-makers lack a clear understanding of how climate change will affect their communities, including its impacts, and how these impacts affect farming. These gaps include knowledge on (i) water availability; (ii) areas vulnerable to droughts and heat waves; (iii) the effects of climate change on crop yields; and (iv) adaptation strategies. These knowledge gaps impede the implementation of adaptation measures such as water-efficient technologies, improved land management practices in particular for the selected pilot sites, the availability of hydrological observed meteorological data is very limited as well as the lack of historical drought records. The only country with a long-term record of rainfall and temperature is the limits of the Dry Corridor. For only three of the countries, there were stations within the prioritized pilot sites and just one station had complete observed rainfall and temperature data for a period of over 30 years with less than 15% of discontinuity.
| Barrier | Limited knowledge and understanding of EEA and water-efficient adaptation measures among decision-makers. Decision-makers lack an understanding of how to implement EEA and water-efficient technologies, as well as the benefits these technologies provide in terms of increased water availability and improved land management practices. This is linked to a lack of knowledge and understanding of EEA and of adaptation measures such as water- and resource-efficient technologies. These gaps are limited to decision-makers involved directly in the value of ecosystem services.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 20 OF 96

## Barrier 3: Inefficient implementation of existing policies on climate change adaptation and limited integration of EBA and other adaptation measures into sectoral policies.
Climate change adaptation is a priority in the NDCs of many countries, but there are significant barriers to its effective implementation. These include insufficient funding for climate change adaptation activities, the need to restore, conserve and sustainably manage the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone forests, wetlands and agroforestry systems, as well as limited institutional capacity at national level to develop and implement knowledge and technical capacity to implement existing policies, preventing countries from achieving the necessary impacts.

## Barrier 4: Limited technical capacity within governments and local communities to implement EBA and other adaptation measures.
Governments have gradually reduced public budgets for the provision of extension services that play a key role in supporting farmers’ adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices. This has led to a decline in the availability of extension services, and limited access to training and technical assistance to implement climate-resilient practices at landscape-, community- and household-level.

## Barrier 5: Limited knowledge and technical capacity to adopt technologies and approaches for efficient water use.
Technical barriers exist that prevent farmers from adopting EBA and other adaptation measures. These include the lack of information on how to effectively integrate these measures into existing agricultural systems, as well as limited access to training and extension services. As a result, there is limited information on water management and improved agricultural practices in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone. In addition, there are few examples of successful water-efficient technologies that are known for large commercial farming (e.g., drip irrigation) or smallholder farmers.

## Barrier 6: Limited access for financial institutions to specific refinancing options for the implementation of EBA- water- and resource-efficient technologies in the private sector.
Private sector investors, credit agencies and microfinance institutions have limited knowledge about how to finance the adoption of EBA and other adaptation measures. In addition, there is a lack of information on how to access specific EBA refinancing options. As a result, financial institutions are not taking into account important long-term benefits when evaluating projects that adopt EBA approaches.

## Barrier 7: Limited access to credit for adaptation interventions among vulnerable populations.
Smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups have limited access to credit from financial institutions to finance the adoption of EBA- water- and resource-efficient technologies. However, the high risk of such credit to financial institutions limits the availability of credit to vulnerable groups.

## Barrier 8: Absence or limited development of economic incentives for investment in sustainable natural resource management and EBA.
Although some water funds and schemes for payment for environmental services (PES) exist in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone, they are not well developed. In addition, there is a lack of financial instruments to support smallholder farmers to reduce their risk in providing credit to individuals and small businesses with little collateral and limited access to credit.

## Programme objective
The proposed programme’s activities have been designed to overcome the above-mentioned barriers and contribute towards achieving the objective of strengthening the adaptive capacity of vulnerable people, including smallholder and commercial farmers, as well as entrepreneurs in rural communities in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone. This will be achieved by increasing access to climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies through improved agricultural and agroforestry practices, as well as reforestation and improved forest management. The programme will also contribute towards improving water availability for agriculture and water demand management, seeking governments promoting large-scale implementation of CCA and other adaptation measures in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zone. This will be achieved by strengthening institutional frameworks and mechanisms of transparent monitoring and management.

## Barriers 1 and 2 will be overcome predominantly through activities under Output 3.
The dissemination of knowledge products on climate change impacts, EBA and other adaptation measures through

## G

### Barrier 4 and 7 will be overcome mainly through activities under Output 4.

These include the establishment of a financial mechanism for EDA investments, which will be financed via a Mixed ESA finance structure with two main finances: namely a) (a) lending facility for small- and large-scale EDA investments at banks and other financial institutions; b) an equity facility to provide additional resources for participating intermediary financial institutions for on-lending. The establishment of this mechanism is expected to increase the capacity of financial institutions to be able to operationalize and deliver these new financial instruments, as well as to provide a wider range of products and services to finance EDA investments being offered by last mile service providers. This will also increase access to financing for EDA activities and other sustainable land management practices, an evident need that can be constructed based on the results of the baseline analysis and the identification of adaptation needs and priorities.

This will help to demonstrate the value and effectiveness of proposed adaptation activities as part of the GCF portfolio, and will contribute to the development of national adaptation plans. A methodology will also be developed to value ecosystem services. It will be used to guide local governments in making policy changes and implementing economic incentives — such as water funds — for EDA.

#### B.3. Project/programme description.
42. Programme interventions will be implemented in all six countries of the Dry Corridor — Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua and Panama — with the exception of the Choluteca watershed in Honduras (see Section 12 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study). A single catchment has been selected for each country based on a detailed climate change vulnerability assessment. The following are the selected catchments:

- **Guatemala** — Canillá, Chichimán, San Andrés Sajcabaja, Uspantán and Zaculeu in the Rio Salinas watershed.
- **Honduras** — Apaciquia, Choluteca, Doygue, Marcala, Moncagua, and Orocuina in the Choluteca watershed.

## Component 1: Mainstreaming of EAs, water-efficient technologies and natural-resource-based businesses into selected catchments

### Under Component 1, three interrelated outputs will facilitate the widespread implementation of adaptation measures across the region. This will include:
- Providing technical assistance to local government, farmers and rural communities to implement adaptation measures.
- Maintaining adaptation measures and increasing their effectiveness through the dissemination of water-efficient technologies (WETs) in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones.
- Implementing on-the-ground EAs and water-resource-efficient technologies across the region.

### Output 1: Strengthened technical capacity of local government, farmers and rural communities to implement EAs and other adaptation measures
Output 1 will be fully executed by FAO who will be responsible for:
- Establishing the National Project Execution Units in each country as well as hosting the Regional Project Management Unit.
- Implementing on-the-ground EAs and water-resource-efficient technologies across the region.

### Activities under Output 1: Will contribute to GCF Outcome AT02 – Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced vulnerability of local government, farmers and rural communities through the mainstreaming of EAs and natural resource-based businesses (NRBs) and alternative climate-resilient livelihoods.
A community-based approach will be applied in all countries. The activities under Output 1 will be executed directly through the national structure of FAO as well as through agreements with national partners, including local government, farmers’ organizations, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders.

### Activities under Output 2: Will contribute to GCF Outcome AT0 – Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced vulnerability of local government, farmers and rural communities through the mainstreaming of EAs and natural resource-based businesses (NRBs) and alternative climate-resilient livelihoods.
A community-based approach will be applied in all countries. The activities under Output 2 will be implemented during the first year of the programme as described in paragraphs 51 and 59, which will be implemented under Output 2a. These interventions will be selected from a pool of eligible interventions that are expected to meet all eligibility criteria and ultimately retain the final decision to approve implementation of all interventions under Output 2b.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.1 | PAGE 24 OF 96

## Technical assistance will also be provided to farmers and rural communities in 36 municipalities on the implementation of on-ground EEA practices, including silvopasture, agroforestry and sustainable land management techniques. The selection process for these municipalities will follow a participatory approach and regulatory frameworks at the local level focusing mainly on water management regulations and water resource use permits. The selection criteria will be based on the following: (a) the vulnerability of each watershed selected following a rigorous vulnerability assessment and extensive consultations with key stakeholders in each country; (b) the capacity of the communities to adopt EEA practices, including their awareness about climate change and rate of changes to which a system is exposed; (c) the sensitivity of the system to those changes; and (d) the potential for scaling up the interventions. The most vulnerable area among all watersheds in each country was identified as the watershed that is most likely to be affected by climate change, where the water supply is most critical, and where the livelihoods are most dependent on the health of the watershed. Details of the vulnerability assessment and site selection process are provided in Section 12 of Annex A: Feasibility Study.

### Country-specific implementation protocols will be developed for (i) the implementation of sustainable EEA practices; (ii) the addition of water-efficient technologies, and (iii) the adoption of resource-efficient technologies that reduce fuelwood demand. These protocols will be developed by the local experts in the Project Execution Units (PEUs), with the technical support of the Technical Assistance Unit (TAU). The TAU will provide supervision of the Executive Unit (EU) prior to the implementation of on-ground interventions, in each country, and will also provide ongoing technical assistance. These protocols will include: (a) a training protocol; (b) a training-of-trainers protocol for local communities; (c) a local community engagement protocol; (d) an environmental awareness-based organization, water committees, local cooperatives, environmental and natural resource management groups, and other relevant stakeholders; (e) a monitoring and evaluation plan; and (f) a training manual. Training will be provided to the communities on the development of small-scale agroforestry mosaics, particularly considering their active role in rain and fuelwood use. Site visits will be arranged to demonstrate the benefits of EEA practices and water-efficient technologies. Communities will also be trained on how to identify and manage invasive species and other environmental issues that may arise from the implementation of EEA interventions.

### In addition to the technical assistance provided for on-ground implementation of EEA practices and water/resource-efficient technologies, training will be provided to communities on the development of small-scale agroforestry mosaics. The training will also include a component on how to identify and manage invasive species and other environmental issues that may arise from the implementation of EEA interventions.

### Activity 1.2: Provide technical assistance to municipal authorities, farmers and rural communities for the implementation of EEA practices in priority watersheds.
Activity 1.3: Provide technical assistance to farmers and rural communities for the development of natural resource management plans and alternative climate-resilient livelihoods.

## Output 2 Demonstration adaptation interventions implemented in rural communities across seven target catchments in the Dry Corridor and Arid Zones

### Technical assistance will not be limited to farmers accessing the financial mechanisms from Output 1, but to any farmers in the target municipalities that are open to adopting improved practices but lack the technical knowledge to do so.

## Adaptive Management Output 3 will contribute to GCF Outcome AT-9 — Strengthened adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks — by providing the necessary resources for the on-the-ground implementation of EBA interventions in seven catchments, each covering approximately 1 million ha. The selected interventions are targeted at vulnerable communities in 36 municipalities*, that lack the technical and financial capacity to implement these measures.

The hydrological modeling undertaken for the programme areas (described in Section B.1 of the proposal and in Annex A: Hydrological Modeling) has identified a range of potential EBA interventions, including structural interventions, such as forest conservation, reforestation, transition to agroforestry systems, introduction of drought-resistant crops, and water harvesting structures, as well as non-structural interventions, such as improved land use planning and community-based monitoring. These interventions are designed to reduce the vulnerability of communities in the catchment areas. As a result, a combination of adaptation measures will be implemented in each of the target municipalities, with the specific mix of interventions varying according to the identified vulnerabilities based on hydrological parameters.

BAF will be the EE for the implementation of these measures under activities 21 and T-7. The EEA will be the EE for the development of the monitoring framework and the provision of examples to summoning communities on how to implement sustainable practices. CABE will be the EE for the implementation of the adaptive management component.

### Activity 50: Work with local communities in each municipality to implement on-the-ground EBA interventions that provide multiple ecosystem goods and services to vulnerable communities

The relevant organizations and institutions have been identified, and a list of potential EBA interventions has been identified for each municipality based on community consultations, sectoral expert workshops, and the hydrological modeling. The selected interventions remain as indicative and are subject to validation during the inception phase.

To foster grant support for identification, incubation or even acceleration of site-specific solutions and distributed models, a pilot regional grant facility will be established to support site-specific Ebas and water/resource-efficient technologies for the most vulnerable communities who are unable to access loan finance.

A non-profit organization (NPO) will be selected by CAREB as the executing entity to manage the grant applications. The Regional Project Steering Committee (RSPSC)—comprising representatives from FAO, CCAD, UNDP, and CAREB—will review and approve the proposals submitted for funding. The RSPSC will define the eligibility criteria for each country at program inception, with final sign-off provided by CAREB as the the Executive Entity.

53. Legal entities can refer to business companies, unions or cooperatives, associations, cooperative sector organisations, public research and training centres and NGOs that directly cater to target and beneficiaries.

54. The qualifying stakeholders will be invited to apply for funding following the application process of the grant mechanism. The criteria for selection include: a) the ability to demonstrate strong capacity in knowledge innovation, service and input innovation and sector organizations and institutions strengthening; b) have or access to a clear business plan; c) New or additional investment capital of existing or new projects; d) Seek funding from new project proposals.

55. National Project Executive Units (NPEUs) will guide communities on how to develop proposals to access the funds, and will be responsible for selecting the most suitable Ebas and water/resource-efficient technologies (WRETs) and will be based in the selected municipalities for the execution of the Eba demonstration sites. NPEUs will identify and select the most appropriate Ebas and WRETs from the following categories: 1) Business linkage innovations, focusing on strengthening commercial cooperation agreements between existing businesses or new business models; 2) Community-based solutions, such as water supply stand-alone solutions), a set of Eba solution providers (e.g. drip irrigation system, water reservoir installation, organic farming, etc.), and the establishment of a community-based organization to manage the provision of Ebas solutions and their finance, bundled in a comprehensive offer to end beneficiaries; 2) Service and input innovations, focusing on strengthening service delivery and access to inputs for smallholder farmers, where services can be “imported” to be made available; and 3) Strengthening of sector organizations and institutions, such as multi-distributor water funds, producer associations or municipal and community contribution entities.

56. On the ground, EBA-oriented grant support will benefit community-based institutions including micro-municipalities (MMs), cooperatives, unions, associations, cooperative sector organisations (CSOs). Eligible projects will include community-level investments, the establishment of water funds, and the provision of services and inputs to smallholder farmers. The grant facility will channel investment to selected Ebas and WRETs that are in line with the national development priorities and best practices and/or water- and resource-efficient technologies. The grant facility will channel investment to selected Ebas and WRETs that are in line with the national development priorities and best practices and/or water- and resource-efficient technologies.

62. Output 3 will also be a key component of the programme in the generation and strengthening of regional climate information and climate impact analysis. The collaboration with regional researchers (sub-activity 3.1.3) under Activity 3.1 will support the development of a regional climate change adaptation strategy, including an effective risk management framework for climate change adaptation, as well as the generation of evidence on the benefits of Nature Based Solutions as an efficient and cost-effective approach to climate change adaptation. The activities will also contribute towards the first year of implementation generating the scientific inputs for the development of the sectoral specific plans:

* **Activity 3.2:** Raise awareness of financial mechanisms for the implementation of CCA interventions.

## Component 2: Financing and implementation of EAs as well as water- and resource-efficient technologies across the region

### Output 3.4: Establish financing arrangements for the implementation of EAs in the Dry Corridor and other regions

The execution arrangements for this component will be distributed among CAREI and FAO. CAREI will be responsible for those activities related to the set up and management of the EA Financing mechanism, including the preparation of a financial plan and budget, as well as the establishment of an institutional framework in relation to the financial mechanism (Activities 4.1 and 4.2). FAO will be responsible for the execution of the preparatory activities required for the implementation of EAs in the Dry Corridor and other regions, including investments undertaken at local level by the local financial institutions through the Project Execution Units (Activity 4.3). LID will be responsible for the preparation of a project proposal to the GCF for funding, as well as support for financial instruments, support in the development of MRV systems and annual support in the monitoring of progress towards the achievement of the outputs under this component (Activities 4.1 and 4.2).

The second objective of the financing programme will be to facilitate additional financial mechanisms that will facilitate investment in EAs practices, as well as the adoption of water- and resource-efficient technologies (Activity 4.3). The establishment of a regional fund for climate change adaptation (RFFCA) through the MEAa project to increase access to climate funding for local organizations and communities — including non-regulated financial institutions such as microfinance institutions (MFIs), cooperatives, credit unions, and others, or an existing network of intermediary financial institutions (IFIs). Using the increasing body of knowledge on EDA Resources, CAREI will support the development of a regional fund for climate change adaptation (RFFCA) and linked finance for EA investments as well as water- and resource-efficient technologies. This will provide an opportunity to scale up the financing mechanism through the established top-down financing model.

The types of EAs and water- and resource-efficient technologies eligible for financing through the established financial mechanisms include, but are not limited to (among others):

* **agroforestry systems;**
* **drip irrigation;**
* **agrosilvopastoral systems;**
* **windbreaks;**
* **fodder banks;**
* **mixed pastel nurseries;**
* **irrigation systems;**
* **soil conditioning;**
* **efficient water use;**
* **freeweed drying and charcoal production;**
* **water harvesting and small reservoirs;**
* **rooftop rainwater harvest systems;**
* **submersible pumping systems;**
* **drip irrigation.**

## Output 4: Financial products and services to finance EBA investments are offered by Partner Financial Institutions (PFI), including PFI access to EBA on-lending funds and support mechanisms.

### 6.6 The EBA lending facility (EBA credit line) will be established and operationalized with the GCF senior loan proposal, which is expected to be approved in Q2 of this year. The EBA credit line will serve as a blended finance mechanism at below-market conditions for small- and large-scale EBA investments at the farm-, enterprise-, and community-levels. It will also provide access to PFI lending funds through innovative financial mechanisms to facilitate investment in EBA practices, as well as the adoption of water- and resource-efficient technologies and climate-smart agricultural practices. The EBA credit line will be established under the PF1 level; while it will be established for a period of 10 years, loans to be disbursed to PFI are expected to have a maturity of up to 25 years. The EBA credit line will provide access to PFI lending funds with end beneficiaries, i.e., focusing more on working capital or investment capital, and in accordance with market standards, the EBA credit line will be structured as a revolving facility with an initial commitment period of the PF1 loans. Returning funds will be reinvested to available PFI's, ensuring a closing of laid out loans before the facility is closed.

### 67 The guarantee mechanism can be used to co-covco-financing, to support the initial introduction of the lending facilities and to provide access to PFI lending funds for small-scale farmers and enterprises that are most vulnerable, an end beneficiary segment that is usually without any access to any finance due to its elevated risk profile. The following figure describes the lending and guarantee facilities deployed under sub-activity 4.2.1 and 4.2.2 respectively.

## Financing Scheme - Lending and Guarantee facilities

### Loan through the Global Credit Line (GCL)

A detailed proposal for financing schemes under the Green Climate Fund (GCF) is outlined here.

#### Key Points:
- The GCF will use a loan.
- There is a tax credit to assist countries in accessing funds.
- The GCF has committed $1.5 billion, with about 60% disbursed as of this writing.
- A further $2 billion is available for disbursement within the Global Credit Line (GCL).
- The funds are released on balance sheet and can be used against the final beneficiaries.

#### Structure:
- **Lending Facility**: Provides loans to eligible entities under the GCF, which will manage the loan portfolio.
- **Guarantee Facility**: Provides guarantees to intermediaries for loans they make. These intermediaries include national banks, rural community banks, among others - Partner Financial Institutions. In parallel, these IFIs will receive GCF funds and CABIF funds to provide loans to final beneficiaries.

#### Investment Target:
Investment will be targeted towards community-level investment, rural MSMEs or other institutions in the EEA, as well as water- and resource-efficient technologies, for end beneficiaries across different sectors.


### Figure 11. Lending and guarantee facilities' Financing Scheme

By providing a blended financing mechanism composed of concessional finance from GCF in combination with a market rate senior debt co-financing from CABIF (and an associated guarantee facility - see above), intermediaries will be able to provide loans at lower interest rates than they would otherwise, or only limited offerings as of today. Intermediary Financial Institutions in CABIF's established network will manage the loan portfolio and disburse funds into two branches: one branch for loans disbursed under the GCF contract which is subject to the same conditions applicable to the GCF and CABIF, while the other branch is not subject to these conditions.

#### Eligibility Criteria:
1. **Partnership Financial Institutions**: Will be able to access the EIA lending facility at concessional below-market rates for loans disbursed under the GCF contract.
2. **Eligibility Criteria for PFIs** (see Section 6.7.1 of Annexes A and B in section 84, including legal and capacity requirements). While PFIs are not compliant with the minimum requirements set out in the EIA financing methodology, they will be able to access the EIA lending facility if the financial institution complies with the conditions for EIA finance methodologies which is a pre-condition prior to gaining access to the EIA Lending Facility and disbursement.

## Access to satellite data insights into the EEA lending portfolio and climate impacts on the portfolio: FAO will be the Executing Entity for this TA Facility (under Activity 4.3) and will receive technical support from UNEP.

### Facilitating access to and operation of the facilities
Activity 4.3 will provide TAs to selected PFFs. Technical assistance will be a mandatory condition for PFFs to access the EEA lending facility. This will ensure that consideration is given to the capacity needs of PFFs in order to manage the EEA finance. Such technical assistance will focus on developing capacity within municipalities and PFFs to: i) identify, assess, and prioritize climate risks; ii) develop adaptation strategies and action plans as to monitor and document the respective economic and ecosystem benefits where possible, Information and Communication Technology (ICT)-based tools for satellite data analytics and satellite data insights into the EEA lending portfolio and climate impacts on the portfolio.

### The TA facility
The TA facility is geared to focus primarily on direct technical assistance and a comprehensive Training-of-Trainers approach. This will include: i) the identification of key challenges related to climate change (e.g., land use, water scarcity); ii) the identification and provision of solutions to overcome these threats; iii) the application of innovative approaches for addressing climate risks (e.g., through the use of ICT-based tools and modern information management solutions), iv) the provision of specific EEA-oriented tailored-made financial products and services (e.g., insurance, microfinance, etc.) as well as capacity building in this area (e.g. training); and v) the monitoring, reporting and verification systems in place via technology transfer to support learning and knowledge sharing among PFFs. The TA facility will also provide a comprehensive package for financial intermediaries on the concepts, financial methodology, and tools to provide products and services oriented towards climate change adaptation and mitigation. This will be done through each country to provide country-specific technical assistance to PFFs based on a standardized methodology.

### Phased approach
The phased approach will allow for more time to strengthen capacities of the financial institutions and gradually increase their involvement as intermediaries and producers.

### The three implementation phases are described below:
#### Launching the loan facility
The loan facility will be initially deployed through PFFs to address priorities and requests from producers and communities in selected municipalities in the Tempisque basin. The specific eligibility criteria for the type of interventions to be included in the loan facility will be detailed during the first year of the programme through consultations with stakeholders, including producers and communities. A technical working group (TWG) system will also be structured to gather information about the deployment of the loan and guarantees facilities and the impact on the targeted municipalities and surrounding vulnerable areas. The TWG will include PFFs and technical experts engaged through output 3) will initiate a climate risk assessment and prioritization of other communities in the Tempisque basin.

#### Replicating the loan facility
As a result of the initial phase, the technical team (as above) and PFFs will count on information about the deployment of the loan facility and initial results from the impact of the financial interventions. The large climate risk assessments will have also provided information about climate risks and adaptation potential for additional municipalities in the Tempisque basin. In addition to the 5 prioritized municipalities, other communities in the 5 prioritized municipalities and other surrounding vulnerable ones within the Dry Corridor, the loan facility will be deployed with a focus on these communities and always within the limits of the Dry Corridor to ensure substantive action to build resilience.

#### Scale up of the loan facility
Finally, and after gathering more detailed information about the results of the deployment of the loan facility, the beneficiaries’ needs assessments and underlying substantive capacity building both with community members and PFFs in the 5 prioritized municipalities and other vulnerable ones within the Dry Corridor, the loan facility will be scaled up to include all communities in these municipalities and always within the limits of the Dry Corridor to ensure substantive action to build resilience.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 34 OF 96

The proposed lending facility was modelled at the regional and national levels to understand the absorption capacity on the ground, and is further described in annexes 3 (financial model) and 30 (demand analysis) of this proposal.

An initial review of existing and publicly available literature, from academic research to practitioner report and projects references, shows not only a growing interest into the topic of adaptation and EBA Finance of the global and Central African region but also a lack of information on the financial institutions willing and interested to engage in the field. Furthermore, there is no clear picture about the level of demand for EBA solutions and the potential demand for the implementation of EBA solutions and in consequence the demand for EBA Finance.

1. REDCMAP (Microfinance Network of Central Africa and the Caribbean) is the most important network of microfinance institutions in the region, with a total membership of 203 institutions as at June 2015, based on data on its members' performance.
The network represents a network of networks, organizing 7 country national networks into a regional one, representing 130 microfinance institutions that reach nearly 1.5 million clients via 1,211 branches. Of the 130 MFIs in the network, 84 are members of REDCMAP.
While REDCMAP is not representing the total of institutions active at all 7 countries, the network clearly provides the most aggregated reference data sets regarding especially inclusive rural finance which is one of the focus of this proposal.

In order to understand financial institutions' installed capacity, two dimensions were reviewed as preparation for the analysis:

1. The traction of inclusion agricultural finance in the seven countries (see also section 2.4.2 in annex 30).
2. The presence of financial institutions in the programme pilot areas in order to understand the level of potential demand and the willingness of financial institutions to engage in the implementation of EBA Finance as part of the programme.

According to indicators from the REDCMAP's MIF Index for June 2015, the microfinance sector has a total lending portfolio of US$34.8 million at 7 counties for 233,471 clients. This represents more than double the amount of loans disbursed in 2014 and shows that the microfinance sector's involvement in inclusive agricultural finance is a good proxy for its ability to serve EBA finance which will probably be targeted on agriculture terms.

Using the data collected, we defined three populations: due to key challenges such as:

- Rural and agricultural finance are more costly due to greater distances to cover for loan origination as well as repayment.
- Especially agricultural producers are considered more risky as, the low levels of technification of Small-scale producers makes them more prone to adverse climate change impacts.

Further, especially for EBA and other green finance, markets are not developed and documentation requirements are rigorous. This is a challenge that needs to be addressed in order to increase awareness and outreach in agricultural finance, especially due to the lack of concessional finance.
1. The need for more client awareness raising especially to not well known alternatives to traditional agriculture financing.
2. More time to be spent with client assessment, due diligence and loan evaluation.
3. Increased personnel training and re-training activities as consequence of higher complexity of EBA and climate related financial risks.

It can hence be expected that the proposed programme will address all these barriers by raising awareness and ultimately demand of target populations for EBA alternatives, providing digital process and data management solutions to reduce costs and improve efficiency in loan origination and disbursement, mitigate climate-related financial risks and provide concessional finance to content perceived risks.
As a result of the above considerations based on prioritization of the Regional Project Steering Committee, under the chair of CABEI, the following considerations are based on the assumption of an equal distribution - (and put the total amount to be disbursed)

## Costa Rica

- Over the past 4 years in relation to the existing agricultural microfinance portfolio.

### Results of the analysis of microfinance portfolios at the national level are summarized below:

#### Costa Rica

- At June 2020, a total agricultural loan portfolio of US$18 million to 10,181 clients has been reported
    * Representing the highest share of a total microfinance portfolio of US$354 million with 42%.
    * All loans are being managed following individual lending methodologies (as opposed to group lending or community bank lending methods).

- The existing US $16 million loan portfolio to 10,181 clients is almost equal to the total amount disbursed via the proposed Lending facility (US$17.5 million) and represents a significant proportion of the current outstanding portfolio.
    * In case of Costa Rica it is highly expected that only a minor part of 19% of that facility (or US$3.2 million) will actually be destined to Small-scale farmer ESA finance, as commercial farming in the more developed agricultural sector is not eligible for this type of credit.

#### Dominican Republic

- At June 2020, a total agricultural loan portfolio of US$18 million to 25,113 clients has been reported
    * Representing the second largest share of a total microfinance portfolio of US$479 million with 3.8%.
    * All loans are being managed following individual lending methodologies (as opposed to group lending or community bank lending methods).

- The existing loan portfolio of US $16 million to 25,113 clients represents 0.5 times the targeted ESA portfolio, which in case of DR is expected to be destined to Small-scale EsaEa finance by 49% - or with US$7.8 million roughly a 16% of the current outstanding portfolios.

#### El Salvador

- At June 2020, a total agricultural loan portfolio of US$38 million to 60,507 clients has been reported
    * Representing the third lowest share of a total microfinance portfolio of US$525 million with 12%.
    * Individual lending methodologies are used for 94% of the portfolio, with 7% using group lending and community bank lending methodologies each for smaller loan amounts – primarily in rural areas.

- The existing loan portfolio of US $38 million to 60,507 clients represents 1.2 times the targeted ESA portfolio.
    * In case of El Salvador is expected to be destined to Small-scale EsaEa finance by 54% - or with US$20.1 million roughly a 19% of the current outstanding portfolios.

#### Guatemala

- At June 2020, a total agricultural loan portfolio of US$8 million to 21,214 clients has been reported
    * Representing the fourth lowest share of a total microfinance portfolio of US$537 million with 1.5%.
    * Individual lending methodologies are used for 96% of the portfolio, with 7% using group lending and community bank lending methodologies each for smaller loan amounts – primarily in rural areas.

- The existing loan portfolio of US $8 million to 21,214 clients represents 2.5 times the targeted ESA portfolio.
    * In case of Guatemala is expected to be destined to Small-scale EsaEa finance by 52% - or with US$9.7 million roughly a 16% of the current outstanding portfolios.

#### Honduras

- At June 2020, a total agricultural loan portfolio of US$8 million to 75,713 clients has been reported
    * Representing the lowest share of a total microfinance portfolio of US$487 million with 23%.
    * Individual lending methodologies are used for 96% of the portfolio, with 3% and 1% using group lending and community bank lending methods each for smaller loan amounts – primarily in rural areas.

- The existing loan portfolio of US $8 million to 75,713 clients represents 2.4 times the targeted ESA portfolio.

The document discusses a Green Climate Fund proposal for Nicaragua and Panama, focusing on the expansion of lending facilities to support small-scale Emission Reductions from Avoided Deforestation (ERAD) projects.

### Nicaragua

- **Total Agricultural Loan Portfolio**: US$47 million in 21,851 clients.
- **Expected Shift**: Expected to be shifted to Small-scale ERAD finance by 60%, with US$10.2 million roughly equaling the current outstanding portfolio.

### Panama

- **Total Agricultural Loan Portfolio**: US$27 million in 30,197 clients.
- **Expected Shift**: Expected to be shifted to Small-scale ERAD finance by 80%, with US$12.5 million roughly equaling a 48% of the current outstanding portfolio.

### Comparisons and Financial Model

- Each country could absorb around US$14 billion in lending facilities.
- At the national level, total disbursement per capita over the lifetime of the lending facility is US$300 per year.
- This translates to an average disbursement of 980 loans per capita, per year, or about 82 loans per person (due to dynamics of building up the total facility as well as winding it down).
- The peak is expected with roughly 15% of the population in the first year and 43% by the end.
- Per country an expected 6 Partner Financial Institutions gain full access to the lending facility, disbursing around 20 loans in average (in peak: 37 more).

### Programmes

The programmes will be set up across concessional Emission Reductions from Avoided Deforestation (ERAD) at 25% of their current agricultural non-concessional portfolio. Disclosed over a period of years and accompanied with increased awareness raising and capacity building activities.

### Implementation

- The implementation is established in Annexes 23, including:
  - Emission Reductions from Avoided Deforestation (ERAD) finance rationale.
  - Governance framework.
  - Funding structure, size and risk management.
  - Risk mitigation measures.
  - Capacity building for lending facility operators.
  - Guarantee facility structure and manual.
  - Facilities closure.
- The document also includes a Summary of Emission Reductions from Avoided Deforestation (ERAD) finance as de-risking strategy.

### Activities

Activities will be implemented in two phases:
1. **Activity 4.1**: Set up and establish the financial structure for the lending and guarantee facilities.
2. **Activity 4.2**: Financing mechanisms of the EDA lending facility and guarantee facility.

Activities will also include capacity building, awareness raising, and non-regulated financial institutions access and channel funds for small- and large-scale ERAD investments.

### Figure: Graphic representation of implementation strategy

#### 9.5 Set up and prerequisites

During this first stage of the programs, the site-specific implementation plans based on vulnerability and hydrological assessments will be developed and finalized in consultation with communities and key actors including detailed climate risk assessments. These plans have been drafted for Costa Rica and Honduras (annex 31) and will be used as a reference to develop similar plans for the other countries involved.

The same approach will be developed for all other 5 countries during the first year following the procedure described in Annex 31.


#### The validation of these indicative interventions will therefore be based on the following criteria:

- Relevance to site-specific climate risks
- Potential impact on key development tasks
- Familiarity and acceptability by stakeholders
- Potential impact on different social sectors of the community
- Positive/impact potential on gender balance
- Technical feasibility
- Compliance with social and environmental safeguards
- Political feasibility
- Minimal level of risks and constraints for implementation
- Robustness of monitoring capacity
- Potential for sustained monitoring capacity

This stage is critical for the engagement of actors and the Monitoring and Evaluation committees will be established within the participating communities. Technical protocols will be designed for the implementation of the large-scale EIA interventions that will serve as capacity building, demonstration and promotion sites.

## Stage 2: Demonstration and demand generation

At this stage and after launching the programme and building on the engagement with local actors, pilot interventions defined by communities and included in component I will be implemented.

Agreements will be set up with research institutions to generate knowledge from programmes interventions on EIA solutions in the Dry Sub-Saharan region. This will allow for identification of potential alternative livelihoods and adaptation financing through a financial mechanism based on vulnerability assessment, hydrological modelling and the regional hub plan.

A grant facility will be set up to strengthen the EBA lending ecosystem in the region including training of commercial and subsistence farmers on EIA measures implementation. The selection criteria for this facility will be based on specific priorities.

At this stage, PFIs will be working on developing the blended EIA lending facility with potential network institutions and non-network institutions. MRF systems will be established and formalized with IPs and PFIs.

The loan and guarantees facilities will make their initial disbursements at this stage. A schematic representation of this intervention phase is provided below:

| Identification of potential alternative livelihoods and adaptation financing opportunities for initial funding through the Grant facility | 1.5, 2.3 |  |  |
| Set up of the grant facility; definition of eligibility criteria based on specific aims and priorities | 2.3 |  |  |

## Implementation

This stage will focus on full implementation of the grant financed activities making emphasis on awareness raising and capacity building of vulnerable communities through demonstration of EDA solutions and benefits through pilot demonstrations.

The grant facility will also be in full implementation at this stage and results from investments will serve as input for further scaling up. The project will continue to work with national governments and other stakeholders, including with national governments at the regional level to inform National Adaptation Plans and Regional strategies.

The deployment of the loan facility will follow a phased approach along the programme to ensure cohesive and sustainable development of the financial sector in the Dry Corridor region. This will be achieved by providing loans for priorities for other vulnerable regions within the Dry Corridor where the intervention could be scaled up. This phased approach will also allow for building capacity with local financial institutions, and to promote the financial mechanism with communities and producers.

The three implementation phases of the loan facility are described below:

### Launching the loan facility

| Period | 1st quarter 2019 - 3rd quarter 2019 |
| Period | 4th quarter 2019 - 6th quarter 2019 |
| Period | 7th quarter 2019 - 9th quarter 2019 |

| --- | --- |

### Conditions for EDA Disbursement

- **Condition 1:** The project must have received the approval of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and/or the National Development Strategy (NDS).
- **Condition 2:** The project must have received the approval of the Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA), Social Impact Assessment (SIA), and Financial.
- **Condition 3:** The project must have received the approval of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and/or the National Development Strategy (NDS).
- **Condition 4:** The project must have received the approval of the Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA), Social Impact Assessment (SIA), and Financial.

### Eligibility criteria

- **Eligible entities:**
  - Government institutions
  - Private sector companies
  - NGOs, CSOs, and other civil society organizations
  - Financial institutions
  - Farmers' associations and cooperatives
  - Women's groups and women's organizations
  - Indigenous communities and indigenous peoples
  - Youth organizations

- **Year 3:** Q12, Q13, Q14, Q15

## Knowledge generation - MRV

At this stage, the programme will have already produced a substantive amount of results that will serve as input to develop knowledge products to be disseminated through the knowledge hub, including resilient landscape management practices and tools, methodologies and guidelines for the assessment of ecosystem services as well as technical assistance, business opportunities, technical guidelines for the assessment of ecosystem services, and other relevant information. These will be developed in close collaboration with governments across the region through the Regional Knowledge Hub with government institutions across the region.

The MRV system (the MRV and PFP) will be systematized by the respective national committees to contribute to sharing results, tools and methodologies through the Regional Knowledge Hub with financial institutions across the region. The MRV system will also benefit from the expansion of ESA credit lines based on demand generated through training, technical assistance and demonstration pilots to each region via the T4A corridor. ## Steps for the elaboration of site-specific intervention plan

The following figure shows the linkages of the programme components to the site specific Eba plan that will be developed during the first phase of the proposal.

| Site-Specific EBA Plan |
| --- |
| **Site-Specific EBA Plan** |
| **1. Vulnerability assessment** |
