| **3. Recommendations on vulnerability reduction measures** |

### Site-Specific EBA Plan

#### Step 1: Vulnerability Assessment
- **Purpose:** To identify and assess vulnerabilities in the selected catchments.
- **Components:**
  - **Vulnerability assessment tools:** Used to evaluate exposure, sensitivity, adaptive capacity, etc.
  - **Variables used for exposure, sensitivity, and adaptive capacity:** Access to credit and irrigation, agricultural employment, among others.

#### Step 2: Identification of up to four prioritized catchment areas within the Dry Corridor
- **Purpose:** To select up to four catchments based on interest per country in the Dry Corridor.
- **Process:** Workshops held with government representatives for each prioritized catchment area.

#### Step 3: Recommendations on vulnerability reduction measures
- **Purpose:** To develop recommendations for reducing vulnerabilities identified in the catchments.


---
**Page 46**
---

Step 1: Local consultations to characterize climate risks and EIA opportunities (already done for the 7 countries). To be performed during the first year of programme implementation.

Step 2.1 Assessment on territory and identification of key actors: A characterization of the territory and key actors was done. Variables assessed included: demographic, indigenous communities, main regulatory and legal frameworks, land use, etc.

Step 2.2 Analysis of main ecosystems, climate risks and perceived climate impacts: The consultation methodology included a participatory assessment of ecosystem services and benefits, climate risks, perceived impacts, among other, in a sex-disaggregated manner.

Step 2.3 Participatory identification of adaptation actions: Consultation participants proposed adaptation measures based on the information gathered during the previous steps (territory characterization, ecosystems).

Step 2.4 Perceived barriers and mitigation strategies during consultations: Participants identified main barriers they perceived could jeopardize the implementation of the proposed adaptation measures and the programme in general.

Step 3 Alignment with demand analysis from microfinance institutions: Financial institutions in the area were interviewed to identify their needs for financing climate change adaptation. Main areas identified during the consultation were compared and aligned with those identified by these institutions.

110. Step 3 Climate impact modelling (done for Costa Rica, Honduras and Dominican Republic): To be developed for the remaining countries during the first year of programme implementation and validated for the three years thereafter.

Step 3.1 Selection of a sub-basin for the modelling purpose: The Soil Water Assessment Tool (SWAT) is used for the hydrological impact assessment. A sub-basin was selected based on the following criteria: the prioritized areas are characterized through land use cover, digital elevation model and soil characterization. Based on these data, the SWAT model was calibrated to simulate the water balance of the basin.

Step 3.2 Identification of the main climate risks: The scenarios considered for modelling climate change impacts include sea level rise, temperature increase, precipitation changes and extreme events. The hydrological variables included in the model are soil moisture, evapotranspiration, runoff, etc. The climatic variables used to estimate climate change impacts include soil water, percolation, water yield and erosion.

Step 3.3 Impact models for main EIA interventions: A modelling scenario is generated to analyse the potential effect of each intervention on the hydrological system. The impact analysis was performed for all EIA interventions included those prioritized through the local consultations and a matrix was generated for the evaluation of impacts. The results from this analysis were used in the development of the site intervention strategy.

Step 3.4 Conclusions of the climate impact models: Results from the hydrological model were discussed through the analysis of scenarios developed in the hydrological variables for the Climate change RCP 4.5 scenarios with and without EIA interventions.


112 Step 4 Site intervention strategy (the defined for Honduras and Costa Rica): To be developed in consultation with key actors for all remaining countries during the first year of programme implementation).

The site intervention strategy will include the following components:

Definition of the expected results: The expected results are based on community consultations and the climate impact modelling, the problem statement can be defined identifying the main climate risks and barriers for adaptation to climate change.

Intervention objective: The specific objective of the programme is defined with a clear identification of the expected results in terms of EIA interventions, enabling conditions and objective population.

## Implementation arrangements

The intervention logic describes the components that will shape the programme, their contribution and their interconnection toward the main objective.

### Grant-financed EBA interventions to address prioritised climate risks in the Temaqui basin (Output 1.2)

A number of EBA interventions have been identified as key building blocks for addressing climate risk in the Temaqui basin at different levels of government, including national and sub-national level.

This section describes what ESA interventions have been prioritized for the specific climate impacts.

### Grant-financed EBA interventions to address prioritised climate risks in the Temaqui basin (Output 1.2)

The GCF facility will then finance a series of ESA intervention, supporting business and distribution models through a competitive bidding process. The selected projects will be implemented by the public sector at national or sub-national level, and this facility is also expected to leverage demand for EBA loans.

### Loan and guarantee facility

Access to finance for other network EBA interventions for the Temaqui basin (Outputs 1.3-1.5) will be provided through a loan and/or guarantee facility. This facility will enable access to financing at small-scale, medium-scale and large-scale investments in farm-, enterprise- and household-level. A number of criteria for the selection of EBA projects under this facility are described below.

### Building capacities of key actors (Output 1 and 2)

To ensure sustainability and as a complementary activity of the GCF grant-financed interventions, training will be organized at the local and national level to meet the needs of targeted stakeholders.

Maintaining EBA into key policy and planning frameworks (outputs 1 and 3). Technical assistance will be provided to strengthen the institutional capacity of the public sector to ensure that entry points of the integrated EBA in policy and regulatory frameworks at the local, national and regional levels.

### Monitoring and evaluation

Monitoring and evaluation is an integral part of the M&E framework and a proposed set of indicators are included in the intervention plan to support the communities in the establishment of an M&E framework during project implementation. The monitoring and evaluation will be carried out by the GCF Secretariat.

## B.4. Implementation arrangements

### 113. Validation of Site-specific EBA plans

A final step prior validation of site plans has been added for the pre-defined site plans in Costa Rica and Honduras where the initial phase of implementation has focused on strengthening the hydrological model and validating and confirming the specific sites for the grant interventions and the refinement of the eligibility criteria for the grant and loan component in consultations with communities, experts and the financial institutions.

### Accredited Entity

As the AE, CABEI will oversee programme implementation, using its experience successfully implementing projects in the region. The AE is responsible for ensuring that the project complies with all relevant laws and regulations in the countries where it operates. CABEI's support to the region has resulted in project approvals amounting to more than US$30 billion and approved loans of over US$15 billion. It will be responsible for the overall management of the programme, and will be accountable for the use of GCF resources under the programme. It will maintain programme accounts, facilitate disbursement of funds from the GCF trust fund, and ensure that all project activities are carried out as co-finance. Financial transactions will be subject to annual audits undertaken by internationally-accredited auditors in accordance with international standards (e.g., IFRS, IAS, GAAP) and/or equivalent national standards. ii) supervise, oversee and manage the implementation of programme interventions; iii) report on progress against approved project plans and budgets; iv) ensure that all activities are aligned to national priorities. To ensure adequate oversight of the programme, CABEI will use its robust fiduciary, technical oversight and risk management systems (including the Financial Risk Management System (FRMS) and its Financing for the Majorities Unit (FMU)) to closely support the Executive Entity (EE) and the Regional Project Steering Committee (RPSC). The AE will also take part in close coordination with a Regional Project Steering Committee (RPSC).

118. The Food and agriculture organization of the United Nations in collaboration with the Central American Commission on Environment and Development (CCAD) will serve as one of the Executive Entities for the programme through a Subsidiary agreement signed with CABEI. FAO will oversee the execution of the programme at the regional level, including the development of the project document, monitoring and supervising operations of the programme through National Project Execution Units (PEUs) based in each target catchment area, as well as providing technical assistance to CCAD. CCAD is responsible for coordinating the implementation and manage relations with stakeholders and institutions in the respective participating countries. CCAD will develop a regional strategy for the programme, including the identification of priority areas, the development and dissemination of information to all countries in the region to strengthen the scientific information of the decision-making process.

117. The CCAD is the environmental division of the Central American Integration Secretariat (SICA) and has independent legal personality exercised by the Pro Tempore Presidency; the highest authority in the CCAD are the SICA Council, which consists of representatives from each Member State. For administrative purposes, all contracts and operational agreements signed by CCAD are not binding on the SICA Council or any other body of the SICA. The CCAD is responsible for: a) providing SICA access to information, performance data and results; b) and CCAD follows up SIGCA's regulations on procurement and financial management. The CCAD's role includes: i) coordinating the implementation of actions at the regional level; ii) developing action areas; and iii) promoting a participatory, democratic and decentralized environment for decision-making in the region. The CCAD is responsible for ensuring that the programme is implemented through institutional presence and its direct links to each participating country. A detailed capacity assessment undertaken by the CCAD is available at annex 21. The CCAD is the coordinating body for Central America in terms of environmental matters, with a mandate to promote sustainable development and the conservation of natural resources within a wide range of donors, including the GEF, the European Union, the World Bank and the Interamerican Development Bank. They also have a role as the regional focal point for the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) and the organisation for the environmental ministers in the region, including all countries participating in the programme.

116. The EES will be accountable to CABEI as the AE for the effective execution of programme interventions. FAO, through the RRMU, will be responsible for ensuring technical and operational coherence at the regional level and will provide support to CCAD in the development of the project document. The RRMU will follow operating policies and procedures will follow CABEI's standards of operation. The EES will work in close coordination with CABEI and CCAD (described below).

Track record of Executing Entities

119. The FAO is an international organisation with the goal of eradicating food insecurity and malnutrition; ensuring sustainable management, conservation and utilisation of natural resources. The FAO supports countries in both mitigating and adapting to climate change through a range of programmes and projects based on the Mission Statement and Strategic Frameworks. The FAO has established a presence at country level with offices in seven cities of the regional programme and SICA region. The FAO maintains a close relationship with the Missionary Secretariat, which is responsible for coordinating the activities of the FAO in the region. Strategic Frameworks set with countries. Technical assistance is provided to Executive Secretaries of both councils of the SICA region. The FAO has also established a presence at the regional level through its representation as GCF as an International Organisation for the category of medium projects (up to US$250 million). It currently has a post in San Jose, Costa Rica and is represented by a representative in Panama City. The FAO also has a dedicated FAO's Office for Mesoamerica; in addition, the FAO has made important efforts from countries to facilitate access to technical assistance through its regional office in Santiago, Chile. The FAO works with governments at national level, and locally, with experience with local governments, farmers and women’s organisations, and using a multicultural approach.

120. The FAO promotes climate-resilient agriculture as a way to increase productivity, build the resilience of smallholders, and, whenever possible, reduce GHG emissions. Regionally-relevant examples of this work include:

* GEF F448 (US$35 million) for the rehabilitation of vulnerable smallholder farmers in the Mayan landscapes and the City Rurales de Quetzaltenango to build their capacity through training and technical assistance groups (US$167.7 million).
* GEF F429 (US$10 million) climate resilience measures in the dry corridor agroecosystems of El Salvador (RECLIMA). This programme seeks to restore and reforest degraded ecosystems to protect water sources and

# Regional Project Management Committee

The RPSC will complete representatives from inter alia i) FAO and CCAD as the EEA (i CABEI as the AE; ii) UNDP as the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP); iii) a technical advisory role, iv) national CCAD liaison; and v) the Regional Project Manager. The RPSC will primarily be responsible for providing technical guidance to the RPU in the areas of: i) programme design and implementation; ii) review and validation annual regional programmes and work plans; iii) review and validation of project proposals; iv) monitoring, evaluation and reporting on the progress of projects under the programme's targets, activities or timelines. In addition, the RPSC will be informed about the final success criteria for each project and the methodology used to determine the final success criteria.

The RPSC will also make a recommendation on the final selection of recipients of grants through the GCF. The RPSC will also provide advice on the development of the regional programme framework to ensure consistent programme progress across all seven countries. Biannual RPSC meetings will be held to review and validate annual regional programmes and work plans, as well as to provide strategic guidance. The RPSC will also be responsible for overseeing the RPU in accordance with the GCF rules and guidelines.

EEs will have the final decision or a veto right over any recommendation or action taken by the RPSC.


## Regional Project Management Unit

127. The RPMU, which is based at the RPSC's regional headquarters in Panama and will be responsible for day-to-day programme administration and execution and the regional technical coordination of programme activities, will consist of a Programme Manager (PM) who will be supported by a team of carefully designed administrative resources that meet CABEI and GCF requirements. A full-time staff member with experience in project management and financial control will be appointed as PM and is responsible for the daily on-the-ground implementation and management of the programme. The PM will be responsible for ensuring that all projects are implemented according to the approved work plan, within the required standards of CABEI and the GCF and within the specified time and budget allocations. To achieve these objectives, the PM will: i) ensure that all project activities are carried out in accordance with the programme in accordance with the specified workplan and allocated budget; ii) ensure that all programme management tasks are completed as per the approved work plan and budget; iii) liaise with national institutions and authorities to ensure that the programme is managed effectively and that the needs of all beneficiaries are met; iv) provide regular progress reports on project implementation to CABEI and GCF, and transfer relevant programmatic partners. The PM will be required to fulfil these roles for the duration of the programme.

128. In addition to the PM, the RPMU will be comprised of the following individuals related to the regional management of the programme:

* Financial and Procurement Officer;
* Administrative Officer; and
* Accountant.


## Regional Technical Specialization on the RPMU

129. Regional technical specialization on the RPMU will include a i) Chief Technical Advisor, ii) Forestry Expert, and iii) Water Resources Expert (where applicable). There will be a National Environmental and Social Safeguards Officer, a National Gender Officer, a National Monitoring and Evaluation Officer, and a National Knowledge Hub Coordinator to be based in the CCAD Headquarters in Panama. A Regional Knowledge Hub Coordinator role will be based in the CCAD Headquarters in Panama to ensure full integration of programme results into the Regional Environmental Observatory.


## National Advisory Committees

130. Each NAC is established in each of the seven participating countries. Each NAC will be comprised of a i) NAC National Coordinator; ii) national climate change officer/department representative, iii) Ministry of Environment representative, iv) representative from the national forestry authority, v) representative from the national water resources authority and vi) representative from Local Government. The RPMU will strongly advise governments to nominate local representatives who are able to provide technical advice on the implementation of projects in their respective countries. The NACs will be responsible for providing guidance on the development of national programme guidance, planning and oversight, as well as supervision of national financial execution.

The NACs will also participate in fostering national access to the three financial facilities established by the GCF with ad hoc meetings held whenever necessary.

## National Project Execution Units (PEUs)

### 133. National Project Execution Units (PEUs) will be established in the target catchments of each of the participating countries. Each unit will be comprised of: a) National Project Execution Unit Coordinator (technical lead); b) National Project Implementation Team (NPI Team), which may include: i) financial institutions; ii) an Administrative Officer; iii) a Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Specialist, v) a Communications Specialist; vi) a Gender Specialist; and vii) a Climate Change Adaptation Specialist. The PEUs will be responsible for providing evidence-based case studies of the adaptation activities for the regional knowledge hub. PEUs will be responsible for daily sensitization and capacity building activities with participating local governments, financial institutions, civil society organizations, farmers and communities on implementing climate-resilient projects. PEUs will also provide technical assistance to local governments, farmers and communities on implementing Emission Reductions from Agriculture-based Lignocellulosic biomass (ERAB) projects in order to ensure that they are able to access the carbon-subsidized bioenergy support EAU, to learn how to use the financial mechanisms established under the programme, and for the development of a business plan. PEUs will also provide technical assistance on project design and implementation financing through the Grant Facility. They will engage with local stakeholders at community, association, cooperative or other levels in order to identify and prioritize adaptation activities that are relevant to them and subsequently make national selections and pass their recommendations to the RFME. The M&E Specialists will be responsible for providing technical support to PEUs on the development of a monitoring framework at national levels to track and measure the targets and indicators defined in the programme's results framework.

## Intermediary Financial Institutions

### 134. IFIs will be the main counterparts to channel GCF funds and C&BE Co-financing from the Lending Facility towards beneficiaries. The IFIs will receive the funding for the Lending Facility from C&BE, which will then provide the IFI with a loan or equity investment in order to enable it to directly recourse loan for GCF funds; both funding streams will be blended at IFL level and then on-loan to Partner Financial Institutions (PFI). The IFIs will be responsible for identifying and selecting PFI as a partner for further leveraging of EAU via the intended Guaranteed Facility (see paragraphs 53 and 54).

## Partner Financial Institutions

### 135. PFIs in each country will be identified through a process following C&BE's and Intermediary Financial Institution's policies and regulations, and through eligibility criteria that C&BE will approve with a no objection. As the sole Executing Entity, C&BE will have final decision on the selection of each PFI. PFIs and end beneficiaries must comply with all applicable laws and regulations in their respective countries. In case there were a case where there is no C&BE-accredited institution within the target catchment area, accredited institutions from other areas may be considered as long as they are able to provide the same level of service as an accredited institution that can directly lend to the end beneficiary. The PFI will work closely with the PEUs to ensure that the projects and programmes supported by the PFI under the Lending Facility are consistent with the programme, are well-aligned with programme objectives. The functioning of the financial facilities and all associated activities must be in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the agreement between the PFI and C&BE, as well as any other applicable laws and regulations. PFIs will also be required to submit analyses, audits of reversibility, sustainability, and cost-effectiveness of all credit lines, thereby ensuring they are consistent with the programme's objectives.

### 136. PFIs will be selected on the basis of the following criteria (to refined and confirmed by C&BE during inception of the project):

- **Financial strength:** The PFI must have a minimum Tier I capital ratio of 8%.
- **Experience in lending to smallholder farmers:** PFIs must be duly licensed in the respective countries and have at least three years of operational experience.
- **Capacity for independent external audits:** PFIs will be required to submit annual financial statements by local authorities as certified by independent external auditors on an annual basis and by quarter reports provided to C&BE.
- **In case the initial eligibility of the PFI fails to satisfy a date that their year-end audits have been already completed, the PFI must provide a letter in an acceptable format confirming its compliance with applicable laws and regulations issued by the authorities.

## FINANCIAL FACILITIES

* **PFIs:** The board of directors and managers must be considered "fit and proper" by IFs. It must have qualified and experienced management, adequate organization and institutional capacity for its specific risk profile.
* **The PFIs shall not exceed the maximum leverage ratio (i.e. credit, operational, liquidity and market risks)**
* **Operational and financial performance of each specific PF must be over 105% for the preceding year.**
* **The PFIs have adequate liquidity (based on the acceptable prudential ratios dictated by the national financial regulatory body of each country).**
* **The PFIs shall not exceed their approved credit risk profile (based on the acceptable prudential ratios dictated by the national financial regulatory body of each country).**
* **The PFIs must hold a diversified portfolio quality, with PAIR<50 of at most 5% of total gross loans.**
* **The PFIs must have adequate internal audits and controls for its specific risk profile.**
* **The PFIs must ensure that the financial resources are allocated to each project in it separately and individually track all loans made with the ESA funds facility.**
* **The PFIs must not invest in projects of commercial farms, households, agroindustry and other enterprises.**
* **The PFIs agree to undergo intensive mandatory technical assistance Where PFIs are not complying with the above defined minimum requirements, these can adhere to the TA facility to establish compliance.**

### 136.

The objective of financial facilities is to enable financing—through both loans and grants—of proven (EaS solutions and resource- and water-efficient technologies for vulnerable communities in the Dry Comfort Zone)—and to ensure their sustainability.

Further information regarding the operation of the financial facilities is presented in Annex 23 Operational Guidelines.

## As observed:

- CABEI will act as trustee for GCF funds for the overall Programme.
- For the Lending facility (CABEI) will:
    - channel GCF funds and provided limited recourse loans to the selected IFIs, each 1 per country and
    - on-lend its co-financing funds to these same IFIs. The IFIs will each select up to two PIFs per country.
- CABEI will use a simplified format for the PIF selection process:
    - a first eligibility check based on a simplified form; and
    - a final selection and lending volume decision based on a full due diligence process.
- The PIFs will be selected by NACs in consultation with their respective national beneficiaries. National beneficiaries can apply via EMA loans directly with the respective PIFs.
- The PIFs will be selected through a two-stage process:
    - an initial selection of projects proposed by each NAC and subject to minimum requirements; the remainder will be passed to the NACO.
    - NACOs will select from among those projects that are eligible for funding in their country according to national priorities, and will pass their selection to the RSPUI.
- The RSPUI will:
    - make a preliminary selection of all projects proposed by each NAC and will pass their selection to the PIFs.
    - The PIFs will make a preliminary selection of eligible projects at the regional level and will issue the list of grant recipients and final approval will be taken by CABEI.
- For PIF non-applications, beneficiaries can apply for loans directly through PIFs.

### 140
Although countries in the region are classified from lower-middle to high income based on GDP per capita, there is a significant variation within each country and across the region. Countries having been prioritised for funding under CCA. For example, less than 3% of the national budget has been committed to climate change adaptation and disaster risk management related to climate change in Guatemala, El Salvador, Nicaragua and Honduras in recent years.

### 141
Across the LAC region, there is a high dependence on non-reimbursable international support to meet CCA costs. This is due to the fact that many countries have not been able to access sufficient amounts of international debt or debt loans in order to respond to climate change impacts. There is a considerable challenge for countries to finance adaptation and mitigation activities through their own resources.

For example, a UNDP study [1] in 2011 estimated an increase in the annual cost of adaptation in Costa Rica for the period 2030-2059 of US$48 million. In El Salvador, the total cost of adaptation is estimated at US$67 million. In the Dominican Republic, annual adaptation costs for the tourism sector are predicted to increase from US16 million to US$20 million by 2050.

### 4.2 Given this constrained fiscal space, there are limited opportunities to implement climate change adaptation interventions in the region without additional assistance.
The seven target countries have already exceeded their borrowing limits under the current framework and will continue to do so unless they can reduce the climate problem. While scope has been identified for partial loan financing at the regional level, the extent of external support required is significant (estimated at US$10 billion per year). This would be a substantial burden with significant risks), constrain their borrowing capacities with regards to global recommended borrowing limits. As a result, they will need to rely on additional financial resources from other sources, including those relying on alternative financing mechanisms. In addition, and at a local scale, the most vulnerable farmers are often constrained by limited access to credit and finance, which is further exacerbated in times of droughts unfavourably viewed by banks using conservative practices. This limits these individuals' access to financial instruments for EDA.

### 4.3 To overcome these constraints, the proposed program is requesting loan finance to establish a credit line for EDA in the region, with additional guarantees to de-risk investments and support uptake of EDA financing mechanisms. The proposed credit line will be established under the framework of the IDA facility. It is requested to establish and administer the credit line and to provide technical assistance for the implementation of EDA. This credit line would enable the countries in the region to increase their capacity for the scaled-up implementation of EDA in the most vulnerable communities as well as to support direct implementation of climate change adaptation interventions in the most vulnerable communities that are not in a position to access loan finance.

### B.6 Exit strategy and sustainability
The long-term sustainability and replicability of the proposed intervention will be ensured through six elements in the program design: namely, (i) operations and maintenance; (ii) building long-term community capacity for EDA implementation; (iii) ensuring that the financial instruments are sustainable over time; (iv) scaling up investments into EDA; (v) developing financial instruments for EDA; and vi) monitoring and evaluation. The details of each of these areas are described below.

#### 4.10 Post-programme operations and maintenance. Regular operations and maintenance (O&M) costs for programmes will be incurred after successful programme implementation as detailed in Annex 21: Operations Manual. Programme O&M costs include the continued undertaking of CDM not only during the programme, but also after project completion. For the purposes of this proposal, the post-programme O&M costs for the programme will be managed by the CCAD. The CCAD is responsible for its own O&M with the existing regional Environment and Climate Change Unit (ECCU) managed by the CCAD. Because the CDM component is linked directly to the CDR, it will not require physical maintenance post programme closure. However, the technical

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 55 OF 96

### Building long-term community ownership.

By engaging closely with target communities—particularly environmental and women's organisations—throughout the programme design and implementation phases, GCF will ensure that the interventions are tailored to their needs and priorities, thereby contributing to gender equality and the long-term sustainability of programme interventions after programme closure. This will be achieved through three key strategies: (i) establishing a training of trainers' programme for the implementation of EEA and WAT; (ii) affording women's organisations a leading role in the design and implementation of all GCF-supported projects, including women's groups, in raising and disseminating information products to support the adoption of EEa and WAT climate change adaptation measures at the community level; and (iii) ensuring that women's groups are involved in the development of all EEA and WAT interventions, as well as in Sub-Activity 1.3.3—establishing livelihood training projects in each municipality for farmers and rural communities to improve their access to information on climate change adaptation measures and sustainable livelihoods. Communities will further be closely involved in the co-development of all specific intervention plans to integrate EEA measures into the seven target catchments (Activity 1.1).

### Integrating EEA into local and national policies and regional strategies.

The programme will support the integration of EEA into local policies focusing mainly on land use management plans, water management plans, forest management plans, and other relevant sectoral plans. The programme will also provide national and local level decision-makers with relevant knowledge and information to adopt climate-resilient policies and incentives in the long-term. This will be achieved through three key strategies: (i) providing technical assistance to local actors is disseminated across the region to increase the capacity of institutions to implement EEA interventions; (ii) supporting the development of a regional policy framework for the integration of EEA into national and sub-national policies, as well as in Sub-Activity 1.3.3—establishing livelihood training projects in each municipality for farmers and rural communities to improve their access to information on climate change adaptation measures and sustainable livelihoods; (iii) supporting the development of a regional policy framework for the update and strengthening of the regional strategies for Environment, Climate Change, Water Resources and Agriculture as an input into the GCFA mandate.

### Incentivising private sector investments into EEA.

The programme will leverage private sector investment through capacity-building and awareness-raising interventions targeting the private sector. Activities under Output 3 will build the private sector's awareness of the potential benefits of EEA, while Outputs 4 and 5 will provide technical assistance to private sector actors in implementing EEA practices. For example, training commercial farmers on sustainable EEa practices (Sub-activity 1.2.2) will demonstrate the benefits of EEA for their businesses, which can then be replicated by other farmers in the region. This will also result in reforestation/conservation of ecosystems on which these businesses rely in the long-term. The financial sustainability of these interventions is further ensured through the creation of a framework for the valuation of ecosystem services, which will facilitate the identification and implementation of large-scale EEA investments and contribute to the long-term incentive of investing into EEA in the region.

### Developing financial instruments for EEA.

The provision of financial mechanisms under Output 4 of the programme will ensure that the benefits of EEA are captured by all stakeholders. The continuous monitoring of the benefits of these systems, will ultimately result in end beneficiaries who are implementing EEA and other adaptation measures displaying a lower risk profile than individuals who are not. ## 152. Capacity building will be provided through several programme activities. Activity A.3 will provide technical assistance to the capacity of these institutions to use the tools and methodologies developed by the consortium (partner financial institutions) to access and channel funds for small-and large-scale EBA investments. Under this activity, the programme will support the development of a data management system for the measurement, monitoring, evaluation, reporting and verification (MMEV) of GCF-funded projects. The programme will also promote and manage the use of ESA finance to end beneficiaries, such as data management for the measurement, monitoring, evaluation, reporting and verification (MMEV) of GCF-funded projects.

## 153. Phase I of the project will focus on capacity building in three key areas: gender equality, including the impacts of gender inequality, and the role of ESA in mitigating climate change impacts to support political and institutional readiness for scaling up investments in EBA (Sub-activity 3.1.4. U.S.$250K; US$147,000). This will be supported by building the capacity of these institutions to use the protocols and criteria developed under Sub-activity A.3.1.4. The programme will also support the development of a data management system for the measurement, monitoring, evaluation, reporting and verification (MMEV) of GCF-funded projects (Sub-activity 3.1.4 U.S.$250K). Finally, the technical capacity of community of practice institutions, including women's groups, and local development actors will be strengthened to support the adoption of ESA and other related best practices (Sub-activity 3.1.4 U.S.$147,000).

The programme will also contribute to the achievement of GCF's fund-level mitigation objective in achieving this objective, the programme will contribute to several indicative assessment factors in the GCF Performance Framework (PF) related to enhanced use of ESA to achieve the desired impact in the region. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 98 OF 96

## Adaptation Impacts

The proposed programme's contribution to climate-resilient sustainable development will directly benefit approximately **34,000** people in the Dominican Republic and indirectly benefit approximately **134,004** of these people who will also directly benefit from the implementation of the combination of EEA measures. The proposed programme will also indirectly benefit 1,368,044 people across the region by facilitating increased access to finance for reduced emissions and adaptation projects in other countries through the development of a market-based mechanism and building the capacity of governments to implement EEA. The financial mechanisms established under this programme will provide an opportunity for the government of the Dominican Republic to scale up its efforts in the region, introducing the potential for the restoration of **142,500 hectares** of degraded agricultural land through the implementation of a combination of EEA measures.

The proposed programme will actively: i) restore ~78,900 hectares of forested areas; ii) protect > 4,100 km² of wetlands and other natural ecosystems; iii) establish ~250 km² of protected coastal zones; iv) establish ~100 km of firebreaks, living barriers & superficial drainage solutions, respectively; and v) develop ~37,000 hectares of agricultural land with improved water management practices. The areas equipped with rainwater harvesting systems will include 156 community-level reservoirs that will be installed: 43 fruit gardens, 29 livestock communities, 80 schools, and 20 commercial livestock production/processing facilities.

**Page 62**

# D.2. Paradigm shift potential

161 The proposed programme will catalyse a paradigm shift in the regional approach to watershed and water development management in the Dry Corridor of Central America and the Azu Republic. This shift will see governments promoting large-scale EEA, and the private sector (including commercial banks) providing financial support for the implementation of these interventions. It will also provide the policy, institutional, and technical skills required to implement impactful adaptation interventions. The transformative effects of the programme are expected to be felt across the region.

162 The proposed EEA approach is inherently scalable, not only within the targeted catchments, but also across the entire Dry Corridor and Azu Republic. The programme has been specifically designed to create an enabling environment for investment in EEA and water-efficient technologies through three areas of support: financial; policy; and technical. The details of these three areas of support are provided below.

## Creation of an enabling environment for investment in EEA and water-efficient technologies

163 The proposed programme will diversify financing streams in the region to create an enabling environment for investment in EEA and water-efficient technologies. This will be achieved through several financial instruments with varying entry points to make climate change adaptation accessible to vulnerable communities, including smallholder farmers, indigenous peoples, and other marginalized groups. These include a) a credit line for promotion of local EEA and water-efficient technologies; b) non-conventional financing mechanisms such as CAFES (Climate Adaptation Financial Enhancement Scheme); c) a facility for scaling up investment in EEA investments from micro-enterprise to household-level; and d) a guarantee facility for smallholder farmers. The enabling environment will be further enhanced by building the technical and institutional capacity of PFIs and non-regulated financial institutions, as well as by re-financing EEA and water-efficient technologies through all three of the proposed financial instruments.

## Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

164 The enhanced local evidence-based case for EEA will be made through the knowledge hub and its monitoring system. This will support national and sub-national decision-makers in implementing policies and incentives for EEA, primarily through improved valuation of natural capital. Decision-makers will be supported by a regional knowledge hub that will provide technical assistance to governments and policies to facilitate the mainstreaming of EEA — including making provision for incentives programmes targeting smallholder farmers and indigenous peoples. The results from these efforts will be included in all programme components. By providing technical support to local governments for the adoption and implementation of national policies, the programme will contribute to the integration of climate adaptation into local-level development planning. This will include the development of catchment-level land use plans that are consistent with sustainable natural resource management practices beyond the scope of the programme's on-the-ground interventions.

## Technical support for the enhancement of regional knowledge and learning

165 The proposed programme will provide technical assistance to enhance knowledge sharing and learning. This support will include the establishment of a regional knowledge hub through which balanced knowledge products will be disseminated to decision-makers, stakeholders, and other actors in the region. The knowledge hub will catalyse a shift in how decision-makers approach land-use and development planning. In addition, the social, economic, and environmental benefits of EEA investments will be better understood by decision-makers as they are measured through ongoing monitoring and evaluation. These findings will be disseminated to policymakers and other stakeholders at national and sub-national levels. The programme will also provide training on the existing financing mechanisms and economic incentives. The programme will also build awareness of the benefits, risks, and associated financing mechanisms with private investors, as well as local and national governments.

## Upscaling of adaptation

166 The programme will catalyse the autonomous upscaling of EEA interventions and water-efficient technologies through policy instruments (e.g., economic incentives such as payments for environmental services) with accessible financial mechanisms (e.g., commercial loans, microfinance and guarantees funds). These efforts will be supported by a regional knowledge hub that will provide technical assistance to climate finance. Furthermore, the programme will promote the development of climate-resilient natural resources.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 81 OF 96

## D.3 Sustainable development

The proposed programme will contribute to the achievement of nine of the 17 UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The main contributions of the programme will be to: SDG 5 – Clean water and sanitation; SDG 6 – Water and sanitation; SDG 9 – Industry, innovation and infrastructure; SDG 10 – Reduced inequalities. The expected positive environmental, economic, social and gender impacts of the programme include: improved access to clean water and sanitation services; increased land use efficiency; sharing, the creation of an enabling environment for private sector investors, and policy improvements in the programmes’ target areas.

### Environmental co-benefits

The proposed programme will contribute to the conservation and restoration of ~119,200 hectares of degraded and climate-vulnerable ecosystems — through reforestation, forest management, agroforestry systems and sustainable fuelwood production. The combination of these climate-smart, resource-efficient technologies will result in several environmental co-benefits. These co-benefits are listed below:

* **EBA interventions:** including the conservation and/or restoration of forests and agroforestry systems, will act as natural carbon sinks and provide ecosystem services such as soil protection, water retention, and habitat for ecosystems in selected sites.
* **Conservation of degraded ecosystems:** will provide habitats for a range of terrestrial species, thereby improving biodiversity on programme sites.
* **EBA interventions:** will enhance soil fertility through accumulation of organic matter at revegetated areas, as well as enhancing carbon storage capacity and reducing soil erosion at programme sites.
* **Conserved and restored areas further enhance water infiltration and soil moisture retention.**


---
**Page 64**
---

# Social-co-benefits

185 The proposed programme is expected to yield substantial social co-benefits, mostly contributing to social development by improving water and food security of vulnerable communities in the region. These co-benefits are listed below:

- Through the adoption of water-efficient technologies such as rainwater harvesting systems, households will have increased access to clean water for domestic use as well as for food production. This will also improve the health status of women and children.
- Increased productivity of agricultural systems as a result of improved water supply will lead to enhanced food security in the region. The programme will contribute to the diversification of crops and increase the quality, quantity and diversity of food products through the adoption of agroforestry methods.
- This will also contribute to the reduction of poverty by increasing income from agriculture and the promotion of sustained fuelwood practices will enhance the sustainability of local livelihoods, contributing to cultural preservation.

The programme will reduce inequality and poverty through increased financial inclusion and improved economic opportunities for women. The programme will provide training in water-efficient technologies as well as transfer facilities for EIA and water-efficient technologies, as well as with the promotion of natural resource-based enterprises. By adopting a gender-sensitive approach, the programme will specifically empower women and contribute to social cohesion.

# Economic-co-benefits

170 Improved access to water and the promotion of EIA have several economic co-benefits for vulnerable communities in the Central America Dry Corridor and the Dominican Republic’s Azores. These are listed below:

- The implementation of water-efficient technologies—such as drip irrigation and rainwater harvesting—in rural areas will increase agricultural productivity, leading to increased income from agriculture.
- Short-term work opportunities will be created for the implementation of ecosystem restoration and protection activities in the region. This will also contribute to the maintenance of sustainable livelihoods through operations and maintenance of ecological infrastructure established by the programme.
- The programme will provide training on water-efficient technologies as well as transfer facilities for EIA and alternative livelihoods in ecotourism and in the collection of non-timber forest products, taking climate change considerations into account. This will contribute to the diversification of income sources from businesses.
- Increased access to credit will further reduce financial barriers to implementing adaptation measures for the longer-term sustainability of programmes.

The implementation of restriction activities and establishment of agroforestry systems under the programme will yield an average net present value (NPV) of US$15.8 million worth of benefits which accrues to local communities over a 50-year period.

# Gender-sensitive development impact

171 The programme seeks to hold traditional, unpaid gender roles associated with domestic responsibilities like motherhood, child- and elder-care as well as household management. These roles generally require women to spend more time on water-related activities such as fetching water from distant sources, the burden of filtering, collecting and storing water increases, with women bearing most of this additional responsibility in rural areas. The programme will provide training for women on water-efficient technologies in a sensitive manner. The deep understanding of water-related challenges for women in the region (described further under gender analysis) is critical to the long-term sustainability of programme interventions. By building on this understanding of water-related challenges, the programme will not only empower women within their communities and promote gender equality, but will also ensure the effectiveness of programmes interventions.

# D.A. Needs of recipient

## Vulnerability

### The Dry Corridor region of Central America and the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic are extremely vulnerable to the impacts of climate change (see Sections B.1 and B.5). A large proportion of people in these dry areas depend on agriculture for their livelihoods. Rising temperatures, shifting rainfall patterns and the increased intensity of droughts are predicted to have a significant impact on agricultural productivity, including: (i) reduced yields and quality of harvests of important crops; (ii) shifting suitability of land area for important crops; (iii) increased frequency of wildfires in areas where fire is not naturally occurring; (iv) increased vulnerability of livestock to heat stress. These impacts will result, inter alia, increased poverty, food insecurity and migration from rural areas to urban centres.

### Women are particularly vulnerable as they are responsible for the majority of agricultural production, collection, food security and unpaid family care. Through the proposed programme, the vulnerability of rural communities in these regions can be reduced by improving their access to climate information services, water management, as well as increasing rural community access to financial resources to fund adaptation interventions. The programme will also provide training on climate change adaptation strategies, as well as training, financial mechanisms and knowledge projects that will increase their resilience to climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 64 OF 96

## Economic and social development

The seven beneficiary countries fall—has achieved an average annual economic growth rate of 3.2%. This growth rate contributed to the region’s overall economic performance, but it was unevenly distributed across the countries. The seven beneficiary countries are classified as low middle to high income, with medium to high Human Development Index (HDI) scores. Some of these countries have experienced rapid urbanization and industrialization in recent years, while others remain economically insecure, with a notable wealth gap existing between urban and rural populations.

This economic insecurity is reflected in the vulnerability of households—including vulnerable rural communities—to climate change impacts. Many measure households—including vulnerable rural communities—are often slow to recover effectively from climatic shocks due to limited access to resources for adaptation interventions, lack of financial resources, and weak adaptive capacity. By improving access to financial resources for adaptation interventions, the adaptive capacity of rural communities can be strengthened, which will in turn improve their resilience against climate change.

## Absence of alternative sources of financing

The options available to donor countries are limited, and poverty eradication and sustainable development have been prioritized for funding over CCA. There is a high dependency on non-reimbursable grants from international organizations and multilateral agencies. The seven beneficiary countries are currently unable to meet their adaptation needs without additional financial resources.

### Added burden of the COVID-19 pandemic

The added burden of the COVID-19 pandemic on the economies of these countries has further exacerbated the situation, making it even more difficult for them to access funding from donor support. As a result, the countries have limited financial resources to implement large-scale EIA interventions at the landscape level; to implement small-scale water and resource-efficient technologies at community or

## Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

At the local level, vulnerable rural communities do not have the financial resources, knowledge base, or technical expertise to implement climate change adaptation interventions effectively. Furthermore, the frequent, cross-sectoral, multi-stakeholder engagement that is necessary for EEA and other adaptation interventions is often lacking in these communities. This lack of capacity can undermine the benefits of ecosystem goods and services produced by restored climate-resilient ecosystems. The proposed programme will provide technical assistance to build the institutional and implementation capacity of rural support structures that will enable rural communities to adapt effectively to climate change in the Dry Corridor.

### Country-specific allocation of GCF funds

The country-specific allocation takes into account the socio-economic, development and environmental contexts of the different participating countries, their respective needs for climate finance. With regards to the financial mechanism (Component 2), the proposed programme will provide technical assistance to build the institutional and implementation capacity of rural support structures that will enable rural communities to adapt effectively to climate change in the Dry Corridor.

### D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Alignment with regional and national policies and strategies

The proposed programme is closely aligned with regional and national policies and strategies. Specifically, the programme is closely aligned with the following national policies and strategies of the Central American Integration System (SICA), including those listed below.

- The Regional Environmental Strategic Framework (2015–2020) provides a framework and objectives for the region’s environmental policy agenda in the areas of climate change adaptation, biodiversity conservation, air quality management, and water resource management.
- The National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) 2013–2024 (El Salvador) provides a framework for the implementation of national policies on climate change.
- The National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP) 2015–2025 (El Salvador) articulates priorities for the regional integration of conservation and biodiversity management efforts.
- The Regional Forest Management and Protection of Forest Ecosystems by promoting sustainable use, conservation, and restoration through integrated governance and thereby recognizing the multifunctional nature of these forests.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 86 OF 96

## ANNEX I: STRATEGIES FOR SUSTAINABLE AGRICULTURE AND FOOD SECURITY IN THE CARIBBEAN

* The Agro-environmental and Health Strategy of Central America (2009–2024) aims to promote sustainable, integrated management of agricultural ecosystems, with a focus on sustainable land management practices.
* The Food and Nutrition Security Policy for Central America and the Dominican Republic (2012–2032) promotes regional food security through improved access to nutritious foods.

## THE REGIONAL STRATEGY FOR SUSTAINABLE AND CLIMATE-ADAPTED AGRICULTURE FOR THE SICA REGION (2017)** sets out priorities for climate change adaptation, sustainable livelihoods and the development of sustainable, low-carbon agricultural landscapes.
* The Caribbean Climate Change Strategy (2015–2030) promotes climate resilience through community-based adaptation strategies, involving communities, government institutions and CSOs to promote innovative and inclusive rural development.

## THE STRATEGY AND PLAN FOR INTEGRATED WATER RESOURCE MANAGEMENT (WIRM) IN CENTRAL AMERICA (2010)** provides a framework for the sustainable management of water resources.
* In addition to this regional alignment, the program contributes to achieving national priorities and aligning with national strategies. The specific objectives and activities of each country are described in Section 8 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

## Capacity of the Accredited and Executing Entities to Deliver

### 183. The Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI) will be the Accredited Entity (AE) for this programme. CABEI has supported economic and social development in the region since its establishment in 1950, with a focus on infrastructure projects. It is currently implementing several large-scale projects that are being carried out extensively in all the target countries and are acting as the AE for the GCF project "Productive Investment for Sustainable Development in Central America" (PISSDCA). CABEI has also been recognized since 2015 as serving as the Executive Agency on the original CAMBio® project funded by GEF, which aimed to assist Mesoamerican countries contributing to sustainable development and the protection of biodiversity through their products and services.

#### 183.1 Through a network of intermediate financial and microfinance institutions, CABEI has achieved a comprehensive regional footprint, including having a significant presence in rural areas that will be targeted by this programme. The organization has extensive experience in working with governments at all levels and has strong relationships with local and national decision-makers. The extensive experience and proven track record of the AE will ensure the success of the programme and contribute to its successful implementation.

### 184. The programme will be co-executed by three entities: CABEI, FAO, and GCF. FAO has been working for more than a decade in Central America, focusing on agriculture and rural sectors, building partnerships with governments at all levels, and supporting the development of national strategies to address climate change. As the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and the Green Climate Fund (GCF) with a view to fostering climate-related investments in developing countries, FAO has been working closely with GEF and GCF to mobilize, through its funding proposals, large flows of climate finance for climate change mitigation and adaptation projects. In addition, as part of the Global Environment Facility’s (GEF) Small Grants Programme Support Programme, As GEF portfolio is growing exponentially, the Organization is prepared to assist countries in accessing GEF resources by supporting them with their GEF grant applications. FAO has also been successful in obtaining GCF grants for small and medium-sized projects (up to USD 250 million) with low and medium levels of environmental risk, which are aligned with the priorities of the GCF. The Central American Commission for Environment and Development (CCAD). This entity has implemented several initiatives related to climate change adaptation and mitigation in the region, including the GEF, the European Union, USAID, as well as through bilateral agreements with individual countries. In addition, it has been involved in the development of the Regional Climate Change Strategy and the Regional Environmental Strategy for Central America. The Council of Ministers, which acts as the highest authority within the CCAD, is responsible for approving the CCAD’s work plan and ensuring alignment with the national priorities and visions of each of the participating countries.

#### Stakeholder Engagement

##### 183.2 The development of this programme was initiated by the Council of Ministers of the CCAD, which included a series of meetings to define the strategic direction of the initiative. Initial consultations with government and national stakeholder consultations have been conducted throughout programme development. Initial consultations were held in October 2017 to obtain input on the draft Concept Note for the programme. Focus areas for the programme were identified. The draft Concept Note was circulated, and further consultations were held in November 2017 to refine the programme concept and incorporate lessons learned from previous initiatives in the region.

##### 184. Further consultations took place at the International Central American Dry Corridor Symposium in October 2018 and in each of the target countries from December 2018 to March 2019. These consultations

### D.5 Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Cost-effectiveness and efficiency of programme interventions

The programme is expected to achieve multi-stakeholder levels in order to prepare for EBA-manifesting and upscaling by combining forces between public and private actors in selected areas. What is more, the programme will be able to reach a total number of 135,049 direct beneficiaries (output 1.2) and 847 indirect beneficiaries (output 1.3), via the strengthening of stakeholders' capacities to support underserved farmers with access to EBA finance. The programme is expected to mobilize US$610.2 million in public and private resources, generating a total impact of US$950.8 million (output 1.4). An estimated number of 134,694 direct beneficiaries and 85,644 indirect beneficiaries (total of 218,238) are expected to access loans to promote EBA in their regions[1]. The senior loans that make up the debt finance will be used for the implementation of the programme. In addition, the programme is expected to engage in EBA-finance, for which there exist no or only limited offerings as of today. While the grant solutions provided by the programme are not intended to replace existing financial instruments, the proposed solutions will be managed fully self-sustained on a commercial basis, targeting potential further investment into the programme. The programme is expected to generate an average net present value (NPV) for all components, as well as direct (financial) and indirect (economic) benefits resulting from their implementation – see Table 2.1. The total NPV of the programme is estimated at US$ 2,055,909,949 (both NPV discounted at an average of issued public bond rates or alternative investments).

#### Application of best practices

This programme draws on best practices from ground-breaking projects in the region and further afield. The project was selected as a Model Experience for Ecosystem-Based Adaptation (MEGA) project which informed the selection of appropriate EBA solutions for the proposed programme. Phase one of the project (MEGA I) was implemented in Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, Bolivia, Paraguay, Brazil, and Argentina; phase two (MEGA II) in Mexico, Costa Rica, Republic Senegal and Benin during phase two (MEGA II). EBA solutions drawn from MEA will be supported by measures to improve the resilience of agricultural systems through the development of early warning systems and half-orange kilns, for adapting to the increasing impacts of climate change, which were selected through a participatory process with stakeholders. The lessons learned in terms of how to support smallholder farmers' access to financial resources for adaptation from the MEA project as well as the productive use of financial instruments will be applied to the design and implementation of the programme. These lessons were applied to the design of activities under Component 2, in particular, to ensure the sustainable use of financial instruments and their application to support smallholder farmers' access to finance for adaptation into climate change adaptation in the seven participating countries in the Central American Dry Corridor and Andean Mountain Range. The lessons learned from the MEA project will be applied to the design of these lessons include appropriate mechanisms for ensuring that all programme activities are implemented in a participatory manner, as well as the identification of key stakeholders and their roles and responsibilities relevant to the programme are presented in Section 10 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.

- increasing commercial and smallholder farmers' capacity and access to finance for implementing GEA solutions and promoting the uptake of water- and resource-efficient technologies to respond to the impacts of climate change.
- effectively managing the available financial and human resources to ensure the efficient and cost-effective implementation of adaptation interventions.
- building an effective evidence base drawing on relevant past and ongoing initiatives to inform the implementation of new projects, including the identification of opportunities for the uptake of new financial mechanisms and resources for adaptation and support capacity-building interventions.
- establishing a robust monitoring and evaluation framework that will allow for continuous learning and ensure that the most effective solutions are being implemented and that an adaptive management approach is being applied in response to changing conditions.
- engaging and collaborating extensively with all relevant programme stakeholders within each of the seven participating countries will encourage buy-in from national and local-level decision-makers, thereby contributing to the sustainability of proposed adaptation interventions over the long term.

| **Activity 1.1: Developing site-specific intervention plans for the 5 target countries** | A community-based approach will be adopted to identify site-specific interventions and develop a monitoring and evaluation framework. The process will be capacitated in each municipality to ensure that it can continue beyond the project phase. | * Establish a community-level monitoring and evaluation framework.\
* Develop a capacity building plan for the municipalities to monitor and evaluate their own projects.\
* Engage local communities, stakeholders, and other relevant actors in the catchment areas as well as to assess vulnerability, risks and opportunities.\
* Organize workshops by these committees with support from regional experts to develop site-specific intervention plans.\
* Facilitate the selection of an appropriate land for the installation of solar panels.\
* Ensure that all stakeholders are engaged during the project implementation phase. | * 36 monitoring and evaluation committees established in each municipality.\
* Site-specific intervention plans developed. |
| **Activity 1.2: Developing site-specific intervention plans for the 5 target countries** | The suite of identified E4A interventions will focus on conservation and restoration of natural resources, sustainable agriculture and forestry, and energy with sustainable fuelwood production. | * Establish a stakeholder engagement workshop to identify key issues and get stakeholder buy-in for the development of site-specific targeted catchments.\

## Activity 1.2 Provide technical assistance to national authorities, farmers and rural communities in the implementation of EIA and water-efficient technologies as well as water and resource-efficient technologies

### 1.2.1 Develop cooperation agreements with commercial farmers for the adoption of water-efficient technologies and provide support services to the farmers on sustainable EIA practices, conservation and restoration of biodiversity, and the use of existing agroforestry systems; a) Organize training workshops for efficient technologies; and c) Provide technical assistance to reduce fuelwood demand. To facilitate this, cooperation agreements will be developed with commercial farmers in each target country to build their capacity to adopt these technologies.

### 1.2.2 Establish a training of trainers workshop for representatives of local development committees, women's groups, and local cooperatives within each target country on EIA and water-efficient technologies. The trainees will be selected from a pool of local departments of environment, CDRs, and other relevant institutions. Cooperation agreements with 3 EIA implementation protocols will also be established.

### 1.2.3 Organize training workshops for representatives of local development committees, women's groups, and local cooperatives within each target country on EIA and water-efficient technologies. The trainees will be selected from a pool of local departments of environment, CDRs, and other relevant institutions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 80 OF 96

## Activity 3.2: Raise awareness of benefits for the implementation of CCA innovations

### Outcomes:
- Establishing several enabling mechanisms to ensure access to communities and municipalities to climate change adaptation practices.
- To ensure the effective uptake of climate change adaptation practices, training will be provided at three levels: (1) municipal level; (2) municipality as an entity; and (3) individual off-farmers. These trainings will be trained through the EEA credit line and will include discussions to knowledge to be shared with medium-scale private sector actors who are interested in taking out loans through the EEA credit line.
- Through Outcome 2, targeted efforts will be made to reach the most vulnerable communities and groups within them. This will include targeting women's groups.

## Technical guidelines for the assessment of ecosystem services
### 3.12 Training workshops
#### 3.12.1 Training workshops through the knowledge hub:
- Facilitate access to information on landscape management standards and practices.
- Provide training on how to access financing and support for landscape empowerment as well as business opportunities, including business opportunities in the context of ecosystem services and their linkages with human well-being.

#### 3.12.2 Training workshops at the municipal level on climate change adaptation:
- Discuss the impacts of gender equality and the role of EEA in mitigating climate change.
- Provide training for decision-making for climate change adaptation.
- Comber and Arzua communities will be trained as champions in developing and disseminating information products to other municipalities in the EEA.

#### 3.12.3 Training workshops at the municipal level:
- Facilitate access to information on landscape management standards and practices.
- Provide training for private organisations through the EEA credit line.
- Discuss the impacts of gender equality and the role of EEA in mitigating climate change.
- Involve at least one municipality per municipality.

#### 3.12.4 Training workshops targeting commercial farmers:
- Facilitate access to information on landscape management standards and practices.

## Activity 3.1: Enhance capacity of national and local public policy makers to mainstream climate change adaptation - particularly in water resources management practices in particular – it is essential that they have the knowledge, skills, tools and effective awareness of sustainable development and climate change adaptation at the national level and translate this into local policies.

### 3.3.1 Build an evidence base using both two-country comparative case studies and a comprehensive ecological-economic cost-benefit analysis to clearly demonstrate the benefits of integrated water resources management for economic growth and poverty reduction, as well as the implementation of economic instruments such as water funds, payments for environmental services, etc., in the context of climate change.

### 3.3.2 Develop or adjust a national policy framework that develops access to public goods and services through an integrated multi-development approach.

### 3.3.3 Develop guidelines for local governments on how to implement economic instruments such as water funds, payments for environmental services, etc., in the context of climate change.

### 3.3.4 Provide technical assistance to policy makers, including developing protocols and criteria for the design and implementation of economic instruments (e.g., water funds) that are sensitive to environmental sensitivities.

### 3.3.5 Develop guidelines targeting municipalities on how to implement economic instruments such as water funds, payments for environmental services, etc., in the context of climate change.

### Activity 4.1: Set up and establish national lending and guarantee facilities

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

189. The Project Manager (PM) will be responsible for the overall monitoring of programme progress against the outcome-based indicators during implementation, as well reporting programme progress to the Regional Programme Support Function (RPSF) and the Regional Programme Management Unit (RPMU). The Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) Specialist, supported by seven National M&E Specialists (one per beneficiary country), will primarily be responsible for developing a monitoring framework and an evaluation framework to track the programme's progress towards achieving its targets. The Regional M&E Specialist will provide technical guidance on the development of these frameworks and will also monitor the implementation of the programme to meet the mid-term and final targets defined in the Logical Framework (Section E.3 of this Funding Proposal). The National M&E Specialists will be responsible for monitoring the implementation of the programme in meeting the targets and the application of gender-disaggregated indicators, in reporting on the PM's progress against these targets, and in providing technical guidance to the PM and beneficiary countries at each beneficiary country level. Given the scale of this programme, national M&E Specialists will be procured in each beneficiary country. The National M&E Specialist will provide technical guidance to the PM and beneficiary interventions at a national level.

190. In addition, the Regional M&E Specialist will be responsible for: i) following guidelines provided by the RPSF and RPMU on how to conduct monitoring visits to the PM and beneficiary countries; ii) measuring progress against targets as set out in the Logical Framework document by the end of the implementation phase; iii) measuring progress against targets at least once a year during the implementation phase, with the aim of meeting the targets and/or achieving results aligned to the M&E indicators defined for each programme component; iv) ensuring that all interventions under the programme comply with the Programme's ESFM process and procedures; v) providing mid-term and final performance reports (PMs and FPs); vi) participating in the production of annual gender-disaggregated indicators; and vii) participating in the production of reports to be sent to the RPSF and RPMU. The National M&E Specialists will be supported by a Local Consultant (GC), who will be employed on a short-term basis to oversee and monitor the application of gender-disaggregated indicators.

195. The Regional M&E Specialist will perform regular visits —at least once a year—to each country regionally, in order to monitor the progress made by the PM and beneficiary countries. They will use these visits to cross-check the information provided by PIs and beneficiaries in programme evaluations and other monitoring reports. These visits will also allow the M&E Specialist to learn about the outcomes and difficulties experienced during implementation through focus group discussions with different beneficiaries, as well as from interviews with the PM and other key stakeholders. The Regional M&E Specialist, supported by the National M&E Specialists, will compile six-monthly programme reports to be submitted to the RPSF and RPMU on progress made in each beneficiary country regionally, and will also feedback and advisory support from the RPSF and RPMU in the case of potential programme issues. The mid-term and final performance reports (PMs and FPs) as well as annual gender-disaggregated reports and annual general reports will also be reported to be sent to the GCF Secretariat with information regarding the progress made by each country regionally.

191. CABEI, as the Accredited Entity (AE), will perform supervision missions from year two of programme implementation onwards, selecting three countries each year to visit and assess the status of programme implementation in terms of alignment with the Logical Framework, achievement of targets, and compliance with policies and procedures, as well as the alignment between implementation and programme objectives.

192. As per the Accreditation Monitoring Agreement (AMA), an independent consultant will be contracted by CABEI to conduct a Mid-Term Evaluation (MTE) and the Terminal Evaluation (TE) of the programme. Both evaluations will be conducted in accordance with the GCF evaluation framework, which includes qualitative and quantitative data analyses, including interviews with relevant stakeholders, data provided by the Regional and National M&E Specialists, and other information gathered during CABEI's supervision missions and participative learning. The PMT will provide an assessment of programme performance against its targets at each beneficiary country level as well as a review of the progress made in terms of alignment between what is on track, what problems and challenges the programmes are encountering, and what corrective actions are required to ensure that the programme continues to be implemented in a manner that ensures it remains efficient and sustainable way. The RPSF and RPMU will be informed of all MTE findings and will develop

---|---|---
