Category | Probability | Impact
---|---|---
Technical and operational | Low | Medium

Description

Natural disasters undermine the implementation of EIA interventions and result in economic loss and/or damage to the interventions, and i) restoration and sustainable management of forests, wetlands and agro-systems.

Mitigation Measure(s)

The programme's EIA and water/resource-efficiency assessments will target areas that have been identified as being vulnerable to climate change impacts. For example, EIA will be implemented in sites that are prone to drought as a result of climate change. The identification of these areas will also inform the design of appropriate solutions which will increase the resilience of these communities to droughts. Furthermore, the programme will integrate water and resource-efficiency assessments into its planning process so that environmental considerations are taken into consideration during the planning and implementation of EIA interventions. The RPMU will also oversee the integration of EIA measures into programmes planning and implementation.

Category | Probability | Impact
---|---|---
Technical and operational | Low | Medium

Social tensions may arise as existing power relations within the community might disrupt equal opportunities to use and benefit from water- and resource-efficiency technologies.

Mitigation Measure(s)

The programme's activities have been designed to be transparent and will maintain a broadly consultative approach throughout. The specific social risks associated with the proposed interventions will be identified through local stakeholder consultations. In addition, the activities have been designed to ensure that all individuals are afforded access to the technologies, which will be installed in public facilities — for example rainwater harvesting systems.

## GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS
### G.1 Environmental and social assessment
- **197:** The environmental and social risk associated with the programme was evaluated in accordance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS). According to the assessment, the programme is considered to be Category 2 or Medium Risk. This categorization is supported by an Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA) which has been prepared for this project. The ESA will ensure that the programme complies with GCF requirements by: i) defining the context and baseline of each target area in which the investment activities are planned, ii) identifying potential adverse environmental or social impacts associated with proposed interventions; and iii) providing a management plan to describe how any adverse impacts identified during the project implementation phase will be mitigated. The ESA also identifies the most significant primary environmental and social risks that may be associated with the programme – as identified by the assessment team.

- **198:** Overall, the environmental and social risks assessed to be presented under this programme are minor and can be mitigated and/or effectively managed with the implementation of an appropriate strategy. The programme will contribute positively to the achievement of GCF’s strategic objectives in several ways including: i) supporting economic security and environmental benefits by facilitating access to micro-finance and grant funding; ii) promoting financial inclusion for vulnerable groups, iii) improving water management through improved catchment management and the provision of water-efficient technologies and systems. However, several risks may be associated with this programme which could have adverse impacts on local communities or result in adverse environmental, social or economic impacts. These potential impacts are detailed below.

### Environmental Impacts

## Social impacts

### Potential for adverse social impacts under the programme has been assessed as more likely than environmental impacts.

#### i) non-recognition or alteration of Indigenous People's livelihood strategies, cultural practices, religious beliefs or governance structures;
#### ii) economic vulnerability and an increase in economic insecurity in the event that credit conditions are not suitable for small producers, or that interest rates are not commensurate with the return rate from IBA interventions;
#### iii) increased competition among Indigenous People for resources and services;
#### iv) unequal opportunities for Indigenous peoples and other marginalised groups to benefit from the programme; and
#### v) limitations of all stakeholder priorities given the large geographic extent, diverse stakeholder groups and range of contextual conditions.

### These impacts are all manageable or avoidable and can be effectively reduced and/or mitigated through implementation of appropriate management strategies. For further information please refer to Annex 6: ESMIF.


## Indigenous Peoples

### Potential adverse impacts on Indigenous Peoples are a critical concern for the GCF and although no unavoidable impacts on Indigenous Peoples have been identified in the risk screening, certain interventions may be required to ensure that the programme is inclusive of all Indigenous Peoples. The process will be open and include and it will consider the languages, dialects and cultural practices of all beneficiary groups through the use of culturally appropriate communication strategies. In addition, a grievance redress mechanism has been designed with an ecosystem-based approach that inherently respects Indigenous Peoples' rights to self-determination and their right to participate in decision-making processes affected. CABEI — as the AE — has experience in facilitating the inclusion of Indigenous Peoples in GCF-financed projects and will maintain oversight.

### Grievance Redress Mechanism

The established CABEI Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) will be implemented for this programme. The GRM is a multi-stakeholder mechanism that will be used at all stages of the programme and will provide an accessible, rapid, fair and effective response to concerns or complaints regarding any form of discrimination they may lack access to formal legal systems or support. For further information please refer to Annex 6: ESMIF.

G.2 Gender assessment and action plan

During programme preparation, a gender assessment was conducted to understand the differential vulnerability of women and men to climate change on women and men in the Dry Corridor and Azonos of the seven participating countries. From the findings of this assessment, and an analysis of the proposed programme activities, a gender action plan has been developed. The gender assessment included a desktop review of available literature, as well as stakeholder and beneficiary consultations held in Guatemala, El Salvador, Honduras, Nicaragua, Costa Rica, Panama, and Mexico. This document notes that gender-disaggregated data is limited, particularly regarding indicators and information on land tenure, poverty, and access to resources.

Traditional gender roles and stereotypes in the programme area, as well as the resulting asymmetrical power differences they produce, create a culture of social exclusion that leaves women vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. These include: i) women’s lower levels of education, literacy, and numeracy; ii) gender norms and stereotypes create inequalities in social relations and cultural institutions, where men maintain production roles and women are relegated to domestic roles; iii) women have less access to information on natural resources and land; iv) greater exposure to violence; v) limited participation in decision-making including in the management of natural resources and land use; vi) lack of access to credit and other financial services; vii) undermining their agency, capabilities and safety. This system limits the realisation of women’s and girls’ rights, which is exacerbated by climate change impacts, particularly where gender inequalities are most pronounced and baseline vulnerability (for all genders) is highest.

The profile of migration from the region has changed in the past decade, with women increasingly becoming active agents in the migration process, rather than passive actors relative to the husbands or male family members who traditionally migrate. This can be attributed to a number of factors including: i) the same gender conditions that shape their vulnerability at origin. This can manifest in isolation, lack of social networks, poor access to education and health services; ii) harsh climatic conditions, unsafe living environments, and exposure to violence, exploitation and even enslavement.

Although potentially more relevant to migration activities, the assessment also notes that female environmental migrants are often left behind at origin when men migrate. This can be attributed to: i) hydroelectric, monocultures and/or oil; for visibility, stigmatisation and criminalisation. These conditions, where women are excluded from decision-making processes, will continue to exacerbate their vulnerability and continued participation and representation in activist spaces, which may result in unfavourable biases and even further inequality.

Regarding climate change, consultations conducted for the assessment described the effects of climate change on women as: i) increased water stress; ii) increased frequency and intensity of heat waves; iii) distribution of work increases; iv) negative impacts on agriculture and fishing for smallholder families, particularly for landless women who are more vulnerable to food insecurity; v) reduced access to productive assets such as access to resources like water, food, fuel, non-timber forest products (including herbs and medicinal plants), and livestock; vi) increased vulnerability of women in the informal sector due to climate change generating work such as agriculture or handcrafts (as a result of reduced resources); vii) reduced access to clean drinking water and sanitation services; viii) negative impacts on women’s health, particularly for those collecting water; ix) negative impacts on women’s largely-marginalised physical and mental health due to climate stress, hunger, lack of access to healthcare and increased domestic violence; x) increased displacement and mass.

The assessment describes how the conditions the vulnerability described above will influence programme implementation, particularly noting how social exclusion and barriers experienced by women from participating meaningfully in decision-making processes and accessing the benefits of the programme, such as training and economic opportunities. The gender action plan outlines several interventions to address these issues during implementation and subsequently.


---

---

### G.3 Financial management and procurement

CABEI, through its current structure, including its Representation Offices in each country of the Programme, the Office of Compliance, the Office of Environmental and Social Safeguards, the Office of Subventions and Grants, the Credit Division, the Environment and Social Sustainability Unit, and the Finance Division, will be responsible for the implementation of the Credit Corridor Programme. ## G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

This section sets out the General Disclosure Policy (GDP) that provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the GDP, project and programme documentation must be made available on the GCF website unless otherwise agreed with the Board subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the GDP. Information provided in confidence is one of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the disclosure of a small part of it would cause significant risk of prejudice or harm.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information:

- [ ] No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- [ ] Confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that parts of the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity will provide two copies of this document to the GCF, one copy for internal use and one copy with all confidential markings redacted for full internal use within the GCF that where the confidential markings are marked accordingly together with an explanation of their meaning and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's disclosure policy; and
- [ ] Redaction of information on the GCF website: The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

# ANNEXES

## H.1. Mandatory annexes

- **Annex 1**: NDA no-objection letter(s) (template provided)
- **Annex 2**: Feasibility study – and a market study, if applicable
- **Annex 3**: Economic and/or financial analyses in spreadsheet format
- **Annex 4**: Detailed budget plan (template provided)
- **Annex 5**: Implementation timeline including key project/programme milestones (template provided)
- **Annex 6**: E&S document corresponding to the EAS category (A, B or C; or II, I2 or III):
    - Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) or
    - Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) or
    - Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP)
- **Others** (please specify – e.g., Resettlement Action Plan, Resettlement Policy Framework, Indigenous People’s Plan, Land Acquisition Plan, etc.)
- **Annex 7**: Summary of consultations and stakeholder engagement plan
- **Annex 8**: Gender assessment and project/programme-level action plan (template provided)
- **Annex 9**: Legal due diligence (regulatory, taxation and insurance)
- **Annex 10**: Environmental baseline data (template provided)
- **Annex 11**: Monitoring and evaluation plan (template provided)
- **Annex 12**: AE fee request (template provided)
- **Annex 13**: Co-financing commitment letter, if applicable (template provided)
- **Annex 14**: Term sheet including a detailed disbursement schedule and, if applicable, repayment schedule

## H.2. Other annexes as applicable

- **Annex 15**: Evidence of internal approval (template provided)
- **Annex 16**: Map(s) indicating the location of proposed interventions
- **Annex 17**: Project implementation plan (template provided)
- **Annex 18**: Appraisal, due diligence or evaluation report for proposals based on up-scaling or replicating a pilot project
- **Annex 19**: Procedures for controlling procurement by third parties or executing entities undertaking projects financed by the entity
- **Annex 20**: Level AML/CFT risk assessment
- **Annex 21**: Operational manual (Operations and maintenance)
- **Annex 22**: Description of ESIA, water-and resource-efficient technologies eligible for finance
- **Annex 23**: Operational guidelines for financial mechanisms
- **Annex 24**: Forestry and agricultural sectors baseline
- **Annex 25**: Project monitoring plan
- **Annex 26**: Description of selected municipalities

**Re: Propuesta de Financiamiento para el GCF y del Banco Centroamericano de Integración Económica (BCIE) sobre Adaptación basada en ecosistemas para aumentar la resiliencia climática en el Corredor Seco Centroamericano y las Zonas Áridas de la República Dominicana.**

Estimado Señor, Gobernador,

Nos referimos a la propuesta titulada: Adaptación basada en ecosistemas para aumentar la resiliencia climática en el Corredor Seco Centroamericano y las Zonas Áridas de la República Dominicana en Panamá como se incluye en la propuesta de financiamiento que nos envió el Banco Centroamericano de Integración Económica (BCIE) del 27 de noviembre de 2019.

El abajo firmante es el representante debidamente autorizado del Ministerio del Ambiente, Micolás Concepción, la Autoridad Nacional Designada / Punto Focal de Panamá.

De conformidad con la decisión BBR / 10 del GCF, cuyo contenido reconocemos haber revisado, por la presente comuniamos nuestra no objeción al programa Adaptación basada en ecosistemas para aumentar la resiliencia climática en el Corredor Seco Centroamericano y las Zonas Áridas de la República Dominicana como se incluye en la propuesta de financiamiento.

Al comunicarnos nuestra no objeción, se da a entender que:

(a) El gobierno de Panamá no tiene una objeción al programa Adaptación basada en ecosistemas para aumentar la resiliencia climática en el Corredor Seco Centroamericano y las Zonas Áridas de la República Dominicana como se incluye en la propuesta de financiamiento.

(b) El gobierno de Panamá no tiene una objeción al programa Adaptación basada en ecosistemas para aumentar la resiliencia climática en el Corredor Seco Centroamericano y las Zonas Áridas de la República Dominicana, como tal se incluye en la propuesta de financiamiento.

(c) De acuerdo con los subvenciones armonizadas y señales del GCF, el programa Adaptación basada en ecosistemas para aumentar la resiliencia climática en el Corredor Seco Centroamericano y las Zonas Áridas de la República Dominicana, como tal se incluye en la propuesta de financiamiento, cumple con los requisitos establecidos por las regulaciones.

También confirmamos que nuestro proceso nacional para determinar si no objeción al programa Adaptación basada en ecosistemas para aumentar la resiliencia climática en el Corredor Seco Centroamericano y las Zonas Áridas de la República Dominicana, como tal se incluye en la propuesta de financiamiento, se ha seguido debidamente.

También confirmamos que nuestra no objeción se aplica a todos los proyectos o actividades que se implementarán dentro del alcance del presente acuerdo.

Reconocemos que esta carta confirma la aceptación del julio en el sitio web del GCF.

Atentamente,

Micolás Concepción
Ministro de Ambiente
Autoridad Nacional Designada / Punto Focal de Panamá.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP174

## Proposal name: Ecosystem-based Adaptation to increase climate resilience in the Central American Dry Corridor and the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic

### Accredited entity:
Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

### Country/ies:
Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, Panama and Dominican Republic

### Programme/programme size: Large

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. **The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:**


| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The program design, featuring three different financing facilities (grant facility, loan grant facility and guarantor facility), will encourage participation of the private sector in Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) type of investments and diversify financial sources. This is a positive feature that can create an enabling environment for investment in EbA and water-energy-food nexus projects in Central America. | The implementation entities include several different entities and therefore result in higher transaction costs, longer project management costs. |
| The technical assistance facility is tailored to increase technical capacities of local governments, farmers and non-regulated financial institutions to mobilize resources for the implementation of ESA and water/energy-efficient technologies, which can enhance the economic and ecosystem benefits of these investments. | GCF and CABEI will be lending to different entities and therefore GCF will be taking higher risks. These issues will be further defined in the term sheet. |
| Target catchments were selected following a rigorous vulnerability assessment that included hydrological modeling, socio-economic household surveys and extensive consultations with key stakeholders. | The programme is heavy on grant financing for capacity-building and technical assistance, and therefore entity (GA) administration costs are higher. |

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Programme background

#### Background/history of the programme to inform assessment of the funding proposal

The Central American Dry Corridor has emerged in the last three decades to describe the increasing frequency and intensity of droughts in the region. With more than one million families in the Central America Dry Corridor living on less than $2 a day, and the majority of these being indigenous or Afro-Caribbean people, the impacts of increasingly severe climate change on agriculture for their livelihoods, the impacts of increasingly severe weather events over the past five years have resulted in the need for considerable increase in climate finance in the region. In 2015 alone, the Central America Dry Corridor experienced a 43% decrease in agricultural yields and losses across the region, with more than 2.5 million people experiencing food insecurity. From 2015 to 2016, the number of people facing acute food insecurity increased by over 70%, with women and rural communities the most likely to suffer from chronic hunger and malnutrition.

By the end of 2016, the region was estimated to be in need of $3.4 billion to address a total of 8.4 million urgently needing food assistance. These impacts are expected to continue to worsen under future scenarios of climate change.

#### Data measures such as forest conservation, reforestation, planting of agroforestry systems, improved irrigation systems and installation of vegetative barriers, result in substantial improvements in soil health and water retention, which in turn allow for more sustainable land use activities take place. Furthermore, EBA measures result in soil humidity improvements and benefit the local communities by increasing their food security.

The impacts on water production (surface water) and percolation (water recharge) show even better results when considering the implementation of a combination of EBA measures. The Dry Corridor and arid zones experience significant changes in rainfall patterns, with an increase in extreme weather events during the year. Lastly, the implementation of this combination of EBA measures also shows very positive impacts on soil health.

#### 5. The programme will address the following barriers: i) access to credit for adaptation interventions among vulnerable populations; ii) access to financial resources for the implementation of climate change adaptation projects in the Dry Corridor and arid zones sector; iii) knowledge and understanding of climate change impacts as well as appropriate adaptation measures within government institutions, civil society organizations, and communities; iv) integrated integration of EBA and other adaptation measures into sectorial policies; v) technical capacity within government institutions to implement climate change adaptation projects in the Dry Corridor and arid zones; vi) development of economic incentives for investment in sustainable natural resource management and EBA.

#### While implementing adaptation interventions, agricultural losses will continue to increase along with ecosystem loss, water shortages, poverty and food insecurity. These impacts are expected to worsen under future scenarios of climate change in the Dry Corridor and arid zones who are already extremely vulnerable to climate change.


Climate objective: The Central American Dry Corridor and the arid zones of the Dominican Republic, Haiti, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua and Venezuela will be more resilient to the impacts of climate change.

### Financing information and environmental and social safeguards (ESSG) category A: 

A combination of grant and loan finance, a guarantee, and senior bank provided co-finance will be used to set up the EDA facility. The GCF will provide USD 30 million as a grant facility to support bottom-up selection and promotion of local EDA activities through non-reimbursable grants. This will be complemented by a USD 150 million loan facility for scale EBA investments at farm-, enterprise- and household-level (USD 102 million) and a USD guarantee facility for the PFI to mobilize more of their own resources, while also allowing them to access co-financing from other sources. The GCF will provide USD 30 million in order to promote EDA finance (USD 30 million). The facilities will be managed by CABIF with a blended financing structure and will be supported by the World Bank, IFC, IDB, and intermediary financial institutions (IFIs) to partner financial institutions (PFIs) towards end borrowers. PFIs will provide co-financing in the form of loans or guarantees, either with an limited recourse loan. By taking this additional risk compared to GCF which is lending to larger IFIs, GCF will lower the interest rate to the PFIs and ultimately the end borrowers, improving their competitiveness. The blended financing structure and a USD 30 million guarantee facility will encourage the PFIs and EBA to mobilize more of their own resources, while also allowing them to access co-financing from other sources. Total GCF financing is USD 84.2 million in the form of grants and USD 150 million in the form of loans. The blended financing structure will be provided in the form of USD and USD 42 million in the form of senior loan, thus bringing the co-financing ratio to 3:7. CABIF has submitted a proposal for the establishment of an EDA facility which has submitted relevant documentation (an environmental and social management framework) as part of the funding proposal package.

# 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

The programme will target seven catchments for programme interventions, one in each of the participating municipalities (see Annex 1: Map of the Republic), which will target one catchment area). The programme's regional approach will create an enabling environment for scalable and sustainable catchment and water demand management, facilitating large-scale investments. A total budget of US$84.6 million is requested to implement this component. Of that request of US$84.6 million will be used in the following ways: US$10.0 million will be used to strengthen the institutional framework for catchment and water demand management; US$72.5 million will be used to implement EBA and other adaptation measures through the activities comprised in output 1; ii) US$4.6 million will be used to support the development of a market-based approach, enabling local communities to access on-grant financing for the creation of a business ecosystem around EBA available technologies as per activity 1.2; iii) US$56.3 million will be used to generate and share information on best practices and lessons learned from the implementation of the component into local and national policies within the output 3; iv) US$4.6 million will be used to set up the financial architecture for EBA, including the development of a market-based approach, non-regulated financial institutions to access and channel funds through them throughout output 4; v) US$50.8 million will be used to support the implementation of the component (Independent evaluations and PMC costs); and vi) US$7.8 million is requested for contingency.

The total budget for this component is US$123.6 million, which includes US$9.8 million in senior loan provision of US$40 million and CARE US$42.8 million on finance together being offered to PFIs as blended finance mechanisms below market conditions for small- and large-scale EBA investments.

**Component 1: Mainstreaming of EBA, water-efficient technologies and natural resource-based business into selected catchments (final cost US$ 112 million; US$10.0 million, or 90 per cent)**

The component includes the mainstreaming of EBA and water- and energy-efficient technologies in the seven catchments through the development of a market-based approach for farmers and rural communities. A community-based approach will be adopted to identify site-specific needs and opportunities for the implementation of EBA and other adaptation measures this first year. This will be supported by the establishment of community-level monitoring and evaluation committees, which will promote county ownership and sustainability of programme interventions in the long term. The component will also support the development of a framework for the monitoring of biophysical, social and economic conditions in their local catchment areas, as well as its assessment against the agreed performance indicators.

Technical assistance will also be provided to farmers and rural communities in the 36 municipalities for the implementation of on-the-ground EBA practices, including irrigation, agroforestry, water harvesting, soil conservation, and other natural resource-based businesses (NRB), which will support the implementation of tree nurseries and the mainstreaming of EBA. The component will also provide technical assistance to reduce non-destructive use of ecosystem goods and services provided through EBA, such as businesses developed in the context of agroforestry or water harvesting.

The technical assistance under this component will also support the integration of EBA in policy and regulatory frameworks at the local level focusing mainly on water management regulations and water management community structures as well as land-use planning.


Technical assistance will not be limited to farmers accessing the financial mechanisms from output 1. It will be available for all those in the target municipalities that are open to adopting improved practices but lack the technical knowledge to do so.

15. A US $28 million grant facility will be established to support site-specific EbA and water/flood risk management interventions in targeted municipalities that are able to access loan finance. Based on approved eligibility criteria, a selected set of interventions will be financed through this facility. The facility will also provide technical assistance for community-based institutions including non-governmental/ municipal associations, farmers' cooperatives, and other local organizations. The types of investments supported by the facility will include community-level investments, the establishment of water funds, seed funding for payment for ecosystem services and support for the adoption of selected EbA practices and/or water risk management measures.

### Under Component 1a: A regional knowledge hub will also be established. The knowledge hub will provide a platform to share information on best practices in the implementation of tools developed by the programme as well as the best practices and lessons learned collected through the programme's monitoring and evaluation processes and previous and ongoing complementary initiatives. It will also provide technical assistance for community-based institutions opportunities for accessing finance provided through the programme, with specific focus on women’s economic empowerment. The hub will be used to disseminate information about: i) the findings from catchments; from programme advances, impacts and experiences learned for dissemination in other regions; ii) lessons learned from the pilot phase of the project.

Lastly, this component will support the preparation of a methodology for the valuation of ecosystem services and development of accounting profiles for natural capital for integration into development planning processes. The knowledge hub will also provide technical assistance to best practices and lessons learned from ongoing initiatives, including the: i) “inventory forestal” in Guanacaste; ii) water funds established in the city of San Jose, Costa Rica; iii) water funds of the city of Quin in Ecuador; and iv) water funds established in cities in Costa Rica, the Dominican Republic and El Salvador. The knowledge hub will also provide technical assistance to be used to promote the establishment of economic incentives for EbA investments such as water funds and payments for environmental services.

### The second component of this component includes large-scale EbA landscape interventions financing through grant support. Financing will be provided to the most vulnerable communities in targeted municipalities that are able to access loan finance or equipment to implement adaptation interventions and cannot receive loans through the loan or grant facilities. Such landscape interventions include: i) establishing one tree nursery per municipality for community-based reforestation; ii) establishing 12 ha of natural forest in key riparian areas across the seven catchment areas; iii) protecting 1,280 ha of natural forest in major riparian areas and other critical ecosystems across the seven catchment areas; iv) protection of 375 ha of natural forest areas across the seven catchments; v) restoring 92 ha of native pine forests in Guanacaste; vi) establishing 1,480 ha of agroforestry systems on smallholder farms using diversified living fence arrangements in basic grain crops; vii) establishing 612 ha of agroforestry systems on smallholder farms using diversified living fence arrangements in cash crops; viii) establishment of 35 km of firebreaks for forests and plantations; ix) establishing 1,280 ha of silvopasture systems using diversified living fence arrangements; x) establishing 612 ha of agroforestry systems on smallholder farms using diversified living fence arrangements in cash crops; xi) establishment of 35 km of firebreaks for forests and plantations; xii) establishing 119 km of living barriers for soil conservation around agricultural plots; and xiii) building 108 km of superficial drainage for

and establishing 109 rainwater harvesting systems, 156 community-level rainwater reservoirs, etc.

**Component 2: Financing and implementation for EBA as well as water- and energy-efficient technologies (total cost: USD 139 million; GCF cost: USD 96.8 million, or 70 per cent)**

Under this component, the EBA lending facility will be established and operationalized with the GCF senior loan provision of USD 60 million and the CARIUS USD 42.8 million co-finance offered by the Government of Germany. The facility will provide financing for small-scale and large-scale EBA investments at the farm-, enterprise- and household-level. By providing a blended finance structure, the facility will be able to leverage additional resources with a market rate senior debt as co-financing from CARI, incentives are created for financial intermediaries to provide loans to farmers, enterprises and households. The GCF will manage the EBA lending facility via the establishment of trusts for direct-on-lending* to local PFIs and/or other institutions that can fund EBA investments in a manner consistent with the GCF’s turn-to-turn on-end beneficiaries. Investments will be targeted towards community-level investment, rural micro, small and medium-enterprises or other institutions that can foster EBA adoption and implementation across different sectors.

An appraisal facility will be linked to the EBA lending facility to secure IFC’s co-financing of CARI’s co-financing funds as well as mobilize additional lending from IFC’s own resources and additional public and private investors’ resources by mitigating against credit risks associated with commercializing climate change adaptation technologies. The facility will leverage the recently established Guarantee Fund. The guarantee facility will cover end-beneficiaries which have no access to formal financial services, and provide a risk-sharing coverage for example via real estate or movable assets; the guarantee facility will cover the missing participants in the market. By providing guarantees on loans to farmers, enterprises, etc., it enables more end beneficiaries to access EBA finance as well as increases security of collateral provided by these borrowers. The facility will be linked with the EBA lending facility.

Lastly, to facilitate access to and operation of the facilities, these will be accompanied by a technical assistance facility that will develop capacity within municipalities and PFIs to monitor and document the respective economic and ecosystem benefits. Projects and verification systems are in place to manage the EBA finance. Such technical assistance will focus on developing capacity within municipalities and PFIs to monitor and assess the implementation of EBA investments, as well as provide a framework for how to monitor and document the respective economic and ecosystem benefits. Where possible, information technology tools such as remote sensing, geographic information systems, and satellite data insights into the EBA lending portfolio and climate impacts on the portfolio.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential

The proposed programme’s contribution to climate-resilient sustainable development will directly benefit 280,644 (50 per cent women) across seven catchments in Central America. While USD $84.3 million in grants are geared towards concrete and tangible benefits for an estimated total of 97,100 people, the programme also aims to build capacity among local communities to understand, develop and implement EBA solutions, a further USD $26.8 million in debt finance will build upon such created capacities, with an estimated number of 756,634 direct beneficiaries across the seven catchments. The programme will directly benefit 109,344 people who will also indirectly benefit 1,368,044 people across the region by facilitating increased access to finance for WBDA projects and increasing the capacity of governments to implement EBA.

The programme will reduce climate change vulnerability for farmers and producers organizing themselves into cooperatives, primarily water regulation. Impacts of climate change have been described in the proposal based on the existing information of regional climate models (RCP 4.5) and hydrological modelling results for the seven catchments in Costa Rica, Honduras and the Dominican Republic. The results of the hydrological modelling show that under a high-emission scenario (RCP 8.5), there will be an inevitable decrease of precipitation that leads to reduced water yield, reduced water infiltration, groundwater depletion and increased soil erosion. However, the programme aims to reduce climate impacts in the target locations and suggested adaptation interventions provide for positive impacts over these variables when applied over climate change scenarios showing the capacity of the proposed solutions to mitigate the negative effects of climate change.

Furthermore, the programme includes the development of site-specific intervention plans through activity 1.1 (sub-activity 1.1.3) for each of the seven catchment areas as a first step in the implementation of the programme. Two draft sample site-specific intervention plans were developed for Costa Rica and Honduras to illustrate how the proposed climate change adaptation plans was also clearly elaborated in the funding proposal. These site plans will be validated during the initial phase of the project, with further validation expected from both countries.

The allocation of grants will be based on the input and activities identified in the site-specific intervention plans, and will follow a clear set of principles set out in the funding proposal, which will ensure that activities to be funded contribute to a cohesive catchment approach targeting climate change adaptation. The programme will also provide for the provision of services they provide, as well as protecting, restoring or using biodiversity and ecosystems in a sustainable manner.

With the financial support under this programme it is estimated that the following will be the impact on the ecosystems and ecosystem services in the region: (i) ~142,500 ha of

3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

The proposed programme will catalyse a paradigm shift in the regional approach to catchment and water demand management in the Dry Corridor of Central America and the arid zones of Mexico and Guatemala. This will be achieved through increased private sector participation, including commercial farmers and enterprises, as well as improved access to finance for smallholder farmers. The programme will also provide the institutional and technical skills required to implement impactful adaptation interventions. The transformative effects of this paradigm shift will be achieved through several innovative approaches anchored into the programme design.

The programme has been specifically designed to create an enabling environment that capitalises on existing knowledge, experience, and capacity in the region at both policy and technical levels. The programme will diversify financial systems in the region to create an enabling environment for investment in climate change adaptation interventions. This will be based on the establishment of several financial instruments with varying entry points to make climate finance more accessible to vulnerable communities. The enabling environment for investment will be further strengthened by creating a conducive policy and regulatory framework that allows non-regulated financial institutions to finance air and water-efficient technologies through microfinance.

The enhanced local evidence base for EBA that will be created through the knowledge hub and the monitoring and evaluation of programme activities will support national and local-level decision-making processes. This will lead to improved climate resilience, increased ecosystem services, and improved valuation of natural capital.

Lastly, the programme will autonomise the mainstreaming of EBA interventions and water-efficient technologies by combining policy instruments (e.g., economic incentives such as payments for environmental services) with accessible financial mechanisms (e.g., commercial loans). This will create a conducive environment that allows smallholder farmers to access the financial landscape in the region to enable vulnerable groups to access climate finance. Furthermore, it will support the development of climate-resilient MFI and alternative livelihoods at the community level.

3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The programme will have significant sustainable development potential with environmental, social and economic benefits. Some of the environmental co-benefits include conserving biodiversity in the selected sites by creating a buffer zone around the project area to act as buffers, controlling and mitigating the impact of floods, droughts, extreme temperatures and wildfires on ecosystems in the selected sites. Conserved and restored ecosystems will provide habitat for wildlife and improve water quality.

Social co-benefits will mostly contribute to social development by improving the water and food security of vulnerable communities in the region. This includes improvement of the

### Needs of the recipient

Scale: Medium to high

1. In the last decade, the Latin America and Caribbean region—under which some beneficiary countries fall—has achieved an average annual economic growth rate of 3.2 per cent.
2. This economic increase is compounded by climate change impacts, including extreme rainfall events, droughts, and increased frequency of tropical storms.
3. Rural communities are often slow to recover effectively from climate impacts.
4. Rural communities have little access to financial resources for adaptation interventions.

### Country ownership

Scale: Medium to high

1. In CCA is recognized as a priority in the Central American region and has been integrated into national policies and strategies, including the Regional Strategy on Climate Change (2019), Regional Strategy for the Conservation and Sustainable Use of Biodiversity in Mesoamerica (2018), National Adaptation Plan (NAP) 2030, and Regional Agro-environmental and Health Strategy of Central America, among others.
2. In addition to this regional alignment, the programme contributes to achieving national priorities for climate change adaptation and resilience.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: Medium to high

1. The AE provided a comprehensive demand and supply analysis to justify the loan request as well as the programme’s financial models, which foresee direct financial and indirect economic benefits.
2. The demand and supply analysis includes details on the absorption capacity at the country level and establishes a comparison with the numbers of expected loans and financial entities through this process.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

##### a. Programme background
The programme aims to overcome the barriers to the adoption of sustainable resilient practices by increasing the capacity of people, including smallholder and commercial farmers, as well as entrepreneurs in the rural communities of the target areas. The programme aims to contribute to the conservation and restoration of natural resources through the implementation of appropriate land management measures. It also aims to unlock financing for the adoption of sustainable resilient practices. The environment will benefit from reduced soil erosion, improved water quality, and biodiversity. Improvements in soil fertility, water infiltration, and soil moisture retention are also expected to reduce evapotranspiration and enhance water quality and availability. The promotion of natural resource-based activities and alternative livelihoods, including the adoption of water-efficient


---
**Page 125**
---

technologies, have expected social benefits of increased access to clean water, reduced exposure to waterborne diseases and improved food security.

### Environmental and social risk category and safeguards instrument

The programme is classified as Category B or Medium Risk by the AI. The Secretariat confirms the environmental and social risks associated with the implementation of the programme are generally avoidable and can be mitigated effectively through the implementation of appropriate management measures. An Environmental, Social and Management Framework (ESMF) which contains the relevant legal and institutional frameworks, general environmental and social safeguards, a grievance redress mechanism (GRM), and provisions for stakeholder engagement and a grievance redress mechanism (GRM).

### Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards

Subparagraph (b)(i) of Article 10.2 of the GCF Agreement requires compliance with GCF ESS standards and requirements:

- **EES 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts** The ESMP has been prepared for the programme, which identifies the environmental and social impacts have been identified, assessed and ranked on their significance to the programme. The ESMP also contains a list of measures that can be used to avoid and manage the environmental and social impacts posed by the programme. The ESMP provides guidance on how to address the potential environmental and social impacts and risks during the implementation of the programme.

- **EES 2: Labour and working conditions** The programme will provide employment opportunities for local people in the project area, including women and other groups of workers involved in the implementation phase of the programme. The ESMP considers the following issues related to labour and working conditions: terms of employment; workers' organization; non-discrimination; equal opportunities; child labour; and forced labour of direct, contracted and third-party workers. The ESMP provides guidance on how to address these issues during the implementation stage and adds labour and working condition concerns of workers from the local population and of contractors.

- **EES 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention** Through promotion of the Ehh options and water-efficient technologies, the programme encourages sustainable resource use and efficient management of waste. The programme also includes measures ensuring sustainability of available water. Nevertheless, the programme may result in the use of hazardous substances during its implementation phase.

The programme aims to address these impacts with the dissemination of specific guidelines on the use of hazardous substances. The ESMP also contains a list of measures as part of an integrated pest management and agrichemicals guide for the programme including the establishment of an effective solid and liquid waste management module for the programme.

- **EES 4: Community health, safety and security** The inadequate handling of pesticides such as those used in agriculture can be harmful to their health and safety. However, as the ESMP indicates that an integrated pest management plan has been developed by the project team, it is expected that any community being exposed to these risks is low. The ESMP also makes proposals for mitigating public health risks from potential contamination of water collection systems. In case of a failure in the water supply system Plan there should be a need to develop one as assessed during programme execution.

- **EES 5: Land acquisition and voluntary resettlement** The programme does not envisage the acquisition of land in the project area for implementation. Nevertheless, the programme identifies the possibility for land use restrictions in implementing


---
**Page 126**
---

### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The programme will also lead to the implementation of new forest restoration, reforestation and adoption of sustainable EIA interventions. Nevertheless, the introduction of new mixed plantation of native species and agroforestry systems including the development of new land uses such as charcoal production may affect the ecosystem if improperly managed and implemented. For instance, mixed species plantations can be more susceptible to pests and diseases than monocultures. Uncontrolled sourcing of forest for charcoal may also exacerbate deforestation in some areas. To mitigate these unintended outcomes from increasing reforestation, training related to biodiversity conservation and sustainable management practices will be implemented. Technical support on sustainable practices such as the use of organic fertilizers and integrated pest management practices will be encouraged. Further, should significant risks and impacts relate to biodiversity conservation, the ESMF provides for a Biodiversity Management Plan that can be developed in consultation with relevant stakeholders.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous peoples

The programme will also lead to the implementation of new forest restoration, reforestation where the programme is to be implemented. The AE recognizes that there is a risk of non-recognition or alteration of indigenous people’s livelihood strategies, cultural practices and traditional land use systems as well as loss of access to resources for indigenous peoples and unequal opportunities for indigenous peoples. Where indigenous peoples are likely to be affected by the implementation of the programme it will require an Indigenous Peoples Plan (IPP) to be developed that will mitigate potential negative impacts on indigenous peoples’ livelihoods, cultural practices and traditional land use systems. The guidelines contained in the ESMF for these IPPs incorporate the process of continuous dialogue with indigenous peoples at all stages including the participation of indigenous peoples (including both men and women) in the project implementation and operation processes and emphasize that PFC will be used in all cases, even where the indigenous peoples are not directly involved. The AE will develop an appropriate strategy that clearly describes measures to enable indigenous peoples to take advantage of the programme’s benefits and to participate fully in its design, plan and manage the use of natural resources on which they depend. The GCF Independent Review Mechanism (IRM) will be available for assistance at all stages, including before a claim has been made, as required by parties involved.

### ESS 8: Cultural heritage

Some of the regions where the programme will be implemented may have cultural value and may be implemented in communities that have significant cultural heritage. The AE recognizes that the protection of tangible and intangible cultural heritage is adequately assessed and will not be adversely affected. The AE will develop an appropriate strategy to ensure that any adverse impacts selected by the programme including continued access to these sites and the use of cultural practices and religious beliefs during implementation.

### Gender policy

The All has submitted a gender assessment and gender action plan; therefore, it completes the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. The gender assessment was completed based on a desk review, primary data collection and stakeholder consultation at various levels including women and other vulnerable groups.

- **Gender equality**: Women are disproportionately affected by customary and statutory laws on marriage, divorce, inheritance, custody and land tenure have significant implications on access, use and control of resources. Women face discrimination in the implementation of customary and traditional beliefs, inadequate basic social services, low representation of women in politics and violence against women, among others, are some of the challenges affecting gender equality and women empowerment in the region. The Horn of Africa Corridor and small landlocked frontier countries play a key role in creating greater vulnerability and gaps in the realization of women’s rights and gender equality. Women in these countries face multiple forms of discrimination, especially continue to assume primary responsibility for unpaid family care and domestic work, and (limited) access to productive resources, education and health services. Women are mainly for the search for food, water and biomass for energy on a daily basis. About 60 per cent of the poor in Sub-Saharan Africa live in rural areas where women are disproportionately represented in the poorest of the poor. The most severe poverty is located in ECOWAS countries, particularly in West Africa, where women are concentrated in rural areas. The feminization of poverty is very significant and is exacerbated by the lack of access to services including health, education, water and transportation. This limits entrepreneurship opportunities for women. Women have limited access to land ownership and control on agriculture, livestock and fisheries. Women face discrimination in access to resources and control of assets. Women are also underrepresented as decision makers at all levels and membership in farmers’ cooperatives and local development organizations and their role in decisions making is very low. The number of women’s associations, women’s groups, women’s associations, irrigation organizations, neighbouring boards and water committees is limited and their presence in positions of social and political leadership is still clearly a minority.

**Page 128**

## Gender and Migration

Women and girls are migrating to escape the extreme poverty derived from drought, loss of crops and livestock, and above all, lack of access to food. Sex disaggregated data is limited, including information on land tenure, participation in decision-making, the level and characteristics of women's economic activity, their access to credit or other financial activities, among other important factors. There are few studies that address the causes of migration, including slavery and normalizes violence against women. In general, there are few institutions that offer protection for women who have been victims of trafficking, sexual exploitation, forced pregnancies of adolescent girls and forced early marriages and unions are also common in many areas. The Green Climate Fund is a sensitive issue with regard to gender equality, violence and the impacts of climate change.

### Gender Action Plan

The GBF has provided a gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirement of the fiduciary framework. The GBF gender action plan provides activities that address the challenges faced by women and includes baseline data, indicators, targets, timelines and budgets. The GBF gender action plan also includes a regional gender strategy and an annual regional gender expert to ensure gender issues are integrated throughout the project, including gender expertise in all phases of the project cycle. The GBF will also implement activities that address gender gaps in a number of areas. One focal area is designed to generate enabling conditions for women's economic empowerment by providing training on business management skills and women's cooperatives to help strengthen women's entrepreneurship initiatives at the local level and with CCA and mitigation actions. This is complemented by building the capacity and awareness of men and boys in relation to gender equality, including through the implementation of the gender approach and how to incorporate it into agricultural public policies and take specific measures for the empowerment of women. The GBF will also implement activities on environmental resilience and gender issues. Protocols will be developed to address the specific barriers that women face when accessing water resources. The GBF will develop specific measures to ensure effective involvement of women in water committees. The programme will also develop a strategy to promote the participation of women in agricultural cooperatives and women's community organizations and strengthen the technical capacities of women's organizations at the local level. Measures will be taken to ensure the active participation of women in all phases of project implementation, including through the provision of training and empowerment for women producers that ensure the inclusion of their priorities into decision-making processes. The GBF will also implement activities on gender equality within community and producer organisations related to the programme. To complement these actions, the GBF will conduct awareness raising campaigns among men and boys and facilitate discussions among men about positive masculinities. Finally, the programme will maintain gender and social inclusion indicators in all phases of project implementation.

### Gender Equality

Since GBF has been identified as a critical challenge, the programme is strongly encouraged to address this issue through its gender action plan. The GBF will develop project intervention and develop measures to ensure that women have equal access to the grievance mechanisms that will provide all beneficiaries with a clear and safe process for reporting incidents of gender-based violence or other forms of discrimination in the project environment (including international conventions ratified, legal policy and institutional support). The GBF will also implement activities designed to promote and strengthen the commitment to the pursuit of gender equality of the countries involved in this programme.

### Gender Action Plan Activities

In general, the gender action plan includes measures designed to address barriers to gender equality. These include: increasing women's participation in decision-making; reducing gender gaps and support progress towards a more gender-equal society.

### 4.3 Risks (This section of the assessment has been redacted and full assessment shared with the Board on limited distribution basis)

#### 4.4 Fiduciary

**a.** The EEs in the project are FAO, the Central-American Commission for Environment and Development (CCAD) and CABEI, which will also perform the role of EE in the project.

- As an EE, CABEI will be responsible for overseeing the implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting and closure of the project. As an EE, CABEI will be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are carried out under component 1, with FAO as EE for the technical assistance facility supported by UNEP as a technical advisor.

- The programme will have a Regional Project Steering Committee (RPSC) comprising of representatives from FAO, CCAD, CABEI and UNEP and the regional project manager (PM). The RPSC will be responsible for overseeing the implementation of the project at the regional level, which will include: i) overseeing project implementation; ii) review and validation of progress reports on project results; iii) ensuring that the project's fulfillment of environmental and social safeguards is met; and iv) approval of changes to the project's targets, activities or timelines. In addition, the RPSC will be informed about the final selection of beneficiaries for each component and the final disbursement of funds across countries, as well as make a recommendation on the final selection of recipients of grants through the grant facility. The RPSC will also be responsible for overseeing the Regional Project Management Unit (RPMU).

- The RPMU will be based in FAO's regional headquarters in Panama and will be responsible for the day-to-day project administration and execution and the regional technical coordination of the project. The PM will be responsible for ensuring that all activities are carried out under clear workplans, terms of reference and carefully designed administrative arrangements that meet CABEI and GCF requirements. The PM will also be responsible for overseeing the implementation and be responsible for the daily on-the-ground implementation and management of the project. The PM will ensure that the project is implemented in accordance with the GCF Strategic Framework to the required standards of CABEI and GCF and within the specified time and budget allocations. The PM will: i) report directly to the RPSC on project management-related matters; ii) ensure that all project activities are carried out according to the approved budget; iii) ensure that all project interventions are implemented according to CABEI and GCF guidelines, including environmental and social safeguards as well as other relevant laws and authorities to ensure that the project is managed effectively and that the needs of all beneficiary groups are met; iv) provide support for the efficient and effective information and knowledge transfer to relevant project partners.

### 4.7 List of proposals conditions (including legal)

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

(a) Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the Fund, confirming that the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals needed by it and has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed programme;

(b) Signature of the Board approval letter by the President of the Fund and delivery to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval, or the date the Accredited Entity has obtained all final internal approvals, whichever is later; and

(c) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP174

## Proposal name: Ecosystem-based Adaptation to increase climate resilience in the Central American Dry Corridor and the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic

### Accredited entity: Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

#### Country/ies:
- Costa Rica
- El Salvador
- Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua,
- Panama and the Dominican Republic

#### Programme/programme size: Large

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Earlier versions of this funding proposal were submitted for review at the twenty-eighth and twelfth meetings of the Board of Directors and at the review and endorsement meeting of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP). In its assessments, the independent TAP raised various concerns about the proposal.

### Completion of a catchment-specific climate rationale for each of the seven catchments where the Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EBA) demonstration interventions and grant facilities are to be implemented. The TAP also recommended that CABEI use these data to validate the choice of model that is used to make future projections about key climate parameters.

### Clear articulation of the adaptation impact of the project as per the GCF investment criteria indicators; and

### One-to-one relationship between the grant portion of the project, to be implemented in the seven target catchments of the participating countries, and the loan and guarantee portion of the project, to be implemented across the entire Dry Corridor of the region.

The core concern raised by the independent TAP in its previous assessments was that the funding proposal did not provide sufficient information on the availability of grant-based EBA interventions proposed. The current funding proposal provides some additional information around the availability of climate data and proposes an ex-ante validation process for interventions to be implemented in the seven target catchments.

The validation process, if implemented robustly, could ensure that proposed EBA interventions are supported by evidence-based needs of the catchments. However, this is not a TAP-preferred option as made clear in its previous assessments and it would only be disbursed for interventions that are supported by the evidence-based needs of the catchments. For that reason, an endorsement here must be conditional on greater clarity regarding the availability of grant-based EBA interventions.

### Funding proposal overview

1.1 Funding proposal overview


---
**Page 134**
---

1. The term "Dry Corridor" describes arid zones in parts of Central America and the Caribbean (Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, Panama and Dominican Republic). The Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI), the accredited entity (AE) under the Green Climate Fund (GCF), is proposing a funding proposal to address the impacts of climate change on agriculture in these countries. The proposal focuses on agricultural droughts and heatwaves, as well as an increase in frequency and severity of extreme weather events such as hurricanes, tropical storms, and heavy rainfall leading to flash floods (in turn causing reductions in aquifer recharge) - because of climate change. The funding report proposes that more than one million families in the Dry Corridor are reliant on agriculture for their livelihoods and are particularly vulnerable to the impacts of agricultural droughts. In 2014 alone, intense drought resulted in an estimated USD 465 million loss of crops and livestock, with a further USD 378 million in losses due to heatwaves affecting crop yields. From 2015 to 2018 food insecurity has increased annually in the region. By the last quarter of 2018, more than one million people were estimated to be acutely food insecure and urgently needing food assistance. According to the AE, these impacts are expected to continue to worsen over time.

4. The funding proposal aims to use a mix of implementation grants, capacity-building grants, financial guarantees and on-lending to further EBA measures such as forest conservation, restoration and sustainable land management practices, water harvesting systems and installation of vegetative barriers. These interventions are expected to mitigate the impacts of climate change in the Dry Corridor region. The total grant request for this funding proposal is USD 268 t with GCF funding expected to make up at least 65 per cent of this amount (USD 174.3).

The funding proposal seeks to address the above-described climate threats by focusing, in part, on promoting EBA interventions that are already being implemented across the region and using a mix of grant and capacity-building approaches to promote EBA interventions in those communities where they have not yet been adopted. The total grant request (including with capacity grants) across the entire region to finance interventions that would improve water demand management in the Dry Corridor, among other potential adaptation benefits. The goal is to link these investments to a financial mechanism that will provide access to credit for farmers as entrepreneurs in rural communities across the seven countries, with increased access to the financial mechanisms expected to lead to improved agricultural and household income.

The specific financial interventions proposed are as follows:

(a) The total grant request of USD 84.3 million will be used in the following ways according to the AE: (i) USD 20.5 million will be used for technical assistance, including mechanical machinery and strengthening the technical capacity of CABEI-accredited and non-regulated financial institutions; (ii) USD 17.6 million will be used for on-lending through a grant facility with which vulnerable communities can access on-grants channeled through CABEI-accredited financial institutions to implement EBA interventions, as well as strengthen the technical capacity of local government, farmers and rural communities to implement EBA and other climate change adaptation measures; (iii) USD 10.5 million will be used for a regional knowledge management platform that will provide access to information on climate change adaptation and finance across the region, and mainstream it into local and national policies; (iv) USD 28.6 million will be used to implement EBA interventions in rural communities through the provision of energy-efficient technologies in rural communities across the region. These interventions represent approximately 50 per cent of the total grant request for each country within the seven countries in the funding proposal; and (v) USD 3.8 million will be used for overall project management, including administrative activities with an estimated USD 0.7 million for contingency.

An EBA lending facility would be established and administered by CABEI with a GCF concessional loan of USD 60 million and approximately USD 43 million in market rate co-finance from CABEI. The financing is intended for small- and large-scale EBA investments at the farm, enterprise and household level. The GCF funds would be

1.2 Impact potential

- There is evidence that the Central American Dry Corridor and the arid zones of the Dominican Republic are among the world’s tropical regions most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, with Honduras, Nicaragua, Guatemala, the Dominican Republic and El Salvador recognized as particularly vulnerable countries (PVCs) for extreme weather events and natural disasters. The PVCs experienced 100 extreme weather events in 1998–2017. Currently, the Dry Corridor encompasses approximately 46 per cent of the monitored area of the Central American region, with a population of around 35 million people.

- Climate projections for the region indicate that, by the end of the century, temperatures will increase in all seasons and precipitation is projected to decrease during the dry season. Changes in the El Niño–Niña cycle, which could result in prolonged droughts as well as more frequent and intense rainfall events, are also expected.

- The independent TAP recognizes that targeted and catchment-specific Eha interventions could reduce hydrological vulnerability associated with increased droughts and periods of heavy rainfall. The TAP has therefore recommended the development of a plan that is tailored to the relevant catchment. The impact potential of Eha interventions is dependent on the specific context, including the availability of water resources in the catchment, as well as a reinforcing suite of actions that, in suitable local combinations, best mitigate those impacts.

- The original funding proposal submitted in B.28 did not contain actionable catchment-specific plans for any of the three catchments. In response to this, the TAP recommended that the Eha grant facility would be applied. In the amended funding proposal for B.29, the AE clarified its intention to apply the Eha grant facility and requested that the independent TAP review it as a critical first phase to ensuring that activities to help fund contribute to a cohesive catchment-specific plan and landscape approach. The AE proposed that part of the grant funding request would finance the development of a catchment-specific plan for each of the three catchments, including further work on the hydrological models at the basin level for three catchments. Two draft sample site-specific interventions were provided in B.29 to illustrate how the TAP intends to approach this.

- In its previous assessments, the independent TAP expressed its preference for first completing a comprehensive assessment of the current state of each catchment followed by the development of a bespoke Eha-catchment plan. The independent TAP appreciated that the AE had proposed an alternative approach to the programme whereby the catchment plans would be completed first, prior to most interventions being funded. However, the TAP noted (i) the need for further work on the hydrological data and approaches proposed to validate interventions; and (ii) the request for grant funding for pre-selected demonstration projects without a persuasive case that these interventions will generate landscape-wide adaptation benefits or that they are critical elements of a catchment-specific Eha plan.

10. The current funding proposal provides some additional information in support of the climate needs of the region and clarifies where the AE believes that a lack of historical climate data has constrained its ability to fill in analytical gaps in support of its climate assertions. The AE states that it is not possible for the proposed catchment plans to be validated against observed historical data sets from meteorological stations, whereas these are available, and not against computer-generated data sets. However, the independent TAP notes that this assertion is not entirely accurate as there have been some instances of validation against computer-generated data sets in the past due to the lack of historical data. The AE asserts that the availability of historical observed meteorological station data is limited within the proposed catchment plans area. The independent TAP observes in the funding proposal that there are only 24 meteorological stations within the catchment plan areas, and of these, only two (Bukoba and Mwanga) have had continuous weather observations since 1950. Furthermore, there were more stations within the prioritized pilot sites and just one of them (Dodoma) had complete historical rainfall and temperature data for a period of over 30 years with less than 15% of discontinuity.

11. The funding proposal also re-states that the catchment-specific plans will be completed first and then validated against the relevant catchment plan. The independent TAP notes that pre-selected interventions will be approved prior to the finalization of the relevant catchment plan. The AE further clarifies in its submission that the validation process is a phased approach, whereby a proposal intervention is confirmed to meet the needs of the specific region in line with the catchment plans. The validation of actual site-specific interventions is to take place during the implementation phase and will be based on a wide range of criteria. A wide range of identified EIA interventions that are adjusted to the specific climate risks and socio-environmental conditions within each catchment plan area will be developed, and pre-selected interventions will be based on the development of the site-specific plans that will include an assessment of the potential impacts of the proposed interventions on stakeholders, as well as sound stakeholder consultations that will allow to identify the most appropriate intervention. The independent TAP notes that this is a reasonable starting point for the validation process but it will depend on (i) the quality of the catchment plans themselves and (ii) the manner and rigour by which these criteria will be applied. The independent TAP further observes in its submission that there may not be an on-site validation of interventions will occur. The independent TAP could endorse a phased programme whereby complete and data-driven catchment plans are first developed and subsequently EIA

In response to a question from the independent TAP on the basis of which criteria would be used to determine whether an investment was eligible for the deployment of the loan facility will follow a phased approach starting first in a select group of five municipalities in the identified catchment and expanding from there geographically to cover additional municipalities over time. The phased approach is intended to allow for more time to strengthen capacities of the participating financial institutions during the initial stages and to provide an opportunity to assess the impact of the loan facility. The phased approach of the loan programme is critical from an efficiency and import standpoint. The independent TAP has been informed by the GCF Secretariat that in addition to the above, the project will also include an integrated catchment management and recharging catchments; (ii) improving biodiversity and ecosystem services through sustainable land use practices and improving ecosystem restoration; and (iii) reducing demand for scarce water resources by implementing water-efficient technologies. Of course, the effectiveness of the phasing approach will hinge on the ability and willingness of the GCF to monitor disbursement rates and adjust loan amounts as necessary to adjust actual capacity constraints.
