1.3 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: Medium**

25. Corporate or large agricultural producers in the Dry Corridor region, where they exist, usually apply climate risk management strategies. According to the funding proposal, PFIs are intended to provide a building block for scaling up these strategies and other interventions, including funding for interventions that the funding proposal categorizes as EBA. However, this is regarded as a paradigm shift in the Dry Corridor region because it would mean that small farming units could play a role in mitigating agricultural losses due to climate impacts, along with households and communities.

26. The proposed programme intends to catalyse a paradigm shift in the regional approach to catchment and water demand management, as well as managing climate change-intensified hazards such as droughts and floods. This would be achieved by scaling up EBA across Central America and the arid zones of the Dominican Republic. This will shift sees governments prioritizing investments for adaptation and resilience building in rural communities, including by commercial farmers and entrepreneurs in rural communities who would then have access to the financial resources and technical skills required to implement impactful adaptation interventions.

27. It is the view of the independent TAP that a leading paradigm shift could offer a building block for scaling up EBA across Central America and the Dominican Republic. The EBA financing model outlined:

(i) there is a path to financial sustainability for this expanded lending sector; and (ii) the links between PFIs, the proposed programme, and the expected climate impacts are clear.

The paradigm shift nature of the proposal is further enhanced by building the technical and institutional capacity of PFIs and non-regional banks to finance EBA projects. The development of a robust enabling environment for investment will be further enhanced by building the technical and institutional capacity of PFIs through the proposed financial instruments.

28. It is the view of the independent TAP that the potential paradigm shift comes from the ability to contribute to improving the regulatory framework and policies that could catalyse EBA demand and acceptance. The funding proposal envisages enhancing the local evidence base for EBA, which will be achieved by conducting a series of pilot projects. These pilot products will be created and disseminated. Additionally, the monitoring and evaluation of project outcomes will be used to inform policy makers about the potential benefits and incentives for EBA Decision-makers within local government institutions will be trained to assess and implement climate-resilient land management practices. The development and implementation of climate-resilient land management practices, the project will help to mainstream climate adaptation into local-level development planning.

29. Creating an enabling environment is a key component in creating an enabling environment that capitalizes on the scalability of EBA, focusing on three core areas of support: financial policy and institutional capacity building. The proposed programme intends to create an enabling environment for investment in EBA and water-efficient technologies. This will be based on the establishment of several financial instruments with various entry points to make climate-resilient land management practices more accessible. The development of PFIs for investment will be further enhanced by building the technical and institutional capacity of PFIs through the proposed financial instruments.

### Gender-related co-benefits

Women are often disproportionately held to traditional unpaid gender roles associated with domestic responsibilities such as motherhood, child- and elder-care as well as household management. These roles generally also include water management and collection. During times of climate change, women are often the ones who bear the brunt of increased workloads, with women bearing most of this additional responsibility. Mitigation of climate impacts should therefore be gender-sensitive.

### Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

From an overall perspective, Latin America and the Caribbean region – under which the seven beneficiary countries fall – has achieved an average annual economic growth rate of 2% over the past decade. However, there are significant differences in development between the countries and even within countries between urban and rural communities. Rural communities have little or no access to adaptation finance, which limits their adaptive capacity.

By supporting the implementation of climate change mitigation projects in selected rural communities in the Dry Corridor will be increased. With increased financial and technical capacity, these communities will be better able to withstand the negative impacts of droughts and severe storms.

Owing to the different socioeconomic, environmental and developmental contexts of the different beneficiary countries, their respective needs for climate finance differ. With regards to the loan and guarantee financial mechanisms being proposed by the AEC, all participating countries are eligible to apply for loans and guarantees up to a maximum amount. This means that some will be able to utilize higher levels of finance than others. There will also be different levels of technical capacity in each country to implement the interventions. The interventions to be implemented in the target municipalities in each country were selected on the basis of extensive stakeholder engagement, availability of area for implementation and appropriateness of interventions.

At the local level, vulnerable rural communities do not have the financial resources, knowledge or skills to develop their capacity to adapt to climate change impacts and other adaptation measures; (ii) co-ordinate the frequent, cross-sectoral, multi-stakeholder engagements required to implement these projects; and (iii) ensure that there is a high utilization on EbS interventions by generating new private income streams from the ecosystem goods and services produced by restored climate-resilient ecosystems.

The following criteria have been used in selecting priority areas: poverty eradication and sustainable development have been prioritized for funding over climate change action. There is a large degree of overlap between these two criteria, as many of the most vulnerable areas across the region where the finance available is far below what is required to meet adaptation needs. 1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium**

Efficiency and effectiveness of the grant portion of this funding proposal would be Low-Medium, if the uncertainties around the expected adaptation impact, given the absence of completed projects, are not resolved. The independent TAP is of the view that, considering the additional climate data clarifications provided, the regional climate scenario for the GCF assessment should be considered as a Medium-Risk scenario and the Medium Rate on the GCF assessment scale, providing the conditions (elaborated below) are fulfilled. However, this remains subject to further elaboration and demonstration.

Efficiency and effectiveness of the loan and guarantee element of the funding proposal is considered Low-Medium. The independent TAP remains concerned about the ability of the project partners to disburse loan funding at such a large scale within the project implementation period. This concern is based on lessons learned from the experiences of the MiBA project which, according to the funding proposal, informed the selection of the GCF as the financing mechanism for this project.

The independent TAP notes that the MEBA programme has only been able to issue USD 30 million in loans since 2014 across five countries. The MiBA loans represent just over 17,000 projects (approximately 5% of the total number of projects implemented by commercial dehydrators). The outcomes of the MEBA programme are substantially more modest than those proposed for the GCF funding proposal.

The CAMBio II programme was approved at A.R.21 and is substantially smaller (USD 15.5 million GCF contribution) than the funding proposal under consideration. To date, CABMII has not yet been implemented due to delays in securing project results. According to the AE, some of this lack of performance is related to the difficult year 2020, in which the programme was affected by the COVID-19 pandemic and subsequent economic crises. The AE informed the independent TAP that it expected the execution of the CAMBio II programme would be completed within two years; however, the independent TAP understands that progress so far in 2021 has been below expectations.

Notwithstanding the above concerns, the independent TAP notes that the overall efficiency and effectiveness of the GCF funding proposal is considered Low-Medium. The proposal presents a phased approach to the deployment of the loan facility. If a phased or truncated approach is appropriate for the project partners in terms of their ability to disburse loans and/or guarantee agreements, this should allow due disbursments to be better tailored to actual demand over time, to increase use of the guarantee product over time, and allow greater time for valorization of the projects. The independent TAP notes that the phased approach is also suitable for the GCF Corridor where the intervention could be scaled up. This phased approach will also allow for


---
**Page 145**
---

more time to strengthen the capacities of the financial institutions and to promote the financial mechanisms with consumers and producers.

5a. The funding proposal projects a co-financing ratio of 1:0.51 and a private source leverage ratio of 1:39.67. The programme's leverage primarily results from the USD 30 million guarantee provided by the GCF, which is used to support the issuance of up to USD 240 million in PPI loan portfolios. The inclusion and use of a guarantee product is a positive feature of the proposal, as it allows for the mobilization of private sector resources at lower cost than would otherwise be possible.

5b. However, the GCF's leverage will not directly benefit the end beneficiaries of the PPI loans. Instead, the GCF will in turn lend to the end beneficiaries. Therefore, the PPIs are like the end-beneficiary credit risk for the GCF, which is a significant risk even though they are not required to contribute their own resources beyond the GCF AR and resources.

5c. Based on the above, the overall rating for efficiency and effectiveness for both the grant and loan components of the programme should still be considered as Medium, subject and dependent on the fulfilment of the conditions hereinafter.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The AD states that the Dry Corridor suffers and will continue to suffer from the effects of climate change. The funding proposal seeks to ameliorate these impacts by focusing on specific catchments in the dry corridor, which are identified as priority areas for investment under the GCF's safeguard policy and guarantee facility. At the catchment level, a successful Ehi approach must be part of a cohesive catchment-specific plan and landscape approach, underpinned by a clear climate rationale for each catchment. The funding proposal does not provide sufficient information to demonstrate how climate change may impact the areas of intervention in the future as well as its implications on the long-term sustainability of the programme, and the scarcity of meteorological zones in these zones.

In this amended funding proposal, the AD further proposes a sequencing approach by which the independent TAP will conduct an ex ante validation process for each of the seven catchments areas at a first step in the implementation of the programme. As set out in the funding proposal, the independent TAP will provide a list of specific intervention plans, establishing the community monitoring committees and initial activities to be implemented within each catchment area.

The independent TAP's preference, as expressed in relation to the funding proposal presented for R2a was that the sequencing approach proposed by the AD should include all seven interventions (particularly the demonstration interventions) can be clearly shown as part of a cohesive approach that, in aggregate, produces the desired adaptation impacts to justify the GCF investment.

The B.30 funding proposal allocates some of these concerns by making the demonstration projects a separate component of the programme and proposing an ex ante validation process for each catchment area (including the demonstration projects) as an ex ante validation process. According to the funding proposal, the selection/validation of the specific Ehi interventions will be based on scientific climate assessment criteria. The independent TAP notes that this approach is similar in spirit to a list of criteria that would be examined in such a validation process; however, the funding proposal is not clear as to how these criteria are defined and applied. The AD has provided a list of the independent TAP's conditions on the framing up and detailed specification of the validation process, which should be considered by the GCF when developing its own approach, as described in the funding proposal, and the potential for a robust validation process to ensure that interventions are selected based on scientific criteria. The AD notes that under these conditions, the independent TAP can endorse this funding proposal subject to the conditions below.

The independent TAP notes that even with these conditions, the funding proposal only minimally satisfies its requirements in terms of sequencing and prioritization. It is important to devote sufficient time and resources to ensure the conditions are thoroughly met.


---

### Conditions precedent to the execution of the EIA

#### (a) The AE shall submit a document containing all information satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat for use in an EBA Intervention Validation Process that will be utilized to ensure that no EBA intervention under the Programme can receive any grant funding until it has been successfully validated by the AE, and that shall contain the following:

- A step-by-step description of the EBA Intervention Validation Process, covering all relevant criteria provided in the funding proposal, including:
  - (1) Sufficient detail on each proposed intervention indicating site selection criteria, expected impacts, and any other relevant information so as to permit analysis and evaluation of the proposed interventions in the context of the validation criteria and the overall GCF Investment Strategy.
  - (2) For each intervention, a clear articulation of the adaptation impact of the proposed intervention to be granted funding as per the GCF investment criteria indicated.
  - (3) For each validation criterion, a clearly articulated and objective standard by which the AE will assess whether an intervention meets it against. A scoring system shall also be designed that can help select amongst interventions. The scoring system to be devised must be deeply rooted in the relevant EBA site system specific criteria and be endorsed by the Secretariat; and
  - (4) The AE shall ensure that all data and materials are collected and presented for each criterion as part of the validation process;
- A third-party review/external assessment for the demonstration EBA site specific interventions; and
- A sample intervention validation document and supporting information as a template.

#### Covenants for inclusion in the EIA

#### (a) The Accredited Entity shall ensure that the provision of grant funding to any EBA intervention under the Programme (project) subject to a requirement that proposed interventions be first successfully validated under the EBA Intervention Validation Process prior to funding.


---
**Page 147**
---

The Accredited Entity shall ensure that GCF funds intended for grant funding of D&A interventions (including demonstration projects) in a select catchment shall only be disbursed to the Executing Entities or Final Recipients (as applicable) after the completion and approval by the Fund of an Environmental, Social and Health specific EIA plan (each, "an 'EIA Plan') for that area, in form and substance acceptable to the GCF Secretariat.

Prior to approving the EIA plans, the Accredited Entity shall:

- Deliver such plans to the Fund for its review and no objection. The EIA plans shall include an independent external assessment of the plan and its underlying climate rationale, including a catchment specific climate rationale, which is utilized by the GCF Secretariat to determine whether to non-object to a given EIA Plan (EIA Plans that are objected to will be returned to the Fund for review by the GCF Secretariat and funded by the Accredited Entity). (i) confirm the level of available historical observed data for the catchment, and (ii) confirm the proper use of such data to generate accurate and informative climate risks and vulnerability analysis. The EIA plans shall also include a TAP report, which is subject to the GCF Secretariat's comments, if any, in the finalization of such EIA Plans, and
- Deliver the final copy of the EIA Plans within five (5) days of their approval by the Fund.

The Accredited Entity shall approve the EIA plans only if they contain the following:

- The catchment EIA plan includes a catchment specific climate rationale, sourcing available historical observed data for the catchment, and utilizes this data in an appropriate manner to generate accurate and informative projections about key climate parameters related to drought and other hazards such as flood and intense rainfall.
- A validated site-specific catchment plan which shall include spatial mapping of the hazard vulnerability of the catchment, and proposed interventions for the catchment. The catchment specific EIA plans must also address the vulnerability of communities to the expected impacts of specific climate hazards.

The Fund will review the EIA plans in accordance with the criteria set out in the funding proposal, including a disbursement schedule for loan proceeds from GCF to the Accredited Entity subject to milestones, to ensure that the relevant GCF procedures are disbursed. If the Fund determines that the EIA plan is not fully satisfied in volume, timing or other material condition, GCF shall have the right to suspend the disbursement of funds until such time as the Fund is satisfied.

Condition precedent to first disbursement of GCF Proceeds under the EAI:

The AEl shall submit to the Fund of an updated investment eligibility criteria for the loan facility in accordance with the funding proposal. The Fund will review this document, taking into consideration all recommendations from the independent TAP. In particular, the referred criteria shall be specific and transparent to provide greater substance to the broad investment objectives currently in the funding proposal.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP174)

## Proposal name:
Ecosystem-based Adaptation to increase climate resilience in the Central American Dry Corridor and the Arid Zones of the Dominican Republic

## Accredited entity:
Central American Bank for Economic Integration (CABEI)

## Country/ies):
Costa Rica, El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Nicaragua, Panama and Dominican Republic

## Programme/programme size: 
Large

### Impact potential
More detailed climate projections to 2050 and 2100 have been included in the revised version that confirm the trends of increased aridity in the Dry Corridor region, strengthening the arguments for the need to adapt. The proposed interventions are considered appropriate to ITAP concerns. CABEI has included a set of criteria for the validation of the selected EIA landfills and other mitigation measures.

In terms of observed data, these are very limited in the region, and it will be necessary to agree on alternative methods to evaluate climate impacts and the potential for adaptation of the proposed interventions.

The pre-defined pilot interventions have been selected through a site-specific consultative process with stakeholders. These interventions aim at reducing vulnerability to climate risks as well as other relevant aspects that determine the relevance of the selected interventions. The positive impacts of these interventions have also been estimated through the hybrid approach, which includes the assessment of water availability, soil moisture availability, groundwater recharge and erosion and are therefore relevant to the needs of the carbon market.

Also, in response to ITAP’s concerns, CABEI has introduced a phased approach for the loan facility to ensure better alignment with climate risks, actual demand over time and stronger capacity of the key stakeholders.

### Paradigm shift potential
As the ITAP points out, the real paradigm shift potential lies on the potential of the enabling environment that capitalizes on the scalability of EIA, focusing on three core areas of support: financial, policy and technical.

For the financial sector, technical capacities will be built within PFIs and non-regulated financial institutions to ensure the inclusion of ecosystem-based adaptation measures into more resilient practices. In addition, the programme foresees also the development of capacities of vulnerable communities to better integrate climate risk and risk management options in their decision-making processes. The proposed interventions are aligned with the land use, agriculture and water management perspective at different administrative levels.


---
**Page 149**
---

The need for adaptation funding in the region is far larger than the actual size of this programme and continues to increase rapidly with local communities facing years of severe droughts followed by years of extreme rainfall events. This programme has the potential to create a sustainable funding mechanism unlocking the private sector participation to implement adaptation measures at scale.

### Sustainable development potential

ITAP's evaluation under this criterion is appreciated and highlights how the EBA interventions included in this programme are not regrettions that provide not only climate resilience benefits, but other environmental, health, and economic co-benefits.

#### Needs of the recipient

ITAP's evaluation under this criterion is appreciated and highlights how the on-the-ground interventions implemented in the target catchments in each country were selected based on extensive stakeholder engagement, availability of area for implementation and appropriate technology.

The assessment also highlights how the region is highly dependent on international support to meet climate change goals across the region where the finance available is far below what is required. The impact of the hurricanes in 2020 and the economic losses from these countries will have even fewer funds available for climate action in the participating countries.

### Country ownership

Country ownership is indeed very high for this programme. It was initiated by the Council of Ministers of the Central American Commission for Environment and Development (CCAD) and there is a strong commitment on the part of the CCAD to support the development of their National Adaptation Plans that are currently under development for at least 5 out of the 7 participating countries.

The strong consortium that will be leading the execution of this programme will ensure an integrated approach between political, financial, and technical aspects that will favour an enabling environment for the scalability of Eba.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

CAEBI considers that this criterion should be assessed at least in Medium High, since the value for money is considered to be high compared to other similar projects. As a matter of fact, this programme is proposing a comprehensive approach addressing all key elements in an integrated manner: capacity, policy and include a strong consortium of leading political and technical institutions. It will create a sustainable funding mechanism unlocking the private sector participation to implement adaptation measures at scale.

CAREI believes this proposal is well aligned with the recently communicated investment priorities from the GCF and its core role in adaptation finance testing and educating the market for EBA investments. The proposal also reflects CAREI’s commitment to developing demand-driven funding mechanisms to scale adaptation investments in the region.

Responding to ITAP's recommendations in its previous assessments, CAREI made adjustments and improvements on the proposal. These include: providing a more comprehensive rationale for all programme sites; developing the first site-specific intervention plans for 2 countries within the region (Ghana and Uganda); and strengthening the detailed analysis for the credit and risk components of the program. # Gender Analysis/Assessment

## Part I: Gender Analysis/Assessment

### 1) CONTEXT OF THE DRY CORRIDOR

The social, political, economic and environmental conditions of the countries occupying the dry corridor, especially those of Central America, remain a trigger for extreme poverty experienced by women and men. The main structural factors that perpetuate this situation are:

- **Economic model:** This model deepens poverty, violence and social exclusion as a result of lack of investment in human capital.
- **Political economy:** It is historically weakened by economic and political elites who defend sectarian interests, justify and sustain acts of corruption and impunity. Social factors mainly include common and organized violence, domestic violence, sexual violence, forced displacement, among others that systematically pushes many people and increasingly women to have to migrate forcibly.

Most violence in these countries is generated by organized crime groups, gangs, and young violent individuals. This violence affects the population's health, disability, migration, disrupts areas of deterioration of public infrastructure, limitations in services in hospitals, health centers and schools, presence of massive security forces, threats, forced displacement, killings, use of women and girls as sexual slaves, among others.

The consequences of this situation are: poverty, extreme vulnerability, family disintegration, extreme devolution of women and girls, loss of physical and economic capital, breakdown of social cohesion, lack of access to education and health services, restriction of freedom of mobilization in the territory that slowly and devastatingly contributes to the loss of leadership and community organization, to the deterioration of the quality of life.

According to the Central American Dry Corridor Characterization Study1, in Central American countries, the percentage of families living below poverty varies from 38% to 50% in Honduras and Guatemala. Of the total rural families, the percentage of families producing basic grains ranges from 46% to 72%. In Nicaragua, 90% of the population is engaged in agricultural activities; 10% of the rural population (38%) engaged in salaried work and other agricultural and non-agricultural activities.

In Guatemala 65% of the producers of basic grains are belonging to the indigenous peoples of the highlands. Producers of basic grains, especially maize and beans, from Guatemala to Nicaragua, produce on average between one and two hectares per family (1 ha in Guatemala, 2-3 ha in Honduras, 4-8 ha in Nicaragua). The local population grows for food purposes for family consumption and only trade when there are surpluses or urgent non-food needs.

Understanding that climate change affects men and women farmers differently is a critical factor in the development of gender-sensitive and sustainable climate-adapted technologies that help producers cope with the impacts of climate change. Better understanding of the vulnerabilities of men and women to climate change and how they differ and what actions should be taken into account to reduce these vulnerabilities is key. To do this, it is:

1 Childbirth Without Borders. Magazine No. 7. April 2015. Reprinted by permission from Child Migrants/UNICEF. www.unicef.org/childmigrants. Vol. 3, No. 4. Ed. 1 (April). P61
2 According to the Central American Dry Corridor Characterization Study: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)


---
**Page 152**
---

necessary to understand the differentiated uses that people make of trees and among others, to support the development of sustainable practices adapted to their needs and opportunities in terms of gender. In this sense, the perspective of gender equality is critical when it comes to decision-making, policy-making and adaptation and mitigation strategies. Women are not only actors of the phenomenon but also bearers of its consequences. They have a dual role: on one hand they have important roles as agents of transformation, they also have specific vulnerabilities that tend to be exacerbated by climate change. On the other hand, women can be empowered through their opportunities access and control of resources. On the one hand, climate change exacerbates gender inequalities, but gender inequalities and contribute to greater negative impacts from climate change.

In this work, we will focus on how gender equality is addressed in the program on climate change in men and women help to ensure a greater impact on the programme's response.

A large majority of women live in conditions of social exclusion due to their limited mobility and access to public spaces. Less access to information in the event of a threat or crisis to early warning systems and natural disasters as well as difficulties in participating in training processes.

Women should be included, but they are more vulnerable, but because they have different perspectives on experiences with which they can contribute; they are nonetheless underrepresented in decision-making on relevant aspects of change climate change: reduction of greenhouse gas emissions, as well as adaptation and mitigation strategies.


# 2) HELD BELIEFS, PERCEPTIONS AND STEREOTYPES RELATED TO GENDER IN THE PROGRAM FOOTPRINT COUNTER OF INTERVENTION

## 2.1 Dominant masculinities

Traditional gender roles and stereotypes attributed to men and women, as well as asymmetrical power differences between men and women are seen as factors of contributing to the definition of a social order in which the subordination of the female is considered natural. This inequality is rooted in a social construction of masculinity, whereas boys learn to exercise power over girls and women, while girls are taught to be passive and subordinate. The result is that women's roles and decisions to men, considering the bodies of women and girls as an object of man who has no rights.

Social and cultural norms are sexist in dry corridor countries. Gender roles, norms and stereotypes are marked by strong inequalities in social relations and patriarchal cultural constructs. Which set expectations for men and women, which can lead to gender-based violence and increasing greater vulnerability to violations and gaps in the realization of women's and girls' human rights and especially in rural and indigenous contexts.

Children are socialized into a homonormative model of masculinity that defines them as strong, dominant and aggressive. This is what they must adhere to a rigid code of honor that forces them to compete or use violence to verify that they are "male". They are also socialized with the expectation that they will be sexually active at an early age. The socialization process for boys in dry corridor countries often means demonstrating your virility (through multiple heterosexual relationships), discussing your conquests and showing off your sexual prowess. In many cases, this is because in these communities children are raised so as not to worry about their own health and not to seek help when they feel stressed. As a result they believe they cannot express doubts or concerns about their bodies, sexuality or gender identity. This can lead them to engage in risky behaviors such as the exercise of their sexuality so they do not commit to the care of themselves and their partner for the


---
**Page 153**
---

prevention of STIs, unwanted pregnancies, etc. These conditions have different consequences for them and for them.

There is a growing recognition in dry corridor countries, where the hegemonic model of masculinity puts women at risk of being victims of gender-based violence and sexual and reproductive health problems, such as the infection of Doria virus human h1 which is one of the agents of cervical cancer. Moreover, women have higher rates of mental disorders, including depression, anxiety, and post-traumatic stress disorder; higher rates of alcohol and drug abuse; violence and exclusion when they do not meet the expected male norms regarding their roles and very low opportunities for participation in the upbringing of their sons and daughters.

### 2.2 The situation of rural and present women

In the social and political context where the dry corridor communities identified for intervention are located, there are certain setbacks of human rights, they form systems of discrimination and privileges that feed on gender, age, class, religion, ethnicity, race, age, the place of residing and disabilities. In this sense, the multiple discriminations faced by women in terms of the benefits of state initiatives, access to decision-making spaces and participation in public life are a reflection of the persistence of the structural conditions of inequality that women live, not detached from the analysis of the impact on life of populations by climate change.

#### a) Population of Poverty

The Dry Corridor is the most densely populated region in Central America, with a population of approximately 10.5 million people. About 60% of the population of the Dry Corridor lives in extreme poverty, and this figure rises to more than 80% in rural areas. The lack of access to basic services has a special impact in rural areas. The effects of extreme events such as drought and flooding particularly affect women because they are responsible for household management and greater poverty. It is caused by the loss of crops, productive activities, problems with livestock and fishing.

#### b) Gender-based violence

The dry corridor region faces a crisis of violence and systematic human rights violations. Large national territories controlled by organized crime or criminal gangs, economic policies that deepen inequality and favor the concentration of wealth in the hands of a few people, as well as the destruction and appropriation of territories and natural goods to native communities and peoples, as well as policies and laws that restrict or eliminate social protest or that violate fundamental rights, are some of the expressions of this crisis. The region is characterized by high levels of impunity.

Impunity is present throughout the region to alarming degrees. At the world's level Nicaragua, Honduras and El Salvador rank sixth, seventh and eighth respectively in the impunity index*. Impunity affects women in all its dimensions: it is a factor that hinders access to justice; it is reflected in the commission of Human Rights (ICHR) has recognized in that several countries there is a pattern of systematic impunity in prosecution and actions around cases of violence against women.

In this context, there is a worrying increase and sharpening of violence against women. Increasingly cruel forms of violence are being used and are more visible. These facts concern the entire dry corridor region amid a culture that tolerates and criminalizes violence against women. There is also an increase


---
**Page 154**
---

In families/female sex aggravated by cruelty in the mode of death, the planning of the death and its execution in the presence of sons and daughters.

Violence against women is an extreme form of gender inequality and affects one in three women in the Americas who have been victims at some point in their lives by violence by their partner or stranger. It takes many forms, but sexual, physical and emotional violence by a male partner are the most prevalent. Violence against women has serious consequences for women's health, including their physical and mental health, the health and psychosocial development of their children, the well-being of families and communities, and the overall social and economic development of countries. It also has profound and lasting consequences for survivors' health, such as physical injury, unwanted pregnancy, abortion, sexually transmitted infections (including HIV/AIDS) and various negative outcomes related to mental health.

This context of violence in which women live must be taken into account within the framework of the areas of intervention, as it also affects women in the selected communities. In the areas of intervention, women are not only victims of violence but also perpetrators of violence. The family is not the safest place for them. The sphere of private space is built around the belief that from doors to the inside what happens is a Family-only theme. Even when they may be in violations of women's rights to autonomy and decision-making, women are often seen as responsible for their own sexual behavior and girls are justified and undetected in rural communities. In turn, physical violence is the most visible expression of gender inequality and the most common form of violence against women. This asymmetry in power relations between men and women, which perpetuates the subordination of women to men in the private space.

In all areas of intervention of the program, the family nuclei are structured around the male gender at all developmental stages. The domination and power of the husband that clearly affects the relationship with women and the role that they have socially assigned to support, dependence and accept (passivity) is a factor that contributes to the lack of self-esteem and confidence in themselves and their self-esteem and their mental health. Which has a direct impact on the physical and psychological health of your sons and daughters. Rural women and women belonging to indigenous peoples are at greater risk of violence than other women.

### a) Access to services

Using in rural areas of the dry corridor and being a pregnant woman incurs socioeconomic disadvantages due to lack of access to services including health, education, water, health and transportation condition.

The lack of services for reproductive health care, childhood care, as well as access to contraceptive methods is one of the main reasons for the high number of births. The limited access to maternal and child health services, the absence of doctors and health providers causes lack of care for women and early childhood. Lack of services in rural areas also means that pregnant women are not able to receive prenatal care or they do not have access to care centers, since they are concentrated in the urban areas of the main cities and have no resources to pay for transportation. In turn, the roads to move are in very poor condition and low transport capacity, which makes it difficult for women to reach health services and hospitals and have no more alternatives. Many decide with no more options to give birth in their own homes and some give birth on the road, when they are on the way to being treated.

d) Femicide

The problem of femicides in dry corridor countries is particularly complex, as it is often linked to other challenges pointed out in the backlines: violence in the couple; family members; organized crime; public insecurity; and lack of access to justice. The causes are also rooted in gender inequality, discrimination, exclusion and structural racism against rural women and girls and indigenous women.

Of the 25 most critical countries in the world, 14 are in Latin America and the Caribbean. According to the gender observatory of Latin America and the Caribbean. In absolute terms, if you compare the rate per 100,000 inhabitants, Honduras is the country with the highest number of femicides: 25.8 per 100,000 women. In another country in the region 10.2 femicides per 100,000 women. In 2016, Honduras registered 5.8 femicides per 100,000 women and in 2017 it was 9.4. The Dominican Republic also saw high rates in 2017, equal to or greater than 3 cases per 100,000 women.

The path to the end of gender-based violence is unclear. According to the agency UN Women, 30% of Latin American women suffer from this phenomenon including women from dry corridor countries. This figure, which represents a total of 25 million of femicide data, achieves the criteria that the World Health Organization establishes as an epidemic.

a) Violence against women environmental defenders in the dry corridor territories, derived from the presence of extractive companies projects

In some territories they have been denounced by the organizations of rural women, that same extractive companies are also responsible for a context of violence, stigmatization and criminalization against community leaders especially towards women who promote the rights of rural communities. This is accompanied in some cases by the persecution of women and young defenders of the environment.

There are large tracts of territory that have been granted to commercial companies for mining, hydroelectric, monocultures and/or oil. These large projects that are causing an impact on the ecosystems of the area also coexist with the smaller territories for family production.

According to the report "Violence against women human rights defenders in Central America", in El Salvador, Guatemala, Honduras, Mexico and Nicaragua, it states that in 2015 and 2016 there were a total of 2,197 assaults on human rights defenders in the region increase of 30% compared to the previous year. The report also indicates that women are the most affected by these attacks: 84% of the aggressions were concentrated, which doubled from 2015 to 2016 in these three countries. Following this trend, it is expected that in 2017 there will be a total of 3,900 cases of violence against women human rights defenders.

## f) Femiization of migration

In this context of climate change, the migration of adolescent women and girls has ceased to be an invisible process. The woman herself personifies as a passive actor accompanying the husband or waiting for the husband to emigrate. The current situation shows the active role that women play in the migration process. They have a role from decision-making to migrating, decisions about where they will settle, and how they will send remittances back home, as well as in the shipment and administration of remittances from the destination country.

The trigger to start the journey is the crisis stemming from the serious effects of climate change and women and girls undertake migratory projects voluntarily or forcefully, to escape the extreme poverty derived from the lack of resources. In addition to the migration of women and girls for reasons of origin, origin also exists when women look for alternatives for agricultural work. As precarious jobs that also puts them at risk of labour exploitation, for example in maquila or even sexual exploitation.

Women who migrate due to climate change also live a great vulnerability as their status as migrants. And during the journey they go through situations of isolation, lack of social and effective networks, precarities in their economic, employment living conditions, their ignorance about their rights in the country of destination. In this context of crisis, women are victims of trafficking and exploitation of new forms of slavery through the recruitment of women through trafficking and trafficking networks for commercial sexual exploitation or domestic work. Once they arrive at the destination countries, they are tricked through advertisements in local newspapers social media or even on the street.

A 2017 study published by[1] the World Food Program analysis data on migrants from El Salvador, Guatemala, and Honduras that were rejected by Mexican immigration authorities when they tried to reach the United States. The study shows that women are more likely than men to be employed in the informal sector before leaving. Contrary to the common perception that migration in these countries is mainly driven by economic factors, 50% of the women interviewed said that their decision was due to lack of agriculture, and 19 % said low incomes and poor working conditions were reasons to leave.

The document also notes that 47% of the families interviewed suffered from food insecurity. Such levels of food insecurity had not previously been recorded in the region in assessments carried out over the past decade.

About 72% of the families interviewed[2] from dry country corridors said they were resorting to "emergency" measures to deal with their situation, such as selling their land, animals and tools to buy food. Most of these families had no access to agricultural credit or loans that could be consumed on a monthly basis, and in 42% of the families surveyed report that remittances were their only source of fixed income, as they did not have access to the agricultural work due to climate change. More than half of the families interviewed said that they had no access to land for agriculture. Followed by agricultural investments – land and animal purchases – and investment in small businesses

[1] “Food Security and Insecurity: Why People Flee and The Impact This Has on Families Who Remain in El Salvador, Guatemala, and Honduras” (p. 6)

[2] Op. Cit.

## The situation of girls in rural areas of intervention

The analysis of the situation of women in dry corridor countries, it is very important to contemplate women throughout their life cycle. Girls and adolescents also have a very important role in the interventions that are carried out with them.

### Forced pregnancy

The list of the 50 countries with the highest fertility rates in adolescents in the world includes almost all countries in the region: Nicaragua, Dominican Republic, Guatemala, Honduras, countries with particular mention to El Salvador (2017). The high fertility rate is a consequence of poverty and an inevitable consequence of the lives of girls and adolescents in the situation of poverty and precariously to which they are exposed in communities. By implying that girls and adolescents have no choice but about their lives. And in turn, it would be a natural condition closely linked to becoming a woman.

Pregnancies among teenage girls in communities are often the result of rape or sexual violence; the couple is rarely found to be of free will. In addition, there is a large number of 15-year-old pregnancies that are also the result of sexual violence and coercion. The age of onset of sexual intercourse was dropping and is often not with peers of the same age. The age of the first pregnancy is evident in community areas in girls under the age of 14.

### Forced early marriage and unions

In Latin America and the Caribbean in 2017, 23% of women aged 20 to 24 married at ages 18 and 5% married 15. The countries in the region with the highest prevalence of women aged 20 to 24 who were married or entered a union before the age of 18 are the Dominican Republic, with 36%, Nicaragua with 19%, and El Salvador with 17%. There is also a significant difference between girls and young women in terms of age differences between girls who marry in childhood and their partners. But here too, data is scarce; a 2010 study found that the average age at marriage for these would be between 6 and 7 years, although the figure may have changed significantly since then.

The poverty generated by the effects of climate change puts producing families in a complex situation to feed their daughters. Delivering a girl in marriage or union with a man who is often older, is thought to be a way out of poverty and a focus for the upbringing of girls; however, the consequences on girls' lives are devastating as it disrupts their life.

# 2.3 Roles And Responsibilities

Gender analysis in statistics shows that the information available is still limited: Women are still not represented in statistics that collect entire information on land tenure, participation in decision-making, and access to resources. This includes type of agricultural production, their involvement in family activities, their participation in activities and their access to credits or other financial activities, among other important aspects.

The social structure within which people, their homes, families, organizations, form the different positions they occupy and opportunities that men and women have in society. It is also one of the areas that is poorly documented in agricultural censuses and surveys.

In general, smallholder farmers grow basic grains for their livelihoods in all zones; in addition, they have a variety of crops depending on the season and the amount of food available); and for the cultivation of fruits, vegetables and herbs. Other forms of land use depend on circumstances and opportunities. First, it depends on the size of the property.

The average age of central American basic grains producers is 69 years; 14 years older than the average age of coffee producers in the same region. This difference may indicate the lack of generational takeover in the field. The presence of other opportunities, cultural changes, ambition and interest in other activities mean that there is a loss of interest among young people in continuing the traditions of their parents.

Gender roles are very marked:

The woman is assigned a reproductive and family care role. She raises children and cares for the sick and the elderly. Their tasks often focus on the private space of the home. They are responsible for preparing meals, cleaning, washing clothes, and other domestic chores. They also manage small plots and domestic use. They have their central work in growing food and rearing poultry and livestock. Because of this, they are responsible for food production for the family, resulting in significant workload and time spent on these activities. Women usually receive low wages or no wage at all with informal work in precarious conditions, with low wages and with seasonal value. In addition, a large part of women's income is used to meet household expenses; therefore, economic activities should not have a decision-making power over the profits collected. Women in rural areas of dry corridor countries spend many hours of the day working and do not have free time.

Caring for minor species care is one of the works that is basically designated to women, as they are the ones who know how to raise them; they know how you can keep poultry and if possible some pigs, since these species provide food to the family. 

Women in their role as caregivers manage to meet the needs of their families through extensive knowledge of natural goods. For the treatment of diseases they are highly dependent on their ancestral knowledge and experience. Women also have a very important role in the care for the sick, although the shortage of plants has affected the care for the sick and adds work and investment to women's activities. In addition, women are responsible for storing seeds and seed preservatives are women however their medicinal knowledge is often ignored and underestimated.

Traditionally they remain in their properties during planting times; however, in the context they live by the effects on land or climate change, they seek other economic options outside of agricultural production to generate resources.


---
**Page 159**
---

This situation has a very big impact on women who must combine their roles to meet the needs of the family nucleus and often the extended family. This creates an additional strain for women in a situation of so much instability that it does not allow them to plan.

Men have a productive role and is one that occupies its time in the crops aimed at the market, they are characterized by being directly involved in production, providing physical work and productive knowledge. Women, on the other hand, are responsible for the care of livestock and income crops, re-presenting to the family, taking the decisions and participating economic organization.

Because of the effects of climate change they sometimes have to move away from their place of origin in order to find alternative jobs. They are isolated livelihoods where the farm’s income is low and labor is cheap; obviously these areas are high of vulnerability. This phenomenon reaches a maximum when it involves forced migration or emigration among certain family members to support their family nucleus with the sending of remittances.

## 2.4 Control and Access to Resources

Most small and medium-sized producers grow for self-consumption and local markets, which suffer from low productivity as their plots are usually located in mountainous areas with little access to irrigation water, road infrastructure to take out its production, credit, modern production technology, improved seeds, tools and basic supplies. Abnormal weather conditions contribute to crop loss in addition to the lack of resources for maintenance.

Underemployment of wealthy farm owners for part of the year in most livelihoods. It has provided labor to well-off agricultural producers, rural enterprises, coffee companies, agribusiness, domestic employment, etc., which allows them to have more resources to migrate to urban centers, other countries in the region, the United States and Spain.

There are factors that make it difficult for women to engage in paid work. From a livelihood approach, these factors are related to women often having neither access nor control over natural resources: land and water.

Water is one of the goods of which women are in the largest percentage in charge of providing in the home where they invest hours to get the resources; however, they are not the ones who have control of births or ecosystems, which visibly affects them

Not having land ownership is very difficult for women to apply for and obtain credits or even save the resources that they need. This also results in a lack of access to social resources such as their participation in the community structures that take definitions. This makes it difficult to engage politically in community decision-making.

Other resources such as energy, technology and transport also limit their options for production and therefore their ability to work for decently paid work. Men often have control and access to resources, as well as decision-making within the family, even if resources are provided by women.


## 2.5 Participation

The participation of women producers is reduced at the local level within producer associations, irrigation organization, neighboring boards, water committees. And also their presence in positions of

social and political leadership is still clearly a minority. In community spaces they have a weak participation also because their voices are not heard or taken into account

### 2.6 Women and Girls Vulnerability And Adaptive Capacity To Climate Change

Holistic analysis is required to talk about climate change, in a complex context such as the dry corridor region with territories with high environmental and climate vulnerability. In turn, in addition to the adverse effects of climate change, which are manifested times ago, they also present a political, social context and with a strong inequality of gender that makes women and girls more vulnerable and less capable to the impacts of climate change

Among the most recent and important impacts of extreme events, the El Niño phenomenon that began in 2014 is one of the most harmful and it has impact to this day still evident. As a result, some 3.5 million people need humanitarian aid and about 1.6 million in moderate or severe food insecurity in the countries most affected: El Salvador, Guatemala and Honduras

Several climate change-related phenomena are becoming stronger according to local consultations with women. Among these phenomena is the decrease of maize crop yields. This has fostered the presence of pests and diseases, and also contributes to the spread of devastating fires. In turn, access to water for livestock and crops has been affected by droughts. On the other hand, extreme rains, hail waves and cyclones have also contributed to the damage of crops

The few studies that exist, complaints from women's environmental organizations and conversations with women during local consultations show that environmental devastation and the effects of climate change are not only a problem for the environment but also for women who face greater challenges, especially those associated with access to water and biodiversity and care of livestock

Identifying and taking into account the differentiated impacts of climate change on women is key, taking into account the central role that women play in the processes of adaptation and mitigation of this phenomenon. The effects highlighted during consultations of the different dry corridor countries are the following:

- **Multiplication of the reproductive day**, with health concerns, generates greater poverty. Women have more work because they must also address food shortages. The workload increases for women to travel longer routes for water
- On the other hand, the consultations indicated that women's work is also increased due to flooding, which forces women to ensure that livestock survive and take them to safer places. In addition, resources are lost on infrastructure such as fences or pens, also adds additional work to women

- **Negative impacts on agriculture and fishing**, for smallholder families especially for women. Family farming as the main livelihood for women is at risk
- Most of the causes of environmental vulnerability mentioned in local consultations and mentioned by women are interconnected. Indiscriminate deforestation causes soil erosion

## Soil degradation has led to loss of fertility and loss of crop yield.

This results in financial loss for the family. They lower family income from corn and bean sales. Women claim and fish farmers of livestock production also expressed concern about the absence of rain.

## Another element of concern is that genetic diversity has been lost because people buy certified seed in some cases even transgenic and have lost their heirloom variety seeds.
All of these are mentioned as threats that are affecting families, and losses in this sector for rural populations to migrate to find work for the family's livelihood or payment of credit installments.

This has a social impact due to the disintegration of families and then leaving children in the care of others in the family or neighbors, which can create various risks for them. When women are not the ones migrating, they take care of the family and try to cover the financial expenses of migration and the credits obtained through the sale of food, jobs in local shops or domestic services.

### Migration and displacement as a risk factor disaster
The "Migration" is a major threat because it is a source of natural risks or conflict. It substantially increases the risk of physical abuse towards women and girls. Women's safety is at risk in addition to other deprivations they suffer when displaced. The most frequent threats mentioned in the areas of the study are: lack of water, food insecurity, loss of income due to environmental degradation, low crop yields, low prices of products on the market, high costs of inputs and supplies, and lack of access to markets.

### Decreased forest cover
Women say that with climate change forest cover has been lost significantly and with it, there has been decreased flows from water sources. The forest has been lost either by the hand of man or by the attack of the weevil. There is a concern on the part of women about the loss of their traditional resources.

Women use forests to obtain food, fuels (wood or coal for cooking), raw material as inputs of products they can sell, herbs, medicinal plants. As forest resources decline, they will be devoid of subsistence supplies.


---
**Page 162**
---

For the treatment of diseases they are highly dependent on their ancestral knowledge as they resort to the use of medicinal plants and herbs to treat illness. In recent years they have observed that some of these species are harder to find, so less or limited access to diversity of medicinal plants and herbs is another impact of climate change, which affects their livelihoods.

> Limited access to formal work through food marketing, many of these informal employment opportunities depend directly or indirectly on natural assets, for example: water. 

For women who make handicrafts as an alternative resource, through the realization of products such as baskets, dumplings or other products that need raw material that has disappeared due to climate change effects are negligible making it difficult to access extra resources for the family economy.

In some countries, women are not recognized as farmers in their identity document, which makes it difficult for them to access agricultural aid or grants from governments. 

> Limited access to water: The availability and access to water has become difficult with an impact on the quality of water and access to safe drinking water has been compromised by increased health problems for families, parasites and other diseases of their own by lack of a way to filter water. This is because the rainwater that falls into the river will flow into the gulf, which is reflected in pollution on the coast, and increased sedimentation.

The water supply is primarily carried out by women and girls; the child's phenomenon has caused the water lines to decrease or dry completely, so they have to move to other aspects that are not related to their daily activities. This has led them to carry containers with water to take to their homes. This can lead to conflicts between communities as before there was no problem of access to water and now it is a problem for some communities and some local consultations fear even wars. The investment of time for water supply can last all day. The reversal of this time indicates is because they have to be aware of the moment when the rain falls, so that they can collect as much water as possible. This has led to the delay or reduction of girls' schooling for their obligations in the search for water impacting their potential education and future opportunities.

The increase in gender-based violence increases; the absence of water puts women and girls at risk, as they may be victims of sexual violence on the routes to water sources.

> Impact on women's health: The impact of women's health is significant and often unviable. In this case, there are many cases where women have died from malaria or other disease due to water contamination. Also mentioned in local consultations are the increase in what are called skin diseases that are related to mosquito bites, such as zika, or mites.

Chronic tiredness also causes overload that significantly affects their health, making an impact on their mental health, with stress and anguish.

Lack of food is causing hunger and malnutrition in women and children. Diet change have been observed due to the lack of access to food, uncertainty of crop yields and livestock deaths, as well as high costs contribute to malnutrition with serious health impacts.

One phenomenon that is occurring in recent years what people refer to as "ustunoke" which is the feeling of higher temperature in the body, due to the increase in temperature.

# Needs and Priorities

The challenges of mobilizing women in local consultations demonstrate the barriers rural women experience when participating in consultation spaces for interventions in the areas where they live. Despite their knowledge and skills, women are often excluded from key roles that are evident to them such as needs and priorities to be taken into account by women.

- **Generating spaces for economic empowerment and strengthening of women's technical capacities to implement Ecosystem-based Adaptation**: It is constant that lack of technical capacity in some areas, and lack of access to information and communication difficult.
- **Provide support mechanisms for women living violence**: Increase the participation of all women at home and in the community space. – Strengthening the role of women as decision-makers
- **Full recognition of women's rights, legal and social empowerment**: Generate spaces for training and opportunities for women producers. Support in facilitating access to credit. Legal accompanying for access to land titling.
- **Financial mechanisms**: Financial mechanisms must be included in the programme and ensure sustainability. Currently, financial mechanisms benefit agriculture before than afforestation. There are no loans or credits available for women who want to invest in agroforestry.
- **Incorporation of Ecosystem-based Adaptation and knowledge management**: Very important. But governance issues (land ownership, agricultural incentives/deforestation) must be resolved for the programme to be successful.
- **Water boards/committees define leadership roles for women: community-level bodies responsible for ensuring access to water resources**: It is necessary that women are included in these committees so that for women the protection of the forest as a key factor for the generation of water for the territories. Funds invested in municipalities considering the needs of women and that really contribute to improving resilience.

| Sustainable production of wood and firewood: | - Improvement in the exploitation of the natural forest. - Protection and conservation of natural habitats - Diversification of family income. - Increased job opportunity. - Improved quality of life. |
| Rainwater collection: | - Generating jobs during dry season women through enterprises. |

# CONCLUSIONS

The high poverty caused by the negative effects of climate change in women and girls lie in the control of land and property. Women are also responsible for unpaid care work within their families. The feminization of poverty is very significant and is exacerbated by the lack of access to family resources, which makes them more vulnerable to the problems that climate change has generated in agriculture, livestock and fisheries.

Women face discrimination in access to resources and control of land and property. Owning the land is important for women for the economic value that this represents, but also to be able to use it as a means of subsistence. Women are not included in credit programs or microfinance programs, linking to women's farmers' cooperatives or local development organizations. When the women do have access to resources and land, they are often excluded from decision-making processes and are not involved and makes it more difficult to participate in community development spaces. As for their participation in decision-making, it is generally limited and when it is done it is shared way with men, it happens in a very passive manner.

Women and girls continue to assume primary responsibility for unpaid care family and domestic work. In this scenario, the serious situation caused by the impact of climate change has increased the burden on women and girls, as they are responsible for the search for food and water on a daily basis. The burden falls disproportionately on girls and women, perpetuating gender inequality and violence against them.

The root of the violence that is rooted in the imbalances of power and structural inequality between men and women is manifested as a social mechanism of people's social relations. And it is


---
**Page 165**
---

underpinned by entrenched social and cultural norms that gender-biased violence in communities.

Gender violence cannot be detached from the impacts associated with climate change, as it impacts women's and girls' lives and puts them on situations of extreme vulnerability to their active contribution to remedy climate change. The different forms of violence against women and girls are exacerbated by poverty generated by climate change, which deepens gender inequalities and the poverty generated by climate change, which in turn perpetuates inequality between men and Women. The lack of access to water and sanitation, food insecurity, and limited access to education and health services in the selected areas of dry corridor intervention, nor the significant impact on women's and girls' health. Especially in the mental health, invisibilization, which is causing in all of them, very serious impacts that are not being addressed.

The start of the migration project is carried out by the extreme poverty caused by the effects of climate change. During the journey, women and girls experience situations of violence, such as new forms of slavery through debt bondage, forced marriages, sexual exploitation and trafficking, and commercial exploitation purposes. They are often tricked through advertisements in local newspapers or on social media or even on the streets.

Other forms of violence caused by poverty experienced by families that are increasing by the effects of climate change include violence experienced by girls and adolescents. Girls are undervalued and face discrimination on the basis of their age, gender, and ethnicity. When their families do not have resources to provide for them, they are often forced into early marriage or sexual exploitation. They are forced to marry or enter unions with much older men who can take care of them. Early child-forced marriages and unions are evident as a standardized and invisibilized problem. This violation of girls' human rights has serious consequences on their health and well-being, including the risk of contracting HIV in poverty resulting from climate change. And that is often linked to sexual exploitation and trafficking in girls. The lack of access to education for girls and women also increases the vulnerability levels of violence that can be exerted against a girl and adolescent, as it is closely linked to high levels of domestic violence. Girls are more likely than boys to experience violence at home, including physical abuse, their access to school and their health. It is one of the causes of forced motherhood, putting their lives at risk from sexual violence and the complications that pregnancy and childbirth cause in the lives of girls.

Lack of access to quality and warm health services, as well as their difficult access because of poor road conditions, have a very serious impact on women in rural communities. Care for pregnant women and girls and children is often delayed or not received at all due to the lack of infrastructure such as indigenous women. In addition, in general there are no institutions that offer protection to women and girls in situations of gender-based violence; they are in an extremely precarious situation and without any support from their families. The lack of access to health services for pregnant women and different bodies creates an additional barrier for women to decide to report and seek legal support.

There is a significant challenge in strengthening health and protection services, in how to respond adequately to cases of gender-based violence and provide good quality confidential medical and psycho-social care to survivors of violence. This requires the development of policies that are in accordance with the basic principles of gender-based violence and the women's and girls' approach.

The constant stress of lack of food is causing serious problems in the health of women and their children, as well as in their mental health. The lack of access to water and sanitation has been exacerbated by a lack of access to food for the children and their children. This represents an


---
**Page 166**
---

additional situation of relevance towards women, who bear the burden of ensuring the survival of their families in a context of climate change.

There are areas in the Dry Corridor, where agriculture is not feasible due to the prevailing climate, which is very dry and soil conditions are not adequate, so diversifying production and complementing traditional sources of through women's entrepreneurship is important. Building spaces for women's economic empowerment can contribute to greater success in poverty reduction.


A key element that needs to be considered is the need to increase women's participation in decision spaces in order to have their demands heard and incorporated, which leads to women to make decisions as well as to the economic empowerment.

Literacy is also necessary, as some women produce from strongholds to read, some had been in second grade. Lack of access to information and documents that women can participate women can have in decision making processes, about their livelihoods and ecosystems. This puts them at a disadvantage because, in the case of having access to information and documents, they do not have the necessary tools to make this information useful.


To promote women's participation in decision-making spaces, individuals surveyed mentioned that it is necessary to strengthen capacities with a percentage of selection


It is necessary to point out that women form social networks within their communities and are aware of the structures and capacities within their territories. Therefore, it is important to include women in the training programs for adaptation to climate change.


The incorporation of the gender approach into the adaptation process, it will not only benefit women, but also men.


The sustainable provision of financial services to low-income households has become an important tool for combating poverty and especially women who, in these areas, are mostly heads of families. Savings, lending, insurance, remittances, as well as business teams, housing, health education and allow low-income households to access credit and other financial services that can help them provide more children to school for longer, and reduce their vulnerability to the crises that make up part of their daily lives. Access to these financial services also significantly contributes to women's empowerment.


Historically, in Central America, these benefits have been largely made possible by the work of micro-financial institutions, which have opted to serve that population that was not credited for formal banking. As a result, governments, development agencies, international cooperation agencies and other organizations have developed various mechanisms and programs to promote enabling mechanisms that encourage microfinance development in different countries around the world, with a special emphasis on gender.

Including representatives of local rural women in these spaces is a fundamental change decision-making processes contributes to the sustainability of the programme.

essential that training and training initiatives for the active participation of women in decision-making and consultation spaces for policy-making are created.

A key element for the strengthening of women's capacity is the empowerment and participation in decision-making spaces in the framework of the demand-based and incorporated, which leads women to make decisions as well as to the economic empowerment.

Generate spaces of empowerment for women producers that ensure the inclusion of topics that they identify as important. This can be done through training programs or by sharing stories of successful experiences in this regard: institution strengthening of women's community organizations, through a wide range of trainings; from courses focused on various agricultural issues to those related to gender equality.

A community-based child care space will need to be generated during sessions that mobilize women. In order to give women the opportunity to participate more actively in training spaces and thus have a more active and productive role in rural economies.

Promote quota systems for the representation of women in Community and producer organizations, as well as in other public institutions. It is a key element in the framework of the strategy for women's participation. It will increase women's bargaining power and raise its voice to remind the community that women are not only producers but also decision-makers who can help combat poverty and improve the livelihoods of rural populations. As well as for women to play an active role in actions to combat climate change.

Women's participation in all social and political processes is a key element in consolidating a quality democracy. Therefore, to strengthen their capacities, that they know their rights and how they can claim them.

Generate community dialogue spaces, which include sessions of discussions between men about post-harvest practices and the role of women as producers. This will help to break down traditional gender roles, limiting access to important productive resources for the family. To advance gender roles and strengthen capacities on the benefits of gender equality for local development and the environment.

Implementation monitoring strategies. It is important to generate strategies and indicators to monitor progress in the implementation of the strategy. The strategy must be adapted to the local condition. It is recommended that women's organizations linked to the agricultural business and sector be developed.

Promote an awareness plan on environmental education that involves women producers Who say in consultations that a lot of environmental education is needed at all levels to solve the problems related to climate change, promoting the conservation of the environment, flora and fauna. The population needs to be aware.

Generate actions that allow women to self-recognize their fundamental role in agriculture and adapt their practices to climate change. This is because in the rural area there are women who simply not yet have the same rights as men.
